# IAMBLICHUS'
# LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS,
## OR
## THE PYTHAGOREAN LIFE.

->ACCOMPANIED BY<-
->FRAGMENTS OF THE ETHICAL WRITINGS<-
->OF CERTAIN PYTHAGOREANS IN THE DORIC DIALECT;<-
->AND A<-
->COLLECTION OF PYTHAGOREAN SENTENCES<-
->FROM STOBÆUS AND OTHERS,<-
->WHICH ARE OMITTED BY GALE IN HIS<-
->*MYTHOLOGICAL WORKS* <note>original: "Opuscula Mythologica"</note>,<-
->AND HAVE NOT BEEN NOTICED BY ANY EDITOR.<-

->TRANSLATED FROM THE GREEK.<-

## BY THOMAS TAYLOR.

> Approach, you genuine philosophic few,
> The Pythagorean Life belongs to you:
> But far, far off, you vulgar herd profane;
> For Wisdom’s voice is heard by you in vain:
> And you, Mind’s lowest link and darksome end,
> Good Rulers, Customs, and Laws alone can mend.

->**London:**<-
->J. M. WATKINS<-
->1818<-

--- Page 5 ---
# INTRODUCTION.

WHEN it is considered that Pythagoras was the father of philosophy, authentic memoirs of his life cannot fail to be uncommonly interesting to every lover of wisdom, and particularly to those who reverence the doctrines of Plato, the most genuine and the best of all his disciples. And that the following memoirs of Pythagoras by Iamblichus are authentic is acknowledged by all the critics, as they are for the most part obviously derived from sources of very high antiquity; and where the sources are unknown, there is every reason to believe, from the great worth and respectability of the biographer, that the information is perfectly accurate and true.

Of the biographer, indeed, Iamblichus, it is well known to every beginner <note>original: "tyro"</note> in Platonism that he was honored by all the Platonists who succeeded him with the title of *divine*. After the praise passed on him by the sharp-witted Emperor Julian—"that he was later indeed in time, but not in genius, to Plato" <note>original: "Οἶδα μὲν οὖν καί Πλάτωνα τὸν μέγαν, καί μετά τουτόν ἄνδρα τοις χρόνοις μέν, οὐ τῇ μήν φύσει, καταδεεστέρον, τὸν Χαλκιδέα φημι τὸν Ἰαμβλιχον, κ. λ." — "I am indeed aware of the great Plato, and after him a man who was later in time, but truly not inferior in nature: I mean Iamblichus of Chalcis, etc." Julian, Oration 4.</note>—all further praise of him would be as unnecessary as the defamation of him by certain modern critics is contemptible and useless. For these little men <note>original: "homonculi"</note>, looking solely to his deficiency in style and not to the greatness of his intellect, perceive only his small blemishes, but have not even a glimpse of his surpassing excellence. They minutely

---

<note>Thus too the celebrated Bullialdus, in his Notes on Theo of Smyrna, speaks of Iamblichus as a man of a most acute genius.</note>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's introductory defense of Iamblichus, transitioning from a critique of his detractors to a biographical sketch and an evaluation of his literary style.</meta>

...notice the motes <note>small particles or specks of dust</note> that are scattered in the sunbeams of his genius, but they feel not its invigorating warmth, they see not its dazzling radiance.

Of this very extraordinary man there is a life extant by Eunapius <note>A 4th-century Greek sophist and historian</note>, the substance of which I have given in my *History of the Restoration of the Platonic Theology*, and to which I refer the English reader. At present I shall only select from that work the following biographical particulars respecting our Iamblichus: He was descended of a family equally illustrious, fortunate, and rich. His country was Chalcis, a city of Syria, which was called *Coele* <note>Hollow Syria</note>. He associated with Anatolius, who was the second in rank to Porphyry, but he far excelled him in his attainments and ascended to the very summit of philosophy. 

But after he had been for some time connected with Anatolius, and most probably found him insufficient to satisfy the vast desires of his soul, he applied himself to Porphyry, to whom (says Eunapius) he was in nothing inferior, except in the structure and power of composition. For his writings were not so elegant and graceful as those of Porphyry: they were neither agreeable, nor clear <note>original: "perspicuous"</note>; nor free from impurity of diction. And though they were not entirely involved in obscurity, and perfectly faulty; yet as Plato formerly said of Xenocrates, he did not sacrifice to the *Mercurial Graces* <note>A metaphor for eloquent and persuasive speech, referencing Hermes/Mercury, the god of rhetoric</note>. Hence he is far from detaining the reader with delight, if that reader merely regards his style; but he will rather turn away and dull the reader's attention, and frustrate his expectation. However, though the surface of his concepts is not covered with the flowers of eloquence <note>original: "elocution"</note>, yet the depth of them is admirable, and his genius is truly sublime. And admitting his style to abound in general with those defects which have been noticed by the critics, yet it appears to me that the decision of the anonymous Greek writer respecting his Answer to the Epistle of Porphyry,¹ is more or less—

---

¹ There is a Greek and Latin edition of this admirable work by Gale, under the title of *Iamblichus on the Mysteries* <note>original: "Iamblichus De Mysteriis"</note>.

<summary>The author provides a biographical sketch of the Neoplatonist philosopher Iamblichus, noting his noble Syrian origins and his education under Anatolius and Porphyry. While acknowledging that Iamblichus lacked rhetorical elegance, the author argues that the profound depth of his philosophical genius far outweighs his stylistic shortcomings.</summary>

<keywords>Iamblichus, Eunapius, Porphyry, Neoplatonism, Chalcis, Anatolius, philosophy, rhetoric, On the Mysteries</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the Neoplatonist philosopher Iamblichus, focusing on his writing style, his circle of disciples, and his personal character.</meta>

vii

applicable to all his other works. For he says, "that his diction in that Answer <note>Referring to Iamblichus's work *On the Mysteries*</note> is concise and definite, and that his conceptions are full of efficacy, are elegant, and divine." ¹

Iamblichus shared in an eminent degree the favor of divinity, on account of his cultivation of justice; and obtained a numerous multitude of associates and disciples, who came from all parts of the world, for the purpose of participating in the streams of wisdom, which so plentifully flowed from the sacred fountain of his wonderful mind. Among these was Sopater the Syrian,² who was most skillful both in speaking and writing; Eustathius the Cappadocian; and of the Greeks, Theodorus and Euphrasius. All these were excellent for their virtues and attainments, as well as many other of his disciples, who were not much inferior to the former in eloquence; so that it seems wonderful how Iamblichus could attend to all of them, with such gentleness of manners and kindness of disposition as he continually displayed.

He performed some few rituals relative to the worship of divinity by himself, without his associates and disciples; but was inseparable from his close friends in most of his daily activities. He imitated in his diet the frugal simplicity of the most ancient times; and during his meals, cheered those who were present by his behavior, and filled them as if with nectar by the sweetness of his conversation.

A celebrated philosopher named Alypius, who was deeply skilled in dialectic <note>The art of investigating or discussing the truth of opinions</note>, was a contemporary of Iamblichus, but was of such a diminutive stature that he had the appearance of a pygmy. However, his great abilities amply compensated for this trifling physical defect.

---

¹ "But also the concise and aphoristic nature of the expression, and the practical, elegant, and inspired quality of the thoughts, etc." <note>original: ’Αλλά καί τὸ τῆς λέξεως κομμάτικον, καί ἀφοριστικόν, καί τό τῶν ἔννοιων πραγματικόν, καί γλαφυρόν, καί ἔνθουν, κ. λ.</note> See the Testimonies prefixed by Gale <note>Thomas Gale (1635–1702), an English classical scholar</note> to his edition of the above-mentioned work.
² This Sopater succeeded Plotinus <note>The founder of Neoplatonism</note> in his philosophical school.

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical accounts of Neoplatonic philosophers, specifically focusing on the relationship between Alypius and Iamblichus.</meta>

defect. For his body might be said to be consumed into soul; just as the great Plato says, that divine bodies, unlike those that are mortal, are situated in souls. Thus also it might be asserted of Alypius, that he had migrated into soul, and that he was contained and governed by a nature superior to man. This Alypius had many followers, but his mode of philosophizing was confined to private conference and disputation, without committing any of his dogmas <note>original: "dogmas"; here meaning formal philosophical principles or doctrines</note> to writing. Hence his disciples gladly applied themselves to Iamblichus, desirous to draw abundantly from the exuberant streams of his inexhaustible mind. 

The fame therefore of each continually increasing, they once accidentally met like two refulgent <note>shining brightly; radiant</note> stars, and were surrounded by so great a crowd of auditors <note>listeners or students</note>, that it resembled some mighty museum <note>original: "musæum"; in the classical sense, a place dedicated to the Muses and the arts/sciences, such as a university</note>. While Iamblichus on this occasion waited rather to be interrogated, than to propose a question himself, Alypius, contrary to the expectation of every one, relinquishing philosophical discussions, and seeing himself surrounded with a theatre of men, turned to Iamblichus, and said to him: “Tell me, O philosopher, is either the rich man unjust, or the heir of the unjust man? For in this case there is no medium <note>middle ground</note>.” 

But Iamblichus, hating the acuteness <note>sharpness or cleverness</note> of the question, replied: “O most wonderful of all men, this manner of considering whether someone excels in externals <note>outward circumstances like wealth or status</note> is foreign from our method of philosophizing; since we inquire whether a man abounds in the virtue which it is proper for him to possess, and which is adapted to a philosopher.” After he had said this he departed, and at the same time all the surrounding multitude was immediately dispersed. 

But Iamblichus, when he was alone, admired the acuteness of the question, and often privately resorted to Alypius, whom he very much applauded for his acumen and sagacity; so that after his decease, he wrote his life. This Alypius was an Alexandrian by birth, and died in his own country, worn out with age: and after him

<summary>This page describes the intellectual encounter between the philosophers Alypius and Iamblichus, highlighting Alypius's ascetic nature and a specific debate regarding wealth and virtue.</summary>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the life and philosophy of Iamblichus and Pythagoras, focusing on the textual history of the primary source.</meta>

Iamblichus,¹ leaving behind him many roots and fountains of philosophy; which, through the cultivation of succeeding Platonists, produced a fair variety of vigorous branches and copious streams.

For an account of the theological writings of Iamblichus, I refer the reader to my above-mentioned *History of the Restoration of the Platonic Theology*; and for accurate critical information concerning all his works, to the *Greek Library* <note>original: "Bibliotheca Græca"</note> of Fabricius.

Regarding the following work, the *Life of Pythagoras*, it is necessary to observe that the original has been transmitted to us in a very imperfect state. This is due partly to the numerous verbal errors in the text, partly to the lack of connection between the narrated events, and partly because many details are repeated in different places using the exact same words. Therefore, the conjecture of Kuster, one of the German editors of this work, is highly probable: that it had not received the final revisions of Iamblichus. Instead, it is likely that others compiled this treatise from the disorganized materials found among his manuscripts after his death. Notwithstanding all its defects, however, it is—as I have previously observed—a most interesting work; the benefits that its distribution is calculated to produce are inestimable. Since two of the most celebrated German critics, Kuster and Kiessling, have produced two splendid editions of this work, it is evident they must have been deeply impressed with a conviction of its value and importance.

As for the Pythagorean Ethical Fragments, all praise of them is unnecessary. One must consider that, besides being written by very early Pythagoreans, they were some of the sources from which Aristotle himself derived his perfect know—

knowledge of morality, as will be at once evident by comparing his *Nicomachean Ethics* with these fragments. With respect to the collection of Pythagorean <note>original: "Pythagoric"</note> Sentences in this volume, it is almost needless to observe that they are incomparably excellent. It is deeply to be regretted that the Greek original of the *Sentences of Sextus* <gloss>1</gloss> is lost, leaving only the fraudulent Latin version by the Priest <note>original: "Presbyter"</note> Ruffinus. I call it a fraudulent version because Ruffinus, wishing to persuade the reader that these sentences were written by a bishop named Sixtus, has in many places perverted and contaminated the meaning of the original. 

In the selection I have made from these sentences, however, I have endeavored—and I trust not in vain—to give the genuine sense of Sextus, unmingled with the crude and corrupted <note>original: "barbarous and polluted"</note> additions made by Ruffinus. If the English reader possesses my translation of the *Sentences of Demophilus*, along with Mr. Bridgman’s translation of the *Golden Sentences of Democrates* and the *Similitudes of Demophilus*, <gloss>2</gloss> he will then possess all the extant Pythagorean sentences, except for those of Sextus which I have not translated due to the very impure and false state in which they currently exist.

I also deem it necessary to observe that the Pythagorean life delineated here is an example of the greatest perfection in virtue and wisdom that a human can obtain in this present state. Hence, it exhibits piety unadulterated by folly, moral virtue uncontaminated by vice, science unmingled with deceptive arguments <note>original: "sophistry"</note>, and a dignity of mind and manners unaccompanied

---

See <note>original: "Vid."</note> *Seneca's Epistles* <note>original: "Senecæ Epistolas"</note>, 59, 64, 98, and book 2 of *On Anger* <note>original: "de Irâ"</note>, chapter 36, and book 3, chapter 36.

<gloss>2</gloss> All these were published in one duodecimo <note>original: "12mo." refers to a small book size</note> volume by Mr. Bridgman under the title of *Translations from the Greek* in the year 1804; they well deserve to be read by the open-minded reader.

# THE LIFE,
## &c.

---

### CHAP. I.

SINCE it is common for all people of sound understanding to call upon the divine when entering into any philosophical discussion, it is certainly much more appropriate to do so when considering that philosophy which is justly named after the divine Pythagoras. For as it originates from the Gods, it cannot be understood without their inspiring aid. To this we may also add that its beauty and magnitude so greatly surpass human power that it is impossible to take it in at a glance; one can only gradually gather some part of this philosophy when, with the Gods as their leaders, they quietly approach it. 

On all these accounts, therefore, having invoked the Gods as our leaders and directing both ourselves and our discussion toward them, we shall follow whatever they may command us to do. We shall not, however, make any apology for this school having been neglected for a long time, nor for it being hidden by outside disciplines and certain mysterious symbols, nor for it being obscured by false and fake writings, nor for many other similar difficulties that have hindered it. For the will of the Gods is enough for us; together with it, it is possible to endure things even more difficult than these. But after the Gods, we shall join ourselves to the leader, prince, and father of this divine philosophy—whose origin and country we must look back a little further into during our investigation.

<summary>The author introduces the study of Pythagorean philosophy, emphasizing the need for divine guidance due to its celestial origins and the difficulties it has faced through historical neglect and forgery.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, divine philosophy, invocation of the Gods, arcane symbols, philosophical school, historical investigation</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>Continuing from Chapter I, this section begins the exploration of Pythagoras's ancestry, starting with Ancæus, the founder of the colony of Samos.</meta>

## CHAP. II.

It is said, therefore, that Ancæus, who lived in Samos in Cephallenia, was fathered by Jupiter—whether he earned the fame of such an honorable descent through virtue or through a certain greatness of soul. He surpassed, however, the rest of the Cephallenians in wisdom and renown. This Ancæus was ordered by the Pythian oracle to form a colony from Arcadia and Thessaly. Besides this, by taking with him some of the inhabitants of Athens, Epidaurus, and Chalcis and placing himself at their head, he was to make an island habitable; because of the richness of the soil and land, it was to be called Melamphyllos; <note>1</note> and he was to call the city Samos, after Same in Cephallenia. The oracle given to him was as follows: “I order you, Ancæus, to colonize the maritime island of Samos instead of Same, and to call it Phyllas.” That a colony was gathered from these places is indicated not only by the honors and sacrifices to the Gods—which were transferred into those regions along with the inhabitants—but also by the related families that live there and the associations of the Samians with each other.

It is said, therefore, that Mnesarchus and Pythaïs, who were the parents of Pythagoras, descended from the family and lineage of this Ancæus who founded the colony. However, because this noble birth was celebrated by the citizens, a certain Samian poet says that Pythagoras was the son of Apollo. For he sings thus:

---

->Pythaïs, fairest of the Samian tribe,<-
->Bore from the embraces of the God of day<-
->Renowned Pythagoras, the friend of Jove.<-

---

<note>1 That is, having black leaves.</note>

<summary>This page details the ancestry of Pythagoras, tracing his lineage back to Ancæus, the founder of Samos who was guided by the Pythian oracle, and notes the poetic tradition of his divine birth from Apollo.</summary>
<keywords>Ancæus, Pythagoras, Samos, Pythian oracle, Mnesarchus, Pythaïs, Apollo, colonization, genealogy</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
<meta>Continuing the account of Pythagoras's parents, Mnesarchus and Pythaïs, and the circumstances surrounding his birth and naming.</meta>

However, it is worth relating how this report became so widespread. The Pythian oracle had predicted to this Mnesarchus (who came to Delphi for the purpose of merchandise, with his wife not yet noticeably pregnant, and who inquired of the God concerning the outcome of his voyage to Syria) that his voyage would be lucrative and exactly as he wished, but that his wife was now pregnant and would give birth to a son surpassing all who ever lived in beauty and wisdom, and who would be of the greatest benefit to the human race in everything pertaining to human life. But when Mnesarchus considered that the God, without being asked about his son, had informed him through an oracle that the child would possess an illustrious privilege and a truly divine gift, he immediately renamed his wife Pythaïs, after her son and the Delphic prophet, instead of Parthenis, which was her former name. And he named the infant, who was soon after born at Sidon in Phoenicia, Pythagoras; signifying by this name that such an offspring had been predicted to him by the Pythian Apollo. For we must not accept the assertions of Epimenides, Eudoxus, and Xenocrates, who suspect that Apollo at that time had an affair with Parthenis, causing her to become pregnant when she was not, and had consequently predicted Pythagoras's birth through the Delphic prophet; for this is by no means to be admitted.<note>1 That is, it must not be admitted that Apollo was actually intimate with Pythaïs, for this would be extremely absurd. Instead, the assertion of Epimenides, Eudoxus, and Xenocrates must be considered as one of those mythological narrations in which heroes are said to have Gods for their fathers or Goddesses for their mothers. The true meaning of it is as follows: According to ancient theology, between those perpetual attendants of a divine nature called *essential heroes*, who are impassive and pure, and the bulk of human souls who descend to earth with passivity and impurity, there must be an order of human souls who descend with impassivity and purity. For as there is no vacuum either in incorporeal or</note>

<summary>Mnesarchus receives a prophecy from the Delphic oracle that his son will be uniquely wise and beneficial to humanity, leading him to rename his wife and name the child Pythagoras. The text also clarifies that the story of Pythagoras's divine fatherhood should be understood metaphorically rather than literally.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Mnesarchus, Pythaïs, Delphi, Pythian Apollo, prophecy, Sidon, Epimenides, essential heroes, ancient theology</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>Continuing the discussion of Pythagoras's divine origins and the celestial nature of his soul as previously suggested by the Delphic oracle.</meta>

Indeed, no one can doubt that the soul of Pythagoras was sent to mankind from the empire of Apollo, either being an attendant of the God, or associated with him in some other more familiar way: for this may be inferred both from his birth and the diverse wisdom of his soul. And so much concerning the birth of Pythagoras.

But after his father Mnesarchus had returned from Syria to Samos with great wealth, which he had collected from a successful voyage, he built a temple to Apollo with the inscription of Pythius; and took care to have his son educated in various and the best disciplines, at one time by Creophilus,

---

physical natures, it is necessary that the last link of a superior order should merge with the peak of one immediately inferior. These souls were called by the ancients *terrestrial heroes*, on account of their high degree of proximity and alliance to those who are essentially heroes. Hercules, Theseus, Pythagoras, Plato, etc., were souls of this kind, who descended into mortality both to benefit other souls and in compliance with that necessity by which all natures inferior to the perpetual attendants of the Gods are at times obliged to descend.

But as, according to the secrets (arcana) of ancient theology, every God beginning from on high produces his own series down to the last of things, and this series contains many essences different from each other—such as the Daemonic, Heroic, Nymphic, and the like—the lowest powers of these orders have a great communion and physical sympathy with the human race, and contribute to the perfection of all their natural operations, and particularly to their procreations. “Hence” (says Proclus in the manuscript scholia on the *Cratylus*) “it often appears that heroes are generated from the mixture of these powers with mankind; for those that possess a certain privilege above human nature are properly named *heroes*.” He adds: “Not only does a daemonic genus of this kind sympathize physically with men, but other kinds sympathize with other natures, such as Nymphs with trees, others with fountains, and others with stags or serpents.”

Olympiodorus, in his life of Plato, observes of that philosopher, “That an Apollonian spirit is said to have had a connection with Perictione, his mother, and that appearing in the night to his father Aristo, it commanded him not to sleep with Perictione during the time of her pregnancy; a command which Aristo obeyed.” Similar accounts of the divine origin of Plato are also given by Apuleius, Plutarch, and Hesychius.

<vocab>Pythagoras, Apollo, Mnesarchus, Creophilus, terrestrial heroes, Proclus, Olympiodorus, Plato, Perictione, Aristo, Apuleius, Plutarch, Hesychius, daemonic, arcana</vocab>

<summary>This page discusses the divine origin of Pythagoras's soul and his early education, while providing a theological explanation for how "terrestrial heroes" like Pythagoras and Plato are born from the interaction between divine powers and humanity.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, divine origin, terrestrial heroes, Plato, ancient theology, Proclus, Mnesarchus, Apollo, daemonic powers</keywords>

--- Page 19 ---
<meta>Continuing the account of Pythagoras’s education and his reputation for divinity and wisdom during his youth in Samos.</meta>

At another time he was educated by Pherecydes the Syrian, and at another by almost all those who presided over sacred matters, to whom his father earnestly recommended Pythagoras, so that he might be as thoroughly instructed in divine concerns as possible. He was educated in such a manner that he fortunately became the most beautiful and godlike of all those celebrated in historical annals. After the death of his father, even though he was still only a youth, his appearance was most venerable and his habits most temperate, so that he was even respected and honored by elderly men. He captured the attention of everyone who saw him or heard him speak, appearing to be an admirable person to all who beheld him. Therefore, many reasonably asserted that he was the son of a God.

But being supported by renown of this kind, by the education he had received since infancy, and by his naturally godlike appearance, he demonstrated even more clearly that he deserved his current honors. He was also distinguished by piety and study, by a transcendently good way of life, by firmness of soul, and by a body properly subjected to the commands of reason. In all his words and actions, he revealed an inimitable quiet and serenity, never being overcome at any time by anger, laughter, jealousy, contention, or any other emotional disturbance or haste; instead, he lived at Samos like some beneficent spirit <note>daemon</note>.

Consequently, while he was still a youth, his great fame reached Thales at Miletus and Bias at Priene—men famous for their wisdom—and also spread to neighboring cities. To all this we may add that the youth was everywhere celebrated as the *long-haired Samian*, and was revered by the common people as one under the influence of divine inspiration. But after he had reached the eighteenth year of his age, around the time when the tyranny of Polycrates <note>Policrates</note> first appeared, foreseeing that under such a government...

<summary>The young Pythagoras is described as an exceptionally beautiful, wise, and temperate youth whose divine reputation and education earned him the respect of both elders and famous philosophers like Thales. His growing fame as the "long-haired Samian" eventually coincided with the rise of the tyrant Polycrates in Samos.</summary>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Pythagoras seeks to further his education under the most renowned philosophers of his era to avoid potential interference with his studies.</meta>

he might receive some impediment in his studies, which engrossed the whole of his attention, he departed privately by night with one Hermodamas (whose surname was Creophilus, and who was the grandson of him who had formerly been the host, friend, and preceptor in all things of Homer the poet) to Pherecydes, to Anaximander the natural philosopher, and to Thales at Miletus. He likewise alternately associated with each of these philosophers in such a manner that they all loved him, admired his natural endowments, and made him a partaker of their doctrines. Indeed, after Thales had gladly admitted him to his intimate confidence, he admired the great difference between him and other young men, whom Pythagoras left far behind in every accomplishment. And besides this, Thales increased the reputation Pythagoras had already acquired by communicating to him such disciplines as he was able to impart; and, apologizing for his old age and the weakness of his body, he exhorted him to sail into Egypt and associate with the Memphian and Diospolitan <note>That is, the priests of Jupiter.</note> priests. For he confessed that his own reputation for wisdom was derived from the instructions of these priests, but that he was neither naturally, nor by exercise, endowed with those excellent prerogatives which were so visibly displayed in the person of Pythagoras. Thales, therefore, gladly announced to him, from all these circumstances, that he would become the wisest and most divine of all men if he associated with these Egyptian priests.

## CHAP. III.

Pythagoras, therefore, having been benefited by Thales in other respects, and especially having learned from him to be sparing of his time; for the sake of this

<summary>Pythagoras travels to study with prominent philosophers like Thales, who recognizes the youth's extraordinary potential and encourages him to seek further wisdom from the priests in Egypt.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Thales of Miletus, Hermodamas, Anaximander, Pherecydes, Egypt, Memphis, Diospolis, education, philosophy, ancient wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Pythagoras follows the advice of Thales to travel to Egypt to study with the priests, having already demonstrated exceptional wisdom and discipline.</meta>

he entirely abstained from wine and animal food, and even before these from gluttony, and confined himself to such nourishment as was light and easy to digest. As a result of this, his sleep was short, his soul vigilant and pure, and his body maintained in a state of perfect and unchanging health. Possessing such advantages, therefore, he sailed to Sidon, being convinced that this was his native country, and also correctly believing that he might easily travel from there into Egypt. Here he spoke with the prophets who were the descendants of Mochus the natural philosopher <note>The original term is "physiologist," referring to one who studies nature.</note>, and with others, as well as with the Phoenician hierophants. He was likewise initiated into all the mysteries of Byblus and Tyre, and into the sacred rituals which are performed in many parts of Syria. He did not engage in these things out of superstition, as someone might be led to suppose, but rather from a love and desire for contemplation, and from a concern that nothing deserving to be learned in the secrets <note>The original term is "arcana."</note> or mysteries of the Gods might escape his observation. Having therefore been previously instructed in the mysteries of the Phoenicians, which were derived like a colony and an offspring from the sacred rites in Egypt, and hoping for this reason that he would partake of more beautiful, divine, and genuine records of learning in Egypt; joyfully recalling also the advice of his teacher Thales, he immediately embarked for Egypt with some Egyptian sailors. These sailors had very opportunely landed at that time on the Phoenician coast below Mount Carmel, in whose temple Pythagoras had lived for the most part, secluded from all society. The sailors gladly received him, anticipating that they would gain much by selling him into slavery. However, when they saw during the voyage the self-restraint and venerable dignity with which he conducted himself, in keeping with the way of life he had adopted, they became more kindly disposed toward him.

<summary>Pythagoras practices strict asceticism and travels through Phoenicia to be initiated into various religious mysteries before departing for Egypt. During the voyage, his disciplined and dignified behavior earns the respect of the sailors who originally intended to sell him.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, asceticism, Sidon, Phoenician mysteries, Egypt, Thales, Mount Carmel, spiritual discipline, Mochus</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Pythagoras has embarked on a voyage to Egypt with sailors who initially hoped to profit from him but have begun to realize his extraordinary nature.</meta>

towards him. Observing also that there was something greater than what belongs to human nature in the modesty of the youth, they recalled how unexpectedly he had appeared to them when they landed, when from the summit of Mount Carmelus—which they knew was more sacred than other mountains and inaccessible to the common people—he leisurely descended without looking back, or experiencing any delay from cliffs or obstructing stones; and that when he came to the boat, he said nothing more than, “Are you bound for Egypt?” Furthermore, when they answered in the affirmative, he boarded the ship and sat silent during the entire voyage, in a part of the vessel where he was unlikely to hinder the work of the sailors. But Pythagoras remained in one and the same unmoved state for two nights and three days, partaking of neither food, nor drink, nor sleep—unless perhaps, while sitting in that firm and tranquil condition, he might have slept for a short time without being noticed by any of the sailors. To this we may add that when the sailors considered how, contrary to their expectations, their voyage had been continuous and uninterrupted, as if some deity had been present, they put all these things together and concluded that a divine <term>dæmon</term> <note>In this context, a "dæmon" refers to a benevolent spirit or divine being, rather than a malevolent entity.</note> had in reality traveled with them from Syria into Egypt. Consequently, speaking both to Pythagoras and to each other with greater decorum and gentleness than before, they completed the remainder of their voyage through a very calm sea and at length landed safely on the Egyptian coast. Here the sailors reverently assisted him in disembarking from the ship; and after they had placed him on the cleanest sand, they built a temporary altar before him. Heaping it with fruit from their current supply and presenting him, as it were, with the first fruits of their cargo, they departed from that place and hastened to their destined port. But Pythagoras, whose body, through such long fasting, was

From there, he also arrived safely at the neighboring lands, constantly preserving the same tranquility and modesty of behavior.

### CHAPTER IV.

BUT here, while he visited all the Egyptian temples with the greatest diligence and with accurate investigation, he was both admired and loved by the priests and prophets with whom he associated. And having learned every particular with the greatest care, he did not neglect to hear of any event that was celebrated in his own time, or of any man famous for his wisdom, or any mystery in whatever manner it might be performed; nor did he omit to visit any place in which he thought something more excellent might be found. On this account, he went to all the priests, by whom he was furnished with the wisdom which each possessed. He spent, therefore, twenty-two years in Egypt, in the <term>adyta</term> <note>The innermost, most sacred parts of a temple, typically restricted to priests.</note> of temples, studying astronomy and geometry, and was initiated—not in a superficial or casual manner—into all the mysteries of the Gods, until at length, being taken captive by the soldiers of Cambyses, he was brought to Babylon. Here he gladly associated with the Magi, was instructed by them in their venerable knowledge, and learned from them the most perfect worship of the Gods. Through their assistance likewise, he reached the summit of arithmetic, music, and other disciplines; and after associating with them for twelve years, he returned to Samos around the fifty-sixth year of his age.

<summary>Pythagoras spends twenty-two years in Egypt studying with priests before being captured and taken to Babylon, where he learns from the Magi for another twelve years. He eventually returns to Samos at age fifty-six, having mastered various divine mysteries and sciences.</summary>

## CHAPTER V.

Upon his return to Samos, however, he was recognized by some of the older inhabitants and was admired no less than before. For he appeared to them to be more beautiful and wise, and to possess a divine gracefulness to an even greater degree. Consequently, he was publicly called upon by his countrymen to benefit everyone by sharing what he knew. He was not opposed to this request, but attempted to introduce the symbolical method of teaching, in a way perfectly similar to the instructions he had received in Egypt. However, the Samians did not readily accept this method of instruction, nor did they follow him with the necessary aptitude.

Therefore, although no one attended his lessons and no one was truly desirous of those disciplines which he tried by every means to introduce to the Greeks, he neither despised nor neglected Samos because it was his homeland. He wished to give his fellow citizens a taste of the sweetness of the mathematical disciplines, even though they were unwilling to be taught them. With this goal in mind, he employed the following method and artifice.

Happening to observe a certain youth—who was a great lover of gymnastics and other physical exercises, but otherwise poor and in difficult circumstances—playing ball in the <term>Gymnasium</term> <note>In ancient Greece, a locality for both physical training and intellectual discussion.</note> with great skill and ease, he thought the young man might easily be persuaded to listen to him if he were sufficiently supplied with the necessities of life and freed from the burden of earning them. As soon as the youth left the bath, Pythagoras called him over and promised that he would provide him with everything required to support his physical training, on the condition that he would receive from him, bit by bit,

<summary>Upon returning to Samos, Pythagoras finds the citizens uninterested in his symbolic and mathematical teachings. To begin spreading his knowledge, he recruits a poor but athletic youth by offering to pay for his physical training in exchange for academic lessons.</summary>

and easily, but continually, so that he might not be burdened by receiving them all at once, certain disciplines which he said he had learned from the foreigners in his youth, but which now began to desert him through forgetfulness and the onset of old age. The young man immediately agreed to the conditions, hoping to have the necessary support for his living. Pythagoras, therefore, endeavored to instruct him in the disciplines of arithmetic and geometry, forming each of his demonstrations on an <term>abacus</term> <note>In this context, a board covered with sand used for drawing geometric figures.</note>, and giving the youth three oboli as a reward for every figure which he learned. 

This he also continued to do for a long time, stimulating him toward geometrical theory by the desire for honor; diligently and in the best order, he gave him (as we have said) three oboli for every figure which he understood. But when the wise man observed that the elegance, sweetness, and connection of these disciplines—to which the youth had been led in a certain orderly path—had so captivated him that he would not neglect their pursuit even if he should suffer extreme poverty, he pretended to be poor and unable to give him three oboli any longer. 

But the youth, on hearing this, replied, “I am able to learn and receive your disciplines without these.” 

Pythagoras then said, “But I do not have the means of procuring sufficient nourishment for myself.” He argued that as it was necessary to labor in order to procure daily necessities and mortal food, it would not be proper for his attention to be distracted by the abacus and by stupid and vain pursuits. 

The youth, however, strongly resisting the thought of discontinuing his studies, replied: “In the future, I will provide for you and repay your kindness in a way resembling that of the stork; for I, in my turn, will give you three oboli for every figure.” 

And from this time on, he was so captivated by these disciplines that he alone, of all the Samians, migrated from his country with Pythagoras. He shared the same name as the philosopher, but was the son of Eratocles.

There are said to be three books by this Samian *On Athletics*, in which he orders the athletes to feed on meat instead of dry figs; these books are very improperly ascribed by some to Pythagoras the son of Mnesarchus. It is likewise said that about the same time, Pythagoras was admired at Delos when he approached the "bloodless altar," as it is called, of father Apollo, and worshipped there. After this, he went to all the oracles. He also lived for some time in Crete and Sparta for the purpose of becoming acquainted with their laws; and, having been a student and listener of all these, he returned home in order to investigate what he had omitted.

And first, indeed, he established a school in the city, which is even now called the semicircle of Pythagoras. In this school, the Samians now consult about public affairs, believing it right to investigate just and advantageous matters in the place constructed by him who paid attention to the welfare of all men. He also created a cave outside the city, adapted to his philosophy, in which he spent the greatest part of both the day and night. He employed himself in the investigation of useful things in various disciplines, framing intellectual concepts in the same manner as Minos, the son of Jupiter. Indeed, he so much surpassed those who afterward employed his disciplines that they thought highly of themselves from the knowledge of theorems of small importance; but Pythagoras completed the science of the celestial orbs and revealed the whole of it through arithmetical and geometrical demonstrations.

He is, however, to be admired to an even greater degree for what he accomplished later. For when philosophy had now received a great increase, he was admired by all Greece, and the best of those who practiced philosophy came to Samos on his account in order that they might share in his learning. The citizens likewise employed him in all their embassies and compelled him to join with them in the admin-

<summary>Pythagoras returns to Samos after traveling to Delos, Crete, and Sparta, establishing a school and a private cave for his philosophical and mathematical research. His mastery of celestial sciences and his reputation attract scholars from across Greece and lead him into a role in public administration.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Pythagoras has explored various disciplines and established a school in Samos, but he finds that his philosophical pursuits are becoming incompatible with the local political environment.</meta>

the administration of public affairs. However, as he easily saw the difficulty of complying with the laws of his country while simultaneously remaining at home and philosophizing, and considering that all philosophers before him had spent their lives in foreign countries, he determined to neglect all political occupations. He was led to this, according to the testimony of others, by the negligence of the Samians in matters relating to education, and he went into Italy, conceiving that place to be his proper country, as it was where men inclined toward learning were to be found in the greatest abundance. Such was the success of his journey that on his arrival at Crotona, which was the noblest city in Italy, he had many followers, amounting, as it is said, to six hundred. These people were not only inspired by his discourses to study philosophy, but also to a friendly sharing of their worldly goods in common; from this practice, they acquired the name *Cænobitæ*.

### CHAP. VI.

AND these indeed were those who practiced philosophy. But the greatest part of his disciples consisted of hearers whom they call *Acusmatici*, who on his first arrival in Italy, according to Nicomachus, exceeded two thousand in number, having been captivated by a single public speech alone. These people, with their wives and children, being gathered into one very large and common assembly called the *Homacoïon*—which for its size resembled a city—founded a place which was universally called *Magna Græcia*. This great multitude of people likewise, receiving laws and mandates from Pythagoras as if they were divine precepts, and without which they engaged in no occupation, lived together with the greatest universal harmony, celebrated and regarded by their neighbors as being among the number of the blessed. At

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, Pythagoras’s followers in Magna Graecia lived in harmony, following his divine-like laws and sharing their possessions.</meta>

At the same time, as we have already observed, they shared their possessions in common. Their reverence for Pythagoras was such that they numbered him among the gods, as a certain beneficent and most philanthropic daemon. Indeed, some celebrated him as the Pythian, while others as the Hyperborean Apollo. Some again considered him as Paeon, but others as one of the daemons that inhabit the moon; and others celebrated him as one of the Olympian Gods,¹ who, in order to benefit and correct mortal life, appeared to the people of those times in a human form, so that he might extend to them the healing light of happiness and philosophy. And indeed, a greater good never came, nor ever will come to mankind, than that which was imparted by the gods through this Pythagoras. Hence, even now, the proverb of the *long-haired Samian* is applied to the most venerable man. However, Aristotle relates, in his treatise *On the Pythagoric Philosophy*, that such a distinction as

---

<note>¹ From what was said in the note on page 3 regarding the divine origin of Pythagoras, it follows that he was a *terrestrial hero* belonging to the lineage of Apollo. In the same way, the Asclepius who once lived on the earth and was the inventor of medicine proceeded—according to ancient mythology—from the God Asclepius, who exists within Apollo, just as the hero Bacchus proceeded from the Bacchus who exists within Jupiter. Hence, the Emperor Julian (according to Cyril) says of Asclepius: 

> "I had almost forgotten the greatest of the gifts of Jupiter and the Sun, but I have appropriately reserved it for the last. For it is not unique to us only, but is also common, I think, to our kindred the Greeks. For Jupiter, among the intelligibles, generated Asclepius from himself; but he was revealed into light on the earth through the creative light of the sun. He therefore, proceeding from heaven to the earth, appeared consistently in a human shape around Epidaurus. But from there, becoming multiplied in his progressions, he extended his saving hand to all the earth. He came to Pergamum, to Ionia, to Tarentum, and afterwards to Rome. Thence he went to the island of Cos, afterwards to Aegae, and at length to wherever there is land and sea. We did not experience his beneficence individually, but collectively. And at one and the same time, he corrected souls that were wandering in error and bodies that were infirm."</note>

<keywords>Pythagoras, divinity, Apollo, daemon, Aristotle, Asclepius, Emperor Julian, Epidaurus, Neoplatonism, Samian, philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which discussed the divine reputation of Pythagoras and Aristotle’s accounts of him, this text details the specific scientific and ethical knowledge Pythagoras introduced to the Greeks.</meta>

the following was preserved by the Pythagoreans among their principal arcana; namely, that of rational beings, one kind is a God, another is man, and another is like Pythagoras. Indeed, they very reasonably understood him to be a being of this kind, through whom a correct conception—conforming to the reality of things—was introduced regarding the Gods, heroes, and daemons; regarding the world and the diverse motions of the spheres and stars, their oppositions, eclipses, and inequalities, their eccentricities and epicycles; and regarding all the natures contained in the heavens and the earth, as well as those that have an intermediate existence, whether visible or hidden. Nor was there anything in all this variety of information that was at all contrary to observable phenomena or the insights of the intellect. To this we may add that Pythagoras revealed to the Greeks all those disciplines, theories, and scientific investigations that truly strengthen the eye of the soul and purify the intellect from the blindness caused by other types of study, enabling it to perceive the true principles and causes of the universe. But besides all this, the best form of governance, public harmony, the sharing of possessions among friends, the worship of the gods, piety toward the dead, legislation, learning, silence, abstinence from eating animals, self-control, temperance, wisdom, divinity—and in short, everything that is eagerly sought by lovers of learning—was brought to light by Pythagoras. For all these reasons, therefore, as I have just said, he was everywhere so transcendently admired.

### CHAPTER VII.

It remains, therefore, after this, that we should relate how he traveled, which places he first visited, what speeches he made, on what subjects, and to

It credits him with introducing essential ethical practices, scientific disciplines, and social systems like the sharing of property.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, Pythagoreans, arcana, astronomy, spheres, epicycles, eye of the soul, governance, abstinence from animals, divinity</keywords>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which described Pythagoras's divine reputation and the broad range of scientific and ethical disciplines he introduced to the Greeks, this page focuses on his specific influence and actions upon arriving in Italy and Sicily.</meta>

to whom they were addressed; for in this way we shall easily understand the nature of his association with the men of that time. It is said, then, that as soon as he came to Italy and Sicily—cities which he understood had oppressed each other with slavery, partly in the distant past and partly recently—he inspired the inhabitants with a love of liberty. Through his listeners, he restored to independence and liberated Crotona, Sybaris, Catanes, Rhegium, Himæra, Agrigentum, Tauromenas, and several other cities. For these cities he also established laws through Charondas of Catania and Zaleucus the Locrian, by which they became flourishing cities and provided an example worthy of imitation to neighboring kingdoms for a long time. He also entirely eliminated sedition, discord, and party zeal, not only among his close associates and their descendants for many generations, as history informs us, but, in short, from all the cities in Italy and Sicily that were at that time disturbed by internal and external conflicts. For the following <term>apothegm</term> was always used by him in every place, whether in the company of many or a few, which was similar to the persuasive oracle of a God and was a summary, as it were, of his own opinions: that we should avoid and cut away by every possible means—by fire, sword, and all kinds of devices—disease from the body, ignorance from the soul, luxury from the belly, sedition from a city, discord from a house, and, at the same time, immoderation from all things. Through this, with a most fatherly affection, he reminded each of his disciples of the most excellent doctrines. Such, therefore, was the common form of his life at that time, in both words and actions. If, however, it is necessary to give a more detailed account of what he did and said, it must be noted that he came to Italy in the sixty-second Olympiad, at which time Eryxidas of Chalcis was victorious in the stadium.

<summary>Pythagoras arrived in Italy and Sicily during the 62nd Olympiad, where he liberated several cities from slavery, established legal systems, and taught a philosophy of absolute moderation to eliminate social and personal discord.</summary>

<meta>Continuing from the previous page, which described Pythagoras's efforts to bring liberty and moderation to the cities of Italy and Sicily, this page details his arrival in Crotona and his first public miracle and speech.</meta>

But immediately upon his arrival, he became conspicuous and illustrious, in the same manner as before, when he sailed to Delos. For there, when he performed his acts of worship at the bloodless altar of father Apollo, he was admired by the inhabitants of the island.

### CHAP. VIII.

At that time also, when he was journeying from Sybaris to Crotona, he met some fishermen near the shore who were then drawing their nets, heavily laden with fish, from the deep; he told them he knew the exact number of the fish they had caught. After the fishermen promised they would perform whatever he should order them to do if the outcome matched his prediction, he ordered them—after they had accurately counted the fish—to return them alive to the sea. And what is yet more wonderful, not one of the fish died while he stood on the shore, though they had been kept out of the water for a considerable time. Having therefore paid the fishermen the price of their fish, he departed for Crotona. But they everywhere publicized the fact, and having learned his name from some children, they told it to everyone. Hence, those who heard of this affair were eager to see the stranger, and what they desired was easily obtained. They were astonished upon looking at his face, and judged him to be exactly the kind of man he was in reality. A few days after this, he entered the Gymnasium, and being surrounded by a crowd of young men, he is said to have delivered a speech to them, in which he urged them to pay attention to their elders, demonstrating that in the world, in life, in cities, and in nature, that which has precedence is more honorable than that which follows in time. As for instance, that the east is more honorable than the west; the morning than the evening; the beginning

than the end; and generation than decay. In a
similar manner he observed that natives were more
honorable than strangers, and the leaders of colonies
than the builders of cities; and universally, Gods than
daemons; daemons than demigods; and heroes than
men. Of these likewise he observed that the authors
of generation are more honorable than their offspring.
He said these things, however, for the sake of proving
by induction that children should greatly esteem
their parents, to whom he asserted they owed as much
gratitude as a dead man would owe to one who was
able to bring him back again into light. Afterwards,
he observed that it was indeed just to love those above
all others, and never to give them pain, who first
benefited us, and in the greatest degree. But parents
alone benefit their children prior to their birth, and
are the causes to their offspring of all their upright
conduct; and that when children show themselves to
be in no respect inferior to their parents in beneficence
towards them, it is not possible for them in this respect
to err. For it is reasonable to suppose that the Gods
will pardon those who honor their parents in no less
a degree than the divinities themselves; since we learned
from our parents to honor divinity. Hence Homer

<meta>Pythagoras continues his discourse to the inhabitants of Crotona, transitioning from the honor due to parents to the necessity of friendship and the virtue of temperance.</meta>

Minerva, and the latter Vulcan, who are of a nature contrary to each other, so that even those things most distant from each other may share in friendship.

Since all his listeners also agreed that the judgment of the immortals is most valid, he said he would demonstrate to the Crotonians—using the example of Hercules, the founder of the colony brought to Crotona—that it is necessary to be voluntarily obedient to the commands of parents. They knew from tradition that the God himself had undertaken such great labors as a result of obeying the commands of one older than himself; and having been victorious in what he had undertaken to accomplish, he had established the Olympic games in honor of his father. He also showed them that they should associate with each other in such a way that they would never be in a state of hostility toward their friends, but would most quickly become friends with their enemies. He taught that they should show the benevolent disposition of children toward their parents through modest behavior toward their elders, and show brotherly love and regard through their philanthropy to others.

Next, he spoke concerning temperance, and said that youth should test its own nature, as this is the period in which desires are in their most flourishing state. Afterward, he urged them to consider that this virtue alone, among all others, was suited to a boy and a maiden, to a woman, and to the rank of those of a more advanced age; and that it was especially suited to the younger members of the community. He also added that this virtue alone encompassed the goods of both body and soul, as it preserved health and also the desire for the most excellent studies. But this is evident from the opposite. For when the Barbarians and Greeks fought each other over Troy, both sides fell into the most dreadful calamities through the lack of self-control <note>The original text uses "incontinence," meaning a lack of restraint or self-mastery.</note> of one man, partly during the war itself, and partly while returning to their native land. And the divinity ordained that the

<meta>Pythagoras continues his discourse to the youth of Crotona, shifting from the consequences of the Trojan War to the supreme value of education and the enduring nature of intellectual pursuit.</meta>

punishment of injustice alone should endure for a thousand and ten years, predicting by an oracle the capture of Troy, and ordering that virgins should be annually sent by the Locrians into the temple of Trojan Minerva. Pythagoras also exhorted young men to cultivate learning, calling on them to observe how absurd it would be if they judged the power of reasoning to be the most commendable of all things and consulted about other matters using this power, yet bestowed no time nor labor in exercising it. For the attention paid to the body resembles degenerate friends and rapidly fails; but <term>erudition</term>, like worthy and good men, endures until death, and for some people procures immortal renown after death. These and other observations of a similar kind were made by Pythagoras, partly from history and partly from <note>philosophical</note> dogmas, in which he showed that erudition is a natural excellence of disposition common to those in each class who rank in the highest level of human nature.

For the discoveries of these individuals become erudition for others. But this is naturally so worthy of pursuit that, compared to other praiseworthy objects of attainment, it is not possible to share some of them through another person—such as strength, beauty, health, and fortitude. Others are no longer possessed by the one who imparts them to another, such as wealth, dominion, and many other things which we shall omit mentioning. However, it is possible for erudition to be received by another without in the least diminishing what the giver possesses. In a similar manner, some goods cannot be possessed by men; but we are capable of being instructed according to our own proper and deliberate choice. And furthermore, he who—having been thus instructed—engages in the administration of his country’s affairs does not do this out of insolence, but out of erudition. For through education alone men differ from wild beasts, the Greeks from the Barbarians, those who

<summary>Pythagoras argues that while physical attributes and wealth are fleeting or finite, education (erudition) is a permanent virtue that can be shared without loss. He emphasizes that learning distinguishes humans from animals and civilized people from barbarians.</summary>

In short, those who possess learning have such a superiority over those who do not, that while seven men from one city and in one Olympiad were found to be swifter than others in the race, in the entire inhabited part of the globe, those who excelled in wisdom were also only seven in number. But in the times that followed, in which Pythagoras lived, he alone surpassed all others in philosophy. For he called himself by this name <note>namely, a philosopher</note>, instead of a wise man.

## CHAPTER IX.

This is indeed what he said to the young men in the Gymnasium. But when they had told their parents what they had heard, a thousand men—having called Pythagoras into the senate-house and praised him for what he had said to their sons—desired him, if he had anything beneficial to say to the Crotonians, to reveal it to those who were the leaders of the government. He was also the first who advised them to build a temple to the Muses, in order that they might preserve the existing concord. For he observed that all these divinities were called by one common name <note>the Muses</note>, that they existed in conjunction with each other, especially rejoiced in shared honors, and, in short, that there was always one and the same choir of the Muses. He likewise further observed that they included within themselves symphony, harmony, rhythm, and all things which produce concord. They also demonstrate that their power does not only extend to the most beautiful <term>theorems</term>, but likewise to the symphony and harmony of things. Next, he said it was necessary they should understand that they received their country from the multitude of the citizens as a common trust. Therefore, it was necessary

<meta>Pythagoras continues his address to the senators of Crotona, shifting from the divine nature of the Muses to the practical and moral responsibilities of those in government.</meta>

they should so govern it, that they might faithfully transmit it to their descendants as an inherited possession. And this would be effectively achieved if they were equal to the citizens in all things, and surpassed them in nothing except justice. For men, knowing that every place requires justice, have claimed in myths that Themis <note>the goddess of divine law and order</note> holds the same rank as Jupiter <note>Zeus</note>, that Dice <note>Dike, the goddess of human justice</note>, that is, justice, sits beside Pluto, and that Law is established in cities; so that he who does not act justly in the duties his social rank requires him to perform may simultaneously appear unjust toward the entire world. He added that it was proper for the senators not to use any of the gods for the purpose of taking an oath, but rather their speech should be such as to make them worthy of belief even without oaths. And likewise, they should manage their own domestic affairs so that the governance of them is the result of their deliberate choice. They should also be genuinely devoted toward their own offspring, as humans are the only animals with a conscious awareness of this relationship. And they should associate with a wife, the companion of life, in such a way as to be mindful that while other contracts are engraved on tablets and pillars, those with wives are embodied in children. They should likewise strive to be loved by their children, not just through nature (which they did not cause), but through deliberate choice; for this is voluntary kindness.

He further observed that they should be careful not to have sexual relations with anyone but their wives, so that the wives do not degrade the lineage through the neglect and immoral conduct of the husbands. They should also consider that they received their wives from the Vestal hearth with libations, and brought them home as if they were suppliants in the presence of the gods themselves. And that through orderly conduct and self-control, they should become examples both to their own families and to the city in which

Besides this, they should take care to prevent everyone from acting wickedly, so that offenders—not fearing the punishment of the laws—are not hidden; and by respecting beautiful and worthy manners, they should be driven toward justice. He also urged them to remove laziness from all their actions; for he said that opportunity was the only good in every action. But he defined the separation of parents and children from one another as the greatest of injuries. And he said that the man who can foresee what will be beneficial to himself should be considered the most excellent; but that the next in excellence is he who understands what is useful from things that happen to others. However, the worst of men is he who waits to perceive what is best until he himself is suffering. He also said that those who wish to be honored will not go wrong if they imitate those who are crowned in the races: for these athletes do not harm their opponents, but only desire that they themselves obtain the victory. In the same way, it is appropriate that those who engage in the administration of public affairs should not be offended by those who contradict them, but should benefit those who are obedient to them. He also urged everyone who aspired to true glory to be in reality what he wished to appear to others: for counsel is not as sacred as praise, since the former is only useful among men, while the latter is mostly associated with the Gods. After all this, he added that their city was founded by Hercules at the time when he drove the oxen through Italy, having been wronged by Lacinius; and while giving help by night to Croton, he killed him by mistake, thinking him to be an enemy. Afterward, Hercules promised that a city should be built around the tomb of Croton and should be named Crotona after him, once Hercules himself achieved immortality. Therefore, Pythagoras said it was right that they should justly return

## CHAPTER X.

Pythagoras, therefore, complying with their wish, is said to have given the boys the following advice: that they should neither revile anyone, nor take vengeance on those who reviled them. He likewise exhorted them to pay diligent attention to learning, which derives its name from their age. He added that it was easy for a modest youth to preserve integrity through the whole of life; but that it was difficult for one to accomplish this who was not naturally well-disposed at that age; or rather, it is impossible that he who begins his course from a bad impulse should run well to the end.

Besides this, he observed that boys were most dear to divinity, and therefore, in times of great drought, they were sent by cities to implore rain from the Gods, because of the belief that divinity is especially attentive to children; even though those who are permitted to be continually involved with sacred ceremonies scarcely achieve perfect purification. For this reason also, the most philanthropic of the Gods, Apollo and Love, are universally represented in pictures as being the age of boys. It is likewise acknowledged that some of the games in which the victors are crowned were instituted on account of boys: the Pythian games, indeed, because the serpent Python was slain by a boy; but the Nemean and Isthmian games on account of the deaths of Archemorus and Melicerta. Besides what has been said,

<meta>Pythagoras concludes his advice to the youth of Crotona, emphasizing respect for elders and the divine nature of guidance, before turning his attention to the women of the city to instruct them on the nature of sacrifices and virtue.</meta>

while the city of Crotona was being built, Apollo promised the founder that he would give him progeny if he brought a colony into Italy. From this, he inferred that Apollo providentially attended to their propagation and that all the Gods paid attention to people of every age; therefore, they ought to make themselves worthy of the Gods' friendship. He added that they should practice listening so that they might be able to speak. Furthermore, as soon as they have entered the path on which they intend to travel toward old age, they should follow the steps of those who preceded them and never contradict those who are older than themselves. For in this way, they will hereafter justly think it right that they themselves should not be mistreated by their juniors. On account of these exhortations, it must be admitted that he deserved not to be called by his own name, but that all men should call him divine.

### CHAPTER XI.

But to the women, he is said to have spoken concerning sacrifices as follows: In the first place, just as they would wish that another person who intended to pray for them should be worthy and good—because the Gods listen to such people—it is also necessary that they should highly value fairness and modesty, so that the Gods may be readily disposed to hear their prayers. Next, they should offer to the Gods things they have produced with their own hands and should bring them to the altars without the help of servants, such as cakes, honeycombs, and frankincense. They should not worship divinity with blood and dead bodies, nor offer many things at one time, as if they never intended to sacrifice again. Regarding their association with men, he exhorted them to consider that their

As a consequence of this, they would do well either not to oppose their husbands or to consider that they have then conquered when they submit to them. Furthermore, in the same assembly, Pythagoras is also said to have made that celebrated observation: that it is holy for a woman to perform sacred rites on the same day after having been intimate with her husband, but that this is never holy after she has been intimate with any other man. He also exhorted the women to use auspicious words throughout their lives and to endeavor so that others might predict good things about them. He likewise admonished them not to ruin their public reputation, nor to blame the writers of fables. These writers, observing the justice of women—how they provide others with garments and ornaments without a witness when it is necessary for someone else to use them, and how neither lawsuits nor disputes arise from this trust—fabled that three women shared only one eye in common, because of the ease of their cooperation with each other. Hence, he called an unmarried woman *Core*, that is, *Proserpine*; a bride, *Nympha*; a woman who has given birth to children, *Mater*; and a grandmother, according to the Doric dialect, *Maia*. In accordance with this, the oracles at Dodona and Delphi are brought to light through a woman. Because of this praise regarding piety, Pythagoras is said to have produced so great a change in female clothing that the women no longer dared to dress themselves in costly garments, but instead consecrated tens of thousands of their clothes in the temple of Juno. The effect of this discourse is also said to have been such that in the region of the Crotonians, the faithfulness of the husband to the wife was universally celebrated; in this respect, they imitated Ulysses, who would not accept immortality from Calypso on the condition that he should abandon Penelope. because investigation, which is implied by *Maia*, produces *discovery*: and as revealing the will of Jupiter, who is an intellectual God, he is the cause of learning or discipline. He first exists in Jupiter, the creator of the world; next among the supermundane Gods; in the third place, among the liberated Gods; fourthly, in the planet Mercury; fifthly, in the Mercurial order of demons; sixthly, in human souls, who are the followers of this God; and in the seventh degree, his properties exist in certain animals, such as the ibis, the ape, and wise dogs. The account given by Socrates in this place is both allegorical and anagogic or elevating. Naucratis is a region of Egypt particularly subject to the influence of Mercury, though the whole of Egypt is assigned to this divinity. Likewise, in this city, a man once flourished who was full of Mercurial power, because his soul formerly existed in the heavens of the Mercurial order. But he was first called *Theuth*, that is, *Mercury*, and a God, because his soul existed according to the perfect likeness of this divinity. But afterwards he was called a demon, because from the God Mercury, through a Mercurial demon, gifts of this kind are transmitted to a Mercurial soul.

## CHAP. XII.

It is also said that <term>Pythagoras</term> was the first person to call himself a <term>philosopher</term>. This was not merely a new name, but one that effectively teaches us about the nature of the thing it describes. For he said that the entry of men into this life resembled the gathering of a crowd at a public festival. There, people of every description assemble with different goals: one hurries to sell his wares for the sake of money and profit; another seeks to acquire fame by exhibiting his physical strength; and there is also a third class of men—the most noble—who assemble to observe the locations, the beautiful works of art, the displays of valor, and the literary productions which are usually exhibited on such occasions. Thus, in this life, men of all kinds of pursuits are collected together in one and the same place. Some are influenced by the desire for riches and luxury; others by the love of power and dominion; and others are possessed by an obsessive ambition for glory. But the purest and most genuine character is that of the man who devotes himself to the contemplation of the most beautiful things, and whom it is proper to call a philosopher.<sup>1</sup> He adds that the observation of the entire heavens, and of the stars that revolve within them, is indeed beautiful when their order is considered. For they derive this beauty and

<note>1 Iamblichus derived this very beautiful passage from Heraclides Ponticus, as is evident from Cicero, *Tusculan Disputations*, book 5, chapter 3, who relates the same story about Pythagoras from the aforementioned author.</note>

<summary>Pythagoras gains great honor in Italy and is credited with coining the term "philosopher," comparing life to a public festival where the highest class of people are those who observe and contemplate beauty rather than seek profit or fame.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Crotonians, philosopher, public spectacle, contemplation, Heraclides Ponticus, Cicero, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras’s philosophical definitions, transitioning from the beauty of the celestial bodies to his unique influence over irrational animals.</meta>

order through participation in the primary and intelligible essence. But that primary essence is the nature of number and productive principles, which pervades all things, and according to which all these celestial bodies are elegantly arranged and appropriately adorned. True wisdom, properly so called, is a certain science concerned with the primary beautiful objects <note>1 That is, with intelligible things properly so called.</note>—those that are divine, unchanging, and possessing an eternal consistency of being. Through participation in these, other things may also be called beautiful. <term>Philosophy</term>, however, is the desire for such a thing. Therefore, the pursuit of <term>erudition</term> is likewise beautiful, which <term>Pythagoras</term> promoted in order to improve mankind.

### CHAP. XIII.

MOREOVER, if we may believe the many ancient and credible historians who have written about him, the words of <term>Pythagoras</term> possessed a persuasive and advisory quality that extended even to irrational animals. From this, it can be inferred that learning prevails in those endowed with intellect, since it tames even wild beasts and those considered to be without reason. For it is said that <term>Pythagoras</term> captured the <term>Daunian bear</term>, which had severely harmed the inhabitants. After gently stroking it with his hand for a long time, feeding it maize and acorns, and compelling it by an oath to no longer touch any living thing, he released it. The bear immediately hid herself in the mountains and woods and was never seen attacking any animal from that time on. Similarly, noticing an ox at <term>Tarentum</term> feeding in a pasture and eating green beans among other things, he advised the herdsman to tell the

<summary>Pythagoras defines wisdom as the study of divine, unchanging numbers and principles, and he demonstrates the power of his teachings by reportedly taming a wild bear and an ox.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, philosophy, wisdom, intelligible essence, Daunian bear, Tarentum, animal taming, ethics, productive principles</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagoras’s supernatural influence over animals and transitions into his teachings on the soul's transmigration, specifically his claim of having lived previous lives.</meta>

ox to abstain from the beans. The herdsman, however, laughed at him and said that he did not understand the language of oxen; but if Pythagoras did, it was pointless to ask him to speak to the ox, but more appropriate that he himself should advise the animal to abstain from such food. Pythagoras, therefore, approaching the ear of the ox and whispering into it for a long time, not only caused him then to refrain from beans, but it is said that he never tasted them afterward. This ox also lived for a long time at Tarentum near the temple of Juno, where it remained until it was old, and was called the sacred ox of Pythagoras. It was also fed with human food by those who came to see it. Likewise, when he happened to be conversing with his associates about birds, symbols, and omens, and was observing that all these are the messengers of the Gods, sent by them to those men who are truly dear to the Gods, he is said to have brought down an eagle that was flying over Olympia, and after gently stroking it, to have released it. Through these actions, therefore, and others similar to them, he demonstrated that he possessed the same dominion over savage animals as Orpheus, and that he charmed and held them by the power of the voice proceeding from his mouth.

### CHAP. XIV.

With him, likewise, the best principle originated of a protective attention to the concerns of men, which ought to be accepted beforehand by those who intend to learn the truth about other things. For he reminded many of his associates, by very clear and evident indications, of the former life which their soul lived before it was bound to this body, and demonstrated by undeniable arguments that he had been Euphorbus, the son of Panthus, who conquered Patroclus. And he especially
