<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagoras’s claim of having lived previous lives, specifically his identity as the Trojan hero Euphorbus, and introduces his methods of education through the senses and music.</meta>

praised the following funeral Homeric verses pertaining to himself, sang them most elegantly to the lyre, and frequently repeated them.

> “ The shining circlets of his golden hair,
> Which even the Graces might be proud to wear,
> Instarred with gems and gold, strew the shore
> With dust dishonored, and deformed with gore.
> As the young olive in some sylvan scene,
> Crowned by fresh fountains with eternal green,
> Lifts the gay head, in snowy florets fair,
> And plays and dances to the gentle air;
> When lo! a whirlwind from high heaven invades
> The tender plant, and withers all its shades;
> It lies uprooted from its genial bed,
> A lovely ruin now defaced and dead.
> Thus young, thus beautiful, Euphorbus lay,
> While the fierce Spartan tore his arms away.” ¹

But regarding what is told about the shield of this Phrygian Euphorbus, which was dedicated among other Trojan spoils to Argive Juno, we shall omit it, as it is a very widely known story. What he wished to indicate through all these details, however, is this: that he knew the former lives he had lived, and that from this point he began his providential care for others, reminding them of their own former lives.

### CHAP. XV.

Believing, however, that the first care which should be given to men is that which occurs through the senses—such as when someone perceives beautiful figures and forms, or hears beautiful rhythms and melodies—he established the first stage of education to be that which exists through music, and also through certain

melodies and rhythms, from which the remedies for human habits and passions are obtained, together with those harmonies of the soul's powers which it possessed from the beginning. He likewise devised medicines designed to repress and expel the diseases of both bodies and souls. And, by Jupiter, what deserves to be mentioned above all these details is this: that he arranged and adapted for his disciples what are called preparations and <term>contrectations</term> (soothing applications), divinely contriving mixtures of certain <term>diatonic</term>, <term>chromatic</term>, and <term>enharmonic</term> melodies. Through these, he easily transferred and redirected the passions of the soul when they had recently been formed in an irrational and secret manner—such as sorrow, rage, and pity, absurd emulation and fear, all kinds of desires, angers, and appetites, pride, sluggishness, and violence. For he corrected each of these by the rule of virtue, tempering them through appropriate melodies as if through certain healing medicines.

In the evening, likewise, when his disciples were retiring to sleep, he liberated them by these means from daily disturbances and tumults, and purified their intellectual power from the incoming and outgoing waves of a physical nature; he rendered their sleep quiet, and their dreams pleasing and prophetic. But when they rose again from their bed, he freed them from nighttime heaviness, lethargy, and sluggishness through certain unique songs and modulations, produced either by simply striking the lyre or by using the voice. 

Pythagoras, however, did not achieve this for himself through instruments or the voice. Instead, employing a certain unspeakable divinity that is difficult to grasp, he extended his hearing and fixed his intellect upon the sublime symphonies of the world. He alone, it appears, heard and understood the universal harmony and consonance of the <term>spheres</term> and the stars that move through them, which produce a fuller and more intense

This melody was also the result of dissimilar and variously differing sounds, speeds, magnitudes, and intervals, arranged in relation to each other in a certain most musical ratio, and thus producing a most gentle, and at the same time varied and beautiful, motion and convolution. Being, therefore, bathed, as it were, in this melody, having the reasoning of his intellect well-ordered through it, and, as I may say, exercised, he determined to show certain images of these things to

**1** <note>Simplicius was a 6th-century Neoplatonist philosopher.</note> "The Pythagoreans," says Simplicius, in his Commentary on the 2nd book of Aristotle's treatise *On the Heavens*, said, "that a harmonic sound was produced from the motion of the celestial bodies, and they scientifically gathered this from the analogy of their intervals; since not only the ratios of the sun and moon, of Venus and Mercury, but also of the other stars, were discovered by them." Simplicius adds, "Perhaps Aristotle’s objection to this assertion of the Pythagoreans may be solved according to the philosophy of those men, as follows: All things are not commensurate with each other, nor is everything perceivable by everything else, even in the sublunary region. This is evident from dogs who scent animals at a great distance, which are not smelled by men. How much more, therefore, in things which are separated by so great an interval—as those which are incorruptible from the corruptible, and celestial from terrestrial natures—is it true to say that the sound of divine bodies is not audible to terrestrial ears? But if anyone like Pythagoras, who is reported to have heard this harmony, should have his terrestrial body set aside from him, and his luminous and celestial vehicle **¹** and the senses which it contains purified—either through a good allotment, or through integrity of life, or through a perfection arising from sacred operations—such a person will perceive things invisible to others, and will hear things inaudible to others. With respect to divine and immaterial bodies, however, if any sound is produced by them, it is neither percussive nor destructive, but it excites the powers and energies of sublunary sounds, and perfects the sense which is coordinated with them. his disciples as much as possible, especially producing an imitation of them through instruments and through the voice alone. For he believed that he alone, of all the inhabitants of the earth, understood and heard the cosmic sounds, and this from their very natural source and root. He therefore thought himself worthy to be taught, and to learn something about the celestial orbs, and to be made like them by desire and imitation, as he was the only one on earth adapted to this by the configuration of his body, through the divine power that inspired him. But he understood that other men ought to be satisfied with looking to him and the gifts he possessed, and with being benefited and corrected through images and examples, because of their inability to truly comprehend the first and genuine archetypes of things. Just, indeed, as for those who are incapable of looking intently at the sun because of the transcendent splendor of its rays, we manage to show the eclipses of that

> also has a certain analogy to the sound which occurs with the motion of terrestrial bodies. But the sound which is with us, because of the sound-producing nature of the air, is a certain activity of the motion of their unaffected sound. If, then, air is not affected there, it is evident that neither will the sound which is there be affected. Pythagoras, however, seems to have said that he heard the celestial harmony as he understood the harmonic proportions in the numbers of the heavenly bodies and that which is audible in them. Someone, however, may very properly wonder why the stars are seen by our sense of sight, but their sound is not heard by our ears? To this we reply that we do not even see the stars themselves; for we do not see their true sizes, or their shapes, or their surpassing beauty. Neither do we see the motion through which the sound is produced; but we see, as it were, a reflection of them, like the light of the sun around the earth when the sun itself is not seen by us. Perhaps, too, it will not be surprising that the sense of sight—as it is more immaterial, existing through activity rather than through being acted upon, and greatly transcending the other senses—should be thought worthy to receive the splendor and illumination of the celestial bodies, while the other senses are not adapted for this purpose. Of these, however, and similar details, if anyone can assign more probable causes, let him be considered a friend, and not an enemy.”

For the words *transcendent*, and *he beheld everything*, and the *wealth of intellect*, and the like, especially demonstrate the illustrious nature of the structure of his mind and body, and its superior accuracy in sight, hearing, and intellectual perception.

### CHAPTER XVI.

This adaptation of souls was therefore achieved by him through music. But another purification of the <term>dianoetic</term> part, <note>1</note> and at the same time of the whole soul, through various studies, was carried out by him as follows: He generally believed that labor should be devoted to disciplines and studies, and, like a legislator, ordained trials of the most diverse nature, punishments, and restraints by fire and sword, for

In addition to these things, he also ordered his close followers to abstain from eating all animals, and furthermore from certain foods that are hostile to the power of reason and hinder its natural energies. He likewise commanded them to practice restraint in speech and perfect silence, training them for many years in the control of the tongue and in a vigorous and persistent investigation and review of the most difficult theorems. Therefore, he also ordered them to abstain from wine, to be frugal with their food, to sleep little, and to maintain a natural contempt for and opposition to fame, wealth, and similar things. He taught them to have a sincere reverence for those to whom reverence is due, a genuine likeness and kindness toward their peers, and a supportive encouragement toward their juniors, free from all envy.

Regarding the friendship that exists among all things—whether it is the relationship of the Gods toward men through piety and scientific contemplation, or of doctrines toward each other, or generally of the soul toward the body and of the rational part toward the irrational part through philosophy and the theory pertaining to it; or whether it is the friendship of men for each other—of citizens through sound legislation, but of strangers through a correct physiology; or of a husband for his wife, or of brothers and relatives through pure connection; or whether, in short, it is the friendship of all things toward all others, and even of certain irrational animals through justice and a natural connection and association; or whether it is the peace and harmony of the body, which is itself mortal, and of its hidden conflicting powers, achieved through health and a diet and self-control suited to it, in imitation of the healthy state of the world's elements—of the title for all these things, which are summarized under one and the same name, that of

<meta>This page concludes the discussion of Pythagoras's teachings on universal friendship and the purification of the soul's "divine eye" through intellectual discipline, transitioning into his strict methods for screening and testing new students.</meta>

friendship, Pythagoras is acknowledged to have been the inventor and legislator. In short, he was the cause of his disciples having the most appropriate communication with the Gods, both when they were awake and when they were asleep. This is something that never takes place in a soul disturbed by anger, pain, pleasure, or—by Jupiter—by any other base desire, or defiled by ignorance, which is more unholy and harmful than all of these. Through all these methods, therefore, he divinely healed and purified the soul, revived and saved its divine part, and guided its divine eye toward the intelligible world. As Plato says, this eye is better worth saving than ten thousand physical eyes; for by looking through this alone, when it is strengthened and clarified by appropriate aids, the truth pertaining to all beings is perceived. Referring therefore to this, Pythagoras purified the <term>dianoetic</term> power of the soul. Such was his form of education, and these were the things toward which he directed his view.

### CHAPTER XVII.

As he thus prepared his disciples for education, he did not immediately accept those who came to him for that purpose into the number of his associates until he had tested and judiciously examined them. Therefore, in the first place, he inquired into how they behaved toward their parents and the rest of their relatives. Next, he observed their inappropriate laughter, their silence, and their speaking when it was not proper; and furthermore, what their desires were, with whom they associated, how they conversed with them, how they primarily employed their leisure time during the day, and what were the subjects of their joy and grief. He likewise observed their physical form, their way of walking, and the entire movement of their body. <term>Physiognomically</term> also considering the

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Pythagoras's rigorous examination of potential students, detailing the initial three-year observation period and the subsequent five-year vow of silence required before they could see the master himself.</meta>

...the natural indications of their physical build, he made these manifest signs of the hidden character of the soul. When, therefore, he had tested someone in this way, he allowed him to be ignored for three years, meanwhile observing his disposition toward stability and a true love of learning, and whether he was sufficiently prepared regarding fame, so as to despise <insert>popular</insert> honor. After this, he ordered those who came to him to observe a five-year silence, so that he might know by experience how they were affected regarding the restraint of speech—the subjugation of the tongue being the most difficult of all victories, as those who established the mysteries have revealed to us. During this <insert>probationary</insert> time, however, each person's property was managed in common and was committed to the care of those appointed for this purpose, who were called politicians, administrators <note>economizers</note>, and legislators. 

Regarding these probationers, those who appeared worthy to share in his <term>dogmas</term>—based on the judgment he formed of them from their lives and the modesty of their behavior—became *<term>Esoterics</term>* after the five-year silence, and they both heard and saw Pythagoras himself from within the veil. For prior to this, they participated in his words through hearing alone from outside the veil, without seeing him at all, providing for a long time a sample of their own unique character. But if they were rejected, they received double the wealth they had brought, and a tomb was raised for them as if they were dead by the *<term>homacoï</term>*; for this is what all the man's disciples were called. And if they happened to meet them afterward, they behaved toward them as if they were different people, saying that those whom they had modeled through education—expecting them to become truly good men through the disciplines they would learn—were dead. They were also of the opinion that those who were slower in acquiring knowledge were poorly organized and, I might say, imperfect

They expelled him from the *Homacoion* <note>lecture hall or assembly</note>, loading him with a great quantity of silver and gold. For these funds were deposited by them in common and were committed to the care of certain people suited to this purpose, who were called <term>Economics</term> <note>stewards or administrators</note> because of the office they held. And if they happened to meet such a person afterward, they considered him to be anyone other than the man who, according to them, was dead. Because of this, Lysis—blaming a certain person named Hipparchus because he had shared the doctrines of the Pythagoreans with the uninitiated, and with those who approached them without the necessary discipline and study—writes the following:

If, therefore, you abandon these habits, I shall rejoice; but if not, you will be dead in my opinion. For it will be righteous to remember the divine and human teachings of Pythagoras, and not to make the riches of wisdom common to those,"

...who have not, even in a dream, had their souls purified. For it is not lawful to extend to every casual person things which were obtained with such great labor and such diligent assiduity, nor to divulge the mysteries of the Eleusinian Goddesses to the profane. For those who do either of these are equally unjust and impious. But it is well to consider what a great length of time we spent wiping away the stains that had insinuated themselves into our breasts until, after the lapse of some years, we became fit recipients of the doctrines of Pythagoras. For just as dyers first purify garments and then set the colors with which they wish them to be imbued—so that the dye may not be washed away and may never fade—in the same manner, that divine man prepared the souls of those who were lovers of philosophy, so that they might not disappoint him in any of those beautiful and good qualities which he hoped they would possess. For he did not impart false doctrines or traps, in which most of the <term>sophists</term> (who spend their leisure on no good purpose) entangle young men; rather, he possessed a scientific knowledge of things human and divine. 

These men, however, using his doctrine as a pretext, perform many dreadful deeds, ensnaring youth in ways that are neither appropriate nor even accidental. Consequently, they make their listeners harmful and impulsive. For they infuse theorems and divine doctrines into confused and turbid characters—just as if someone were to pour pure and clear water into a deep well full of mud; for he would stir up the mud and ruin the clear water. The same thing happens between those who teach and those who are taught in this way. For dense thickets full of briars surround the intellect and heart of those who have not been purely initiated into the disciplines; these obscure the mild, tranquil, and reasoning power of the soul, and openly prevent the intellectual part from growing and being elevated. It is

<meta>This page continues the text describing the purification of the soul, using the metaphor of clearing a dense thicket of passions before the seeds of philosophy can be planted.</meta>

It is likewise necessary to call intemperance and avarice the mothers of these thickets, for both are naturally prolific. From intemperance, therefore, blossom unlawful marriages, unjust desires, corruptions, intoxication, unnatural pleasures, and certain intense appetites which drive those who possess them into depths and over precipices. For now, desires have compelled some not to abstain even from their mothers or their daughters; and violating the law, their country, city, and king, they are violently dragged along like slaves to extreme destruction, as if their hands were bound behind them. But from avarice germinate theft, robbery, parricide, sacrilege, sorcery, and other such sister evils. In the first place, therefore, it is necessary to clear the woods in which these passions have made their home, using fire and sword and all the tools of discipline; and having liberated the power of reasoning from such mighty evils, we may then implant in and deliver to it something useful and good." Such was the great and necessary attention which, according to Pythagoras, ought to be paid to disciplines prior to philosophy. He likewise ordained that a singular honor and the most accurate investigation should be given to the teaching and sharing of his dogmas, as he judiciously examined the ideas of those who came to him through various documents and ten thousand forms of scientific theory.

### CHAP. XVIII.

After this, we must narrate how, when he had admitted certain persons to be his disciples, he distributed them into different classes according to their respective merits. For it was not fit that all of them should equally participate in the same things, as they were naturally dissimilar; nor was it indeed right that

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras’s organizational methods, detailing how he distributed his disciples into different classes based on their merit and capacity for learning.</meta>

...some should participate in all the most honorable lectures, while others in none, or should not partake of them at all. For this would be exclusionary and unjust. Therefore, while he shared an appropriate portion of his discourses with each person, he benefited all of them as much as possible and maintained the balance of justice by making each a participant in the lectures according to their merit. Hence, in accordance with this method, he called some of them <term>Pythagoreans</term>, but others <term>Pythagorists</term>; just as we call some men <term>Attics</term>, but others <term>Atticists</term>. Having thus appropriately divided their names, he considered some of them to be genuine members, but he ordained that others should show themselves to be followers of these. He ordered, therefore, that among the <term>Pythagoreans</term> possessions should be shared in common and that they should always live together; but that each of the others should possess his own property separately from the rest, though they would assemble in the same place to spend their leisure time together in the same pursuits. And thus, both of these ways of life were derived from Pythagoras and passed down to his successors. 

Again, there were also two forms of philosophy among the <term>Pythagoreans</term>; for there were likewise two classes of those who pursued it: the <term>Acusmatici</term> and the <term>Mathematici</term>. Of these, however, the <term>Mathematici</term> are acknowledged as <term>Pythagoreans</term> by the rest; but the <term>Mathematici</term> do not admit that the <term>Acusmatici</term> are truly so, or that they received their instruction from Pythagoras, but rather from <term>Hippasus</term>. Regarding <term>Hippasus</term>, some say that he was a <term>Crotonian</term>, but others a <term>Metapontine</term>. The philosophy of the <term>Acusmatici</term> consists of lectures unaccompanied by demonstrations or a reasoning process; this is because it simply orders a thing to be done in a certain way, and they must strive to preserve such other things as were said by him as if they were divine dogmas. They, however, claim that they will not speak of these things and that they are not to be spoken of; but they con-

The auditions, therefore, which signify what a thing is, are such as: What are the islands of the blessed? The sun and moon. What is the oracle at Delphi? The <term>tetractys</term>. What is harmony? --- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean oral teachings (auditions), specifically those defining the nature of things and moral conduct. It also completes the footnote from the previous page regarding the relationship between number, intellect, and the soul.</meta>

...most excellent? <term>Felicity</term>. What is that which is most truly stated? That men are depraved. Hence they say that Pythagoras praised the Salaminian poet Hippodomas because he sings:

> Tell, O Gods! the source from which you came,
> Say from where, O men! you became so evil?

These, therefore, and others like them, are the <term>auditions</term> of this kind. For each of these shows what a thing especially is. This, however, is the same as what is called the wisdom of the Seven Wise Men. For they investigated not what is simply good, but what is especially so; nor what is difficult, but what is *most* difficult—namely, for a man to know himself. Nor did they investigate what is easy, but what is *most* easy—namely, to do what you are accustomed to doing. For it seems that such <term>auditions</term> as the above are consistent with, but later in time than, the wisdom of the Seven Wise Men, since they existed before Pythagoras. 

The <term>auditions</term> likewise, regarding what should or should not be done, were such as: That it is necessary to father children, for it is necessary to leave behind those who may worship the Gods after we are gone. That it is necessary to put the shoe on the right foot first. That it is not proper to walk in the public ways, nor to dip in a sprinkling vessel, nor to be washed in a public bath. For in all these cases, it is not evident whether those who use them are pure. Others also of this kind are the following: Do not assist a man in laying a burden down, for it is not proper to be the cause of avoiding labor; but assist him

---

wisdom are the same. But by the 'founder of names,' he obscurely signified the soul, which indeed exists from intellect; it is not the things themselves like the first intellect, but possesses the images and <term>essential transitive reasons</term> of them as statues of beings. Being, therefore, is imparted to all things from intellect, which knows itself and is full of wisdom; but that they are named is from the soul, which imitates intellect. Pythagoras therefore said that it was not the job of just any random person to create names, but of one looking toward intellect and the nature of things.”

...in picking it up. Do not approach a woman for the purpose of procreating children if she is wearing gold. Do not speak about Pythagorean ¹ matters without light. Perform libations to the Gods from the handle of the cup for the sake of a favorable omen, and so that you do not drink from the same part from which you poured the liquid. Do not wear the image of God in a ring, so that it is not defiled; for it is an image that should be kept in the house. It is not right to treat a woman poorly, for she is a <term>suppliant</term>. For this reason, we also bring her from the Vestal hearth and take her by the right hand. Nor is it proper to sacrifice a white rooster; for this also is a <term>suppliant</term> and is sacred to the moon. For this same reason, it announces the hours. To someone who asks for advice, give no other counsel than that which is the best, for counsel is a sacred thing. Hardships are good, but pleasures are in every respect bad. For since we came into this present life for the purpose of punishment, it is necessary that we should be punished. It is proper to sacrifice and to enter temples barefoot. When going to a temple, it is not proper to turn out of the way, for the divine should not be worshipped in a careless manner. It is good to endure and to have wounds in the chest, but it is bad to have them in the back. The soul of man alone does not enter into those animals which it is lawful to kill. Therefore, it is proper to eat only those animals which it is appropriate to kill, but no other animal whatever. And such were the <term>auditions</term> of this kind.

The most extensive teachings, however, were those concerning sacrifices: how they should be performed at all other times, and likewise when departing from this life; and concerning <term>sepulture</term>, and in what manner it is proper to be buried. For some of these, therefore, the reason why they are commanded must be given; such as, for instance, that it is necessary to father children, for the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean precepts, moving from the specific reasons behind behavioral rules to the overarching theological principle that all actions should be directed toward following God.</meta>

...sake of leaving behind another who may worship the Gods in your place. But for others, no reason is to be assigned. Indeed, for some, the reasons are assumed directly, but for others, remotely; such as the rule that bread is not to be broken because it contributes to the judgment in <term>Hades</term>. However, the probable reasons added about things of this kind are not <term>Pythagoric</term>; instead, they were devised by some who philosophized differently from the Pythagoreans and who endeavored to apply probability to what was said.

For instance, regarding what has just been mentioned—why bread is not to be broken—some say that it is not proper to dissolve that which brings people together. For in the past, all those who were friends assembled in an ancient manner around one piece of bread. But others say that it is not proper, at the start of an undertaking, to produce an omen of this kind by breaking and diminishing.

Moreover, all such precepts that define what should or should not be done refer to divinity as their ultimate goal, and every life is organized so as to follow God. This is also the principle and the doctrine of philosophy. For men act ridiculously when they search for good anywhere else but from the Gods. When they do so, it is just as if someone in a country governed by a king should revere one of the citizens who is a magistrate but neglect the one who is the ruler of them all. For the Pythagoreans thought that the men we have just mentioned performed a thing of this kind. For since God exists and is the lord of all things, it is universally acknowledged that good should be requested from Him. For all men give good things to those they love and those with whom they are delighted, but they give the opposite of good to those to whom they are negatively disposed. Such indeed is the wisdom of these precepts.

There was, however, a certain person named Hippomedon, an *Aegean*, a Pythagorean and one of the *<term>Acusmatici</term>*, who asserted that Pythagoras gave the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the two groups within the Pythagorean school. There is a textual gap between the end of the previous page ("the opposite of good") and the start of this page ("reasons and demonstrations").</meta>

...reasons and demonstrations for all these precepts. However, because these were delivered to many people—including those of a slower intellect—the demonstrations were removed, and only the problems themselves were left. Those Pythagoreans called the *Mathematici* acknowledge that these reasons and demonstrations were added by Pythagoras. They say even more than this and contend that their assertions are true, but they affirm that the following circumstance caused the dissimilarity.

Pythagoras, they say, came from Ionia and Samos during the tyranny of Polycrates, when Italy was in a flourishing condition, and the leading men in the city became his associates. But for the older associates, who lacked the leisure <insert>for philosophy</insert> because they were occupied with political affairs, Pythagoras's discourse was not accompanied by a reasoning process. This was because it would have been difficult for them to grasp his meaning through systematic studies and demonstrations. He believed they would still benefit from knowing what should be done, even if they lacked the knowledge of the *why*—just as those under the care of physicians regain their health even if they do not hear the reason for everything that is done for them. But with his younger associates, who were able both to act and learn, he conversed through demonstration and academic disciplines.

These, therefore, are the assertions of the *Mathematici*, while the former are those of the *Acusmatici*. Regarding Hippasus specifically, they assert that he was one of the Pythagoreans, but because he revealed and described the method of forming a sphere from twelve pentagons,¹ he perished in the

sea, as an impious person, but gained the fame of having made the discovery. In reality, however, this, as well as everything else pertaining to geometry, was the invention of *that man*; for in this way, without mentioning his name, they refer to Pythagoras. But the Pythagoreans say that geometry was revealed under the following circumstances: a certain Pythagorean happened to lose the wealth he possessed, and as a result of this misfortune, he was permitted to enrich himself through geometry. But geometry was called *Historia* by Pythagoras. And so much for the difference between each mode of philosophizing and the classes of Pythagoras's listeners. For those who heard him either within or outside the veil, and those who heard him while seeing him or without seeing him—and who are divided into interior and exterior listeners—were none other than these. And it is necessary to categorize the political, economic, and legislative Pythagoreans under these groups.

### CHAPTER XIX.

Generally, however, it is worth knowing that Pythagoras discovered many paths of learning and that he delivered a suitable portion of wisdom according to the nature and ability of each person; the greatest evidence for this is as follows. When Abaris the Scythian came from the Hyperboreans, unskilled and uninitiated in Greek learning and already at an advanced age, Pythagoras did not introduce him to learning through various theorems. Instead of requiring silence, a long period of listening, and other trials, he immediately considered him fit to be a listener to his doctrines and instructed him in the shortest way through his treatise *On Nature* and another treatise *On the Gods*. For Abaris came from the Hyper-

<meta>This page continues the account of Abaris the Scythian's arrival from the land of the Hyperboreans and his initial encounter with Pythagoras.</meta>

boreans, being a priest of the Apollo who is worshipped there, an elderly man, and most wise in sacred matters. At that time, however, he was returning from Greece to his own country so that he might consecrate to the God in his temple among the Hyperboreans the gold which he had collected. Passing, therefore, through Italy and seeing Pythagoras, he especially compared him to the God of whom he was the priest. Believing that he was none other than the God himself, and that no man resembled him but that he was truly Apollo—both from the venerable signs he saw about him and from those which the priest had known before—he gave Pythagoras a dart which he had taken with him when he left the temple, as a thing that would be useful to him in the difficulties that would befall him on such a long journey. 

For he was carried by it when passing through inaccessible places, such as rivers, lakes, marshes, mountains, and the like; through it, as it is said, he performed purifications and expelled pestilence and winds from the cities that requested him to liberate them from these evils. We are informed, therefore, that Lacedæmon, after having been purified by him, was no longer infested with pestilence, though prior to this it had frequently fallen into this evil through the harmful nature of the place in which it was built; the mountains of Taygetus produced a suffocating heat by being situated above the city, in the same manner as Cnossus in Crete. Many other similar details are related concerning the power of Abaris. 

Pythagoras, however, upon receiving the dart, was neither astonished at the novelty of the gift nor did he ask the reason why it was given to him; but as if he were in reality a God himself, he took Abaris aside and showed him his golden thigh as an indication that Abaris was not <note>wholly</note> deceived <note>in the opinion he had formed of him</note>. Having enumerated the various items that were deposited in the temple, he gave Abaris sufficient reason to believe that he had

...not mistaken <note>in comparing him to Apollo</note>. Pythagoras also added that he came <note>into the world of mortals</note> for the purpose of helping and improving the condition of humanity, and for this reason, he had taken a human form, so that people, troubled by the unusual nature of his transcendence, would not avoid the teachings he possessed. He also urged Abaris to stay in that place and join him in improving <note>the lives and behaviors</note> of those they might encounter, and to share the gold he had collected with his associates. These associates were guided by reason to prove through their actions the principle *that the possessions of friends are common.* Thus, Pythagoras explained physiology and theology in a concise way to Abaris, who stayed with him, as we have just said; and instead of divination by the internal organs of animals, he taught him the art of predicting the future through numbers, believing that this was purer, more divine, and better suited to the heavenly numbers of the Gods. He also taught Abaris other subjects that were suited to him. To return, however, to the purpose for which this treatise was written, Pythagoras sought to correct and improve different people according to the nature and capacity of each. Therefore, not all such details have been passed down to the knowledge of men, nor is it easy to recount everything that has been passed down to us about him.

### CHAP. XX.

However, we shall present a few examples, and the most famous ones, of the Pythagorean discipline, as well as the records of the studies in which those men were involved. First, therefore, Pythagoras, in testing <note>the suitability of those who came to him</note>

<summary>Pythagoras instructs Abaris in theology and numerology, emphasizing the communal sharing of goods among friends. The text then transitions to describing the specific methods and disciplines used by Pythagoras to test and teach his students.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Abaris, Apollo, numerology, divination, Pythagorean discipline, theology, physiology, communal property</keywords>

--- Page 65 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Pythagoras’s methods for vetting potential students, specifically focusing on the character traits and mental disciplines he required before admitting them into his school.</meta>

him] he considered whether they could practice *echemuthein*, that is, whether they were able to refrain from speaking (for this was the word which he used), and he examined whether they could keep silence and preserve what they had learned and heard. Next, he observed whether they were modest. For he was much more anxious that they should be silent than that they should speak. He likewise directed his attention to every other detail, such as whether they were overwhelmed by the power of any immoderate passion or desire. Nor did he consider in a superficial manner how they were affected by anger or desire, or whether they were argumentative or ambitious, or how they were inclined toward friendship or conflict. And if, after examining all these details accurately, they appeared to him to be endowed with virtuous manners, then he directed his attention to their ease in learning and their memory.

In the first place, he considered whether they were able to follow what was said with speed and clarity; and in the next place, whether they possessed a certain love and self-control regarding the disciplines they were taught. For he examined how they were naturally inclined toward gentleness. He called this *catartysis*, that is, *elegance of manners*. And he considered ferocity as hostile to such a method of education. For impudence, shamelessness, lack of self-control, laziness, slowness in learning, unrestrained licentiousness, disgrace, and the like are the traits of savage manners; but the opposites belong to gentleness and mildness. He considered these things, therefore, when testing those who came to him, and in these areas, he trained the students. Those who were suited to receive the benefits of the wisdom he possessed, he admitted as his disciples, and thus endeavored to raise them to scientific knowledge. But if he perceived that any one of them was unsuited, he expelled him as if he were of another tribe and a stranger.

<summary>Pythagoras rigorously evaluated potential students on their ability to maintain silence, their emotional self-control, and their natural aptitude for learning. He prioritized gentle manners and the ability to keep secrets, rejecting any candidates who displayed "savage" traits like impudence or laziness.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, education, silence, echemuthein, catartysis, character assessment, discipline, discipleship</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the daily routine and disciplinary practices Pythagoras established for his students after they had been vetted and admitted to his school.</meta>

Next, I will describe the studies that he provided to his associates throughout the day. For those who committed themselves to the guidance of his doctrine acted in the following way: they took their morning walks alone in places where there was an appropriate solitude and quiet, and where there were temples and groves and other things designed to give delight. For they thought it was improper to converse with anyone until they had made their own souls calm and had harmonized their reasoning power. For they understood it to be a turbulent thing to mingle in a crowd as soon as they rose from bed. For this reason, all the Pythagoreans always selected the most sacred places for themselves. But after their morning walk, they associated with each other, especially in temples, or if this was not possible, in places that resembled them. Likewise, they used this time for the discussion of doctrines and disciplines and for the correction of their manners.

### CHAP. XXI.

After this kind of association, they turned their attention to the health of the body. Most of them, however, used unction <note>The practice of rubbing the body with oil.</note> and the course <note>Running or racing.</note>; but a smaller number employed themselves in wrestling in gardens and groves; others in jumping with leaden weights in their hands or in pantomime gestures, with a view to the strength of the body, carefully selecting different exercises for this purpose. Their dinner consisted of bread and honey or the honeycomb, but they did not drink wine during the day. They also spent the time after dinner on the social management pertaining to strangers and guests, in accordance with the mandate of the laws. For they wished to transact all

It emphasizes the importance of starting the day in quiet contemplation before engaging in social or physical activities.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoreans, daily routine, meditation, morning walk, temples, physical exercise, unction, diet, hospitality, social duties</keywords>

--- Page 67 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the daily routine of the Pythagoreans, specifically focusing on their evening activities and dietary habits.</meta>

business of this kind in the hours after dinner. But when it was evening, they again went out to walk; yet not alone as in the morning walk, but in parties of two or three, recalling as they walked the disciplines they had learned, and exercising themselves in beautiful studies. After they had walked, they made use of the bath; and having washed themselves, they assembled in the place where they ate together, which contained no more than ten who met for this purpose. These, however, being gathered together, libations and sacrifices were performed with incense and frankincense. After this they went to supper, which they finished before sunset. They made use of wine and maza <note>A type of barley cake or porridge.</note>, and bread, and every kind of food that is eaten with bread, as well as raw and boiled herbs. The meat of such animals was also placed before them as it was lawful to sacrifice; but they rarely fed on fish, for this food was not, for certain reasons, beneficial to them. In a similar manner, they were also of the opinion that any animal which is not naturally harmful to the human race should neither be injured nor killed. After this supper, libations were performed, and these were succeeded by readings. It was the custom among them for the youngest to read, and the eldest ordered what was to be read and in what manner. But when they were about to depart, the cup-bearer poured out a libation for them; and once the libation was performed, the eldest announced the following precepts to them: That a gentle and fruitful plant should neither be injured nor destroyed, nor, in a similar manner, any animal which is not harmful to the human race. Furthermore, that it is necessary to speak piously and form proper conceptions of the divine, demonic <note>Referring to "daimones," or intermediate spirits between gods and humans.</note>, and heroic classes; and in a similar manner, of parents and benefactors. That it is likewise proper to support the law and to be hostile to lawlessness. Once these things were said, each departed to his own home.

of abode. They also wore white and pure garments. In a similar manner, they lay on pure and white beds, the coverlets of which were made of thread; for they did not use woolen coverlets. Regarding hunting, they did not approve of it, and therefore did not engage in that kind of exercise. These, therefore, were the precepts that were daily delivered to the disciples of Pythagoras regarding nutrition and their way of living.

## CHAPTER XXII.

Another method of education is also passed down to us, which was achieved through Pythagorean precepts and maxims that applied to human life and opinion; I shall relate a few of these out of many. One of these contains an exhortation to remove contention and strife from true friendship—and especially from all friendship, if possible. But if this is not possible, at least to remove it from friendship with parents, and generally from that which exists with elders and benefactors. For to contend <term>pervicaciously</term> with these individuals—when anger or some other similar passion intervenes—is not to preserve, but to destroy the existing friendship. They say it is necessary that only the smallest "lacerations" and "ulcerations" <note>Metaphors for minor disagreements or wounds to the relationship.</note> should take place in friendships. This will be achieved if both friends know how to yield and subdue their anger, especially the younger of the two and whoever belongs to one of the previously mentioned ranks. They likewise thought it necessary that the corrections and admonitions which they called <term>pædartases</term>, and which the elder used toward the younger, should be performed with great gentleness of manners and great caution; they also believed that much care and suitability should be shown in these admonitions. For in this way, the admonition

<summary>This page describes the Pythagoreans' preference for white garments and thread bedding, their rejection of hunting, and their ethical guidelines for maintaining friendships through the suppression of anger and gentle correction.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Pythagorean lifestyle, white garments, friendship, pædartases, contention, education, ethics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Pythagorean ethics regarding friendship and introduces the symbolic method of teaching used by Pythagoras, influenced by Egyptian traditions.</meta>

will become proper and beneficial. They also say that trust should never be separated from friendship, neither in seriousness nor in jest. For it is no longer easy for an existing friendship to remain in a healthy condition once falsehood insinuates itself into the behavior of those who claim to be friends. And again they say that friendship should not be rejected on account of misfortune or any other weakness that occurs in human life; rather, the only praiseworthy rejection of a friend and of friendship is that which occurs because of great and incurable vice. Such, therefore, was the form of correction among the Pythagoreans through maxims, which extended to all the virtues and to the whole of life.

### CHAPTER XXIII.

However, the method of teaching through symbols was considered by Pythagoras to be most necessary. For this form of education was cultivated by nearly all the Greeks, as it was very ancient. But it was exceptionally honored by the Egyptians and adopted by them in the most diverse ways. Accordingly, it will be found that Pythagoras paid great attention to it, if anyone clearly reveals the meanings and secret concepts of the Pythagorean symbols, and thus develops the great integrity and truth they contain, freeing them from their enigmatic form. For they are adapted, according to a simple and consistent doctrine, to the great geniuses of these philosophers, and they deify in a way that surpasses human understanding. For those who came from this school—especially the most ancient Pythagoreans, and also those young men who were the disciples of Pythagoras when he was an old man, namely—

<meta>This page continues the list of prominent Pythagoreans and explains the intentional obscurity of their symbolic writing style, designed to protect sacred knowledge from the uninitiated.</meta>

Philolaus<note>In this list of Pythagoreans, Iamblichus should not be thought to list only those who were contemporary with Pythagoras. If he did, he would contradict what he says of Philolaus in Chapter 31—specifically, "that he lived many generations after Pythagoras." Instead, he refers generally to those who came from the school of Pythagoras and were his most celebrated disciples.</note> and Eurytus, Charondas and Zaleucus, and Brysson, the elder Archytas also, and Aristaeus, Lysis and Empedocles, Zanolxis and Epimenides, Milo and Leucippus, Alcmaeon, Hippasus and Thymaridas, and all of that age—comprising a multitude of learned men who were exceptionally excellent—all these adopted this mode of teaching in their discourses with each other, and in their commentaries and annotations. Their writings also, and all the books which they published—*most of which have been preserved even to our time*<note>From this passage it is clear that Iamblichus had many sources of information that are unknown to modern critics; this fact alone should restrain their educational arrogance.</note>—were not composed by them in a popular and common language, or in a manner usual with all other writers so as to be immediately understood. Instead, they were written in such a way as not to be easily grasped by those who read them. For they adopted that silence which was established as a law by Pythagoras, concealing divine mysteries from the uninitiated in a secret manner, and obscuring their writings and discussions with each other. Hence, anyone who selects these symbols but does not reveal their meaning through a proper explanation will cause those who happen to encounter them to consider them ridiculous and empty, and full of triviality and wordiness. When, however, they are explained in a way consistent with these symbols, and become obvious and clear even to the public—instead of being obscure and dark—then they will be found to be similar to prophetic sayings and the oracles of the Pythian Apollo. They will then also display a wonderful meaning and will produce a divine inspiration in those who combine intellect with

<summary>This page lists several famous Pythagorean philosophers and explains that they intentionally wrote in an obscure, symbolic style to keep divine mysteries hidden from the uninitiated. Only through proper explanation do these symbols reveal their profound, oracle-like wisdom and divine inspiration.</summary>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean symbolic teaching, providing specific examples of their maxims, and begins a new chapter detailing the dietary regulations Pythagoras established for his followers.</meta>

erudition. Nor will it be improper to mention a few of them, so that this method of discipline may become clearer: Do not enter a temple negligently, nor, in short, worship carelessly, even if you should stand at the very doors themselves. Sacrifice and worship barefoot. Turning away from the public roads, walk in unfrequented paths. Do not speak about Pythagorean matters without light. These are the outlines of the method Pythagoras adopted for teaching through symbols.

### CHAP. XXIV.

Since, however, nutrition greatly contributes to the best discipline when it is used properly and in an orderly manner, let us consider what Pythagoras also established as a law concerning this. Generally, therefore, he rejected all food that is gas-producing and a cause of disturbance, but he approved of the nutrition contrary to this and ordered it to be used—namely, such food as settles and strengthens the constitution of the body. For this reason, he also thought that millet was a plant well-suited for nutrition. 

However, he completely rejected any food that is foreign to the Gods, because it withdraws us from intimacy with the Gods. Again, in another way, he ordered his disciples to abstain from food that is considered sacred, as it is worthy of honor and should not be used for common or human utility. He likewise urged them to abstain from things that are an obstacle to prophecy, or to the purity and chastity of the soul, or to the habit of temperance or virtue. Lastly, he rejected all things that are opposed to sanctity, and which cloud and disturb the other purities of the soul and the visions that occur in sleep. These things, therefore, he established as general laws regarding nutrition.

<summary>Pythagoras utilized symbolic maxims to guide behavior in sacred spaces and private life, and he established strict dietary laws favoring foods like millet to maintain physical stability and spiritual purity.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, symbolic teaching, dietary laws, nutrition, spiritual purity, millet, temperance, prophecy, sacred food, visions</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean dietary laws, moving from general principles of nutrition to specific prohibitions against eating animals and particular plant or animal parts based on a person's spiritual status.</meta>

Separately, however, he forbade the most contemplative of philosophers—those who have reached the summit of philosophical achievement—from using superfluous and unjust food. He ordered them never to eat anything living, nor to drink wine, nor to sacrifice animals to the Gods, nor to injure animals in any way, but to most earnestly maintain justice toward them. He himself lived in this manner, abstaining from animal food and worshipping at altars undefiled by blood. He was likewise careful to prevent others from destroying animals that share a similar nature with us; he chose to correct and instruct savage animals through words and deeds rather than injuring them through punishment. Furthermore, he also urged those politicians who were legislators to abstain from eating animals. Since they wished to act with the highest degree of justice, it is certainly necessary that they should not injure any related animal. For how could they persuade others to act justly if they themselves were found indulging an insatiable greed by consuming animals that are allied to us? For through a shared life and the same elements, and the mixture arising from these, they are, as it were, joined to us by a brotherly bond. 

He permitted others, however, whose lives were not entirely purified, sacred, and philosophical, to eat certain animals; and for these people, he appointed a specific time of abstinence. He ordered these people, therefore, not to eat the heart or the brain; he entirely prohibited all <term>Pythagoreans</term> from eating these parts. For these organs are of a governing nature and are, in a sense, the ladders and seats of wisdom and life. But other <note>For the word "these" here, I read "other," following the version by *Obrechtus*.</note> things were considered by him to be sacred because of the nature of a divine reason. Thus, he exhorted his disciples to abstain from mallows, because this plant is the first messenger and signal of the sympathy of

<summary>Pythagoras established strict dietary tiers, forbidding philosophers and legislators from eating animals to maintain justice and kinship with living beings. He also prohibited all followers from eating hearts and brains, regarding them as sacred seats of wisdom.</summary>

And also not to eat the fish <term>erythinus</term>, for other similar reasons. He likewise urged them to abstain from beans, on account of many sacred and physical causes, and also such causes as pertain to the soul. And he established other similar precepts as laws, beginning through nutrition to lead men toward virtue.

### CHAP. XXV.

Pythagoras was likewise of the opinion that music contributed greatly to health if it was used in an appropriate manner. For he was accustomed to use a purification of this kind, but not in a careless way. And he called the medicine which is obtained through music by the name of purification. He employed such a melody as this during the springtime. For he placed in the middle a certain person who played the lyre, and seated in a circle around him those who were able to sing. And thus, when the person in the center struck the lyre, those who surrounded him sang certain <term>paeans</term>, through which they were seen to be delighted, and to become elegant and orderly in their manners. But at another time they used music in the place of medicine. And there are certain melodies devised as remedies against the passions of the soul, and also against despondency and lamentation,<note>For <term>δήγμους</term> here, I read <term>ὀδυρμούς</term>; as I do not see what *morsus* (gnawing) has to do with this place. Obrechtus has in his version "pectorisque morsus" (gnawing of the heart); but I have no doubt lamentations is the proper word, which aptly associates with despondency.</note> which Pythagoras invented as things that afford the greatest assistance in these maladies. And again, he employed other melodies against rage and anger, and

against every aberration of the soul. There is also another kind of modulation invented as a remedy against desires. He likewise used dancing, but employed the lyre as an instrument for this purpose. For he believed that the pipe was designed to excite insolence, was a theatrical instrument, and by no means had a noble sound.<note>“Well-established states,” (says Proclus in his manuscript commentary on the first *Alcibiades*) “are opposed to the art of playing wind instruments; and therefore, Plato does not allow it either. The reason for this is the complexity of this instrument, the pipe, which shows that the art using it should be avoided. For instruments called <term>Panarmonia</term>, and those consisting of many strings, are imitations of pipes. For every hole of the pipe emits, as they say, at least three sounds; but if the cavity above the holes is opened, then each hole will emit more than three sounds.”</note> Select verses from Homer and Hesiod were also used by him for the purpose of correcting the soul.

Among the deeds of Pythagoras, it is also said that he once extinguished the rage of a Tauromenian youth through the <term>spondaic</term> song of a piper. The youth had been feasting by night and intended to burn down the entrance to his mistress's house because he had seen her coming from the house of his rival. For the youth was inflamed and excited to this rash attempt by a <term>Phrygian</term> song, which Pythagoras, however, very quickly suppressed. While Pythagoras was engaged in astronomy, he happened to meet the Phrygian piper at an unseasonable time of night and persuaded him to change his <term>Phrygian</term> song for a <term>spondaic</term> one. Through this, the youth’s fury was immediately repressed, and he returned home in an orderly manner, although just before this, he could not be restrained at all, nor would he listen to any warning; he had even stupidly insulted Pythagoras when he met him. 

When a certain youth also rushed with a drawn sword at Anchitus, the host of Empedocles, because Anchitus, acting as a judge, had publicly condemned the youth's father to death—and would have killed him as a murderer—Empedocles

changed the intention of the youth by singing to his lyre that verse of Homer,

->Nepenthe, without bitterness, over every ill<-

and thus snatched his host Anchitus from death, and the youth from the crime of homicide. It is also related that from that time on, the youth became the most celebrated of the disciples of Pythagoras. Furthermore, the whole Pythagorean school produced, through certain appropriate songs, what they called *exartysis* (adaptation), *synarmoge* (elegance of manners), and *epaphe* (contact), usefully directing the dispositions of the soul toward passions contrary to those which it previously possessed. For when they went to bed, they purified their reasoning power from the disturbances and noises to which it had been exposed during the day by using certain odes and peculiar songs; by this means, they procured for themselves tranquil sleep and a few good dreams. But when they rose from bed, they again liberated themselves from the torpor and heaviness of sleep by songs of another kind. Sometimes, also, by musical sounds alone, unaccompanied by words, they healed the passions of the soul and certain diseases—truly enchanting them, as they say. And it is probable that from this, the name *epode* (that is, enchantment) came to be generally used. In this manner, therefore, Pythagoras used music to produce the most beneficial correction of human manners and lives.

Since, however, we are narrating the wisdom employed by Pythagoras in instructing his disciples, it will not be inappropriate to relate that which is next in order...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras’s use of music for moral and physical improvement, transitioning into the specific story of how he discovered the mathematical ratios of musical harmony.</meta>

in the following order: namely, how he invented the harmonic science and harmonic ratios. But for this purpose, we must begin a little further back. Once, while intently considering and reasoning with himself whether it would be possible to devise a certain mechanical aid for hearing that would be reliable and unerring—much like the sight gains through the compass and the ruler, or, by Jupiter, through a dioptric instrument; or such as the touch obtains through the balance or the invention of measures—while thus considering, as he was walking near a smith's shop, he heard by a certain divine coincidence the hammers beating out a piece of iron on an anvil and producing sounds that accorded with each other, with only one exception. But he recognized in those sounds the harmony of the <term>diapason</term>, the <term>diapente</term>, and the <term>diatessaron</term>. He saw, however, that the sound which was between the <term>diatessaron</term> and the <term>diapente</term> was dissonant on its own, yet it nevertheless completed the larger sound among them.

Delighted, therefore, to find that the discovery he was so anxious to make had succeeded according to his wishes through divine assistance, he went into the smith's shop. There he found through various experiments that the difference in sound arose from the weight of the hammers, but not from the force of the strokes, the shape of the hammers, or the positioning of the iron that was being beaten. When, therefore, he had accurately examined the weights and the equal balance of the hammers, he returned home and fixed a single beam diagonally against the walls, so that if there were many, no difference would arise from that circumstance—or, in short, so that the unique nature of each beam would not cause a suspicion of variation. Afterward, from this beam, he suspended four strings made of the same material, and of the same size and thickness, and equally twisted. To the end of each string, he also tied a weight. And

--- Page 77 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Pythagoras's experiments with weights and sound, following his observation of the blacksmith's hammers. He now applies these principles to a controlled apparatus using strings and weights to define the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</meta>

When he had arranged it so that the strings were perfectly equal to each other in length, he then alternately struck two strings at once and found the previously mentioned harmonies—namely, a different harmony in each different combination. For he discovered that the string which was stretched by the greatest weight produced, when compared with that which was stretched by the smallest, the harmony <term>diapason</term>. The former of these weights was twelve pounds, and the latter was six. Therefore, being in a <term>duple ratio</term>, it exhibited the <term>consonance</term> <term>diapason</term>, as the weights themselves made apparent. 

But again, he found that the string from which the greatest weight hung, when compared with the one from which the second-smallest weight hung (which weight was eight pounds), produced the harmony <term>diapente</term>. Hence he discovered that this harmony is in a <term>sesquialter ratio</term>, which was also the ratio of the weights to each other. And he found that the string which was stretched by the greatest weight produced, when compared with the one next to it in weight (which was nine pounds), the harmony <term>diatessaron</term>, in proportion to the weights. 

This ratio, therefore, he discovered to be <term>sesquitertian</term>; but the ratio of the string from which a weight of nine pounds hung to the string which had the smallest weight <note>or six pounds</note> was <term>sesquialter</term>. For 9 is to 6 in a <term>sesquialter ratio</term>. In the same way, the string next to the one from which the smallest weight hung was to the string which had the smallest weight in a <term>sesquitertian ratio</term> <note>for it was the ratio of 8 to 6</note>, but to the string which had the greatest weight, it was in a <term>sesquialter ratio</term> <note>for such is the ratio of 12 to 8</note>. 

Hence, that which is between the <term>diapente</term> and the <term>diatessaron</term>, and by which the <term>diapente</term> exceeds the <term>diatessaron</term>, is proved to be in an <term>epogdoan ratio</term>, or that of 9 to 8. But either way it may be proved that the <term>diapason</term> is a system consisting of the <term>diapente</term> in conjunction with the <term>diatessaron</term>, just as the <term>duple ratio</term>...

<summary>Pythagoras demonstrates that musical harmonies correspond to specific mathematical ratios by hanging weights of 12, 9, 8, and 6 pounds from strings of equal length. He proves that the octave, fifth, and fourth are defined by duple, sesquialter, and sesquitertian ratios respectively.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the explanation of Pythagoras's musical experiments, detailing how he applied mathematical ratios found in weights to stringed instruments and other sound-producing objects to define specific musical intervals and the diatonic scale.</meta>

...consists of the <term>sesquialter</term> and <term>sesquitertian</term>, as for instance, 12, 8, and 6; or conversely, of the <term>diatessaron</term> and the <term>diapente</term>, as in the <term>duple ratio</term> of the <term>sesquitertian</term> and <term>sesquialter</term> ratios, as for instance 12, 9, and 6. In this manner, therefore, and in this order, having trained both his hand and his hearing to the suspended weights, and having established based on them the ratio of the relations, he transferred by a simple method the shared suspension of the strings from the diagonal stake to the bridge of the instrument, which he called the *<term>chordotonon</term>*. But he produced, by the aid of pegs, a tension of the strings analogous to that created by the weights.

Employing this method, therefore, as a foundation and as an infallible rule, he afterward extended the experiment to various instruments: namely, to the striking of *<term>patellæ</term>* (or pans), to pipes and reeds, to <term>monochords</term>, triangles, and the like. And in all these, he found an unchangeable harmony with the ratio of numbers. He named the sound which corresponds to the number 6 *<term>hypate</term>*; that which corresponds to the number 8 and is <term>sesquitertian</term>, *<term>mese</term>*; that which corresponds to the number 9, but is higher in pitch by a tone than *<term>mese</term>*, he called *<term>paramese</term>* and *<term>epogdous</term>*; and that which corresponds to the <term>dodecad</term> <note>the number 12</note>, he called *<term>nete</term>*. Having also filled up the middle spaces with analogous sounds according to the <term>diatonic genus</term>, he formed an <term>octochord</term> from harmonious numbers—namely, from the <term>duple</term>, the <term>sesquialter</term>, the <term>sesquitertian</term>, and from the difference of these, the <term>epogdous</term>. And thus he discovered the <note>harmonic</note> progression, which moves by a certain physical necessity from the lowest <note>that is, the most grave or flat</note> to the highest <note>the most acute</note> sound, according to this <term>diatonic genus</term>. For from the <term>diatonic</term>, he made the <term>chromatic</term> and <term>enharmonic</term> genera clear, as we shall at some time or other show when we treat of music. This <term>diatonic genus</term>, however, appears to have such physical gradations and progressions as the following: namely, a

--- Page 79 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical description of Pythagoras's musical system, explaining how he mathematically structured intervals like the fourth and fifth into scales, and begins a new chapter on the political influence and moral teachings of his followers.</meta>

...a semitone, a tone, and then another tone. This is the <term>diatessaron</term>, a system consisting of two tones and what is called a semitone. Afterward, by adding another tone—namely, the intermediate one—the <term>diapente</term> is produced, which is a system consisting of three tones and a semitone. Next to this is the system of a semitone, a tone, and a tone, forming another <term>diatessaron</term> (that is, another <term>sesquitertian ratio</term>). Thus, in the more ancient <term>heptachord</term>, all the sounds—starting from the lowest—that are a fourth apart from each other produce the <term>diatessaron</term> harmony everywhere. In this arrangement, the semitone shifts its position, appearing in the first, middle, or third place according to the <term>tetrachord</term>.

In the Pythagorean <term>octachord</term>, however—which is a system formed by joining a <term>tetrachord</term> and a <term>pentachord</term>, or if separated, a system of two <term>tetrachords</term> apart from each other—the progression begins from the lowest sound. Therefore, all the sounds that are a fifth apart produce the <term>diapente</term> harmony with each other, while the semitone moves successively through four places: namely, the first, second, third, and fourth. In this manner, it is said that music was discovered by Pythagoras. After reducing it to a system, he passed it on to his disciples as a tool for everything that is most beautiful. <note>Iamblichus took what he wrote in this chapter about music from Nicomachus.</note>

### CHAP. XXVII.

Many of the political actions of his followers are also <insert>deservedly</insert> praised. It is reported that when the people of Croton were once driven to hold extravagant funerals and burials, one of them told the people that he had heard Pythagoras, when he was

<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagoras’s influence in Croton, transitioning from his political advice to his specific teachings on the nature of the gods and his methods of arbitration.</meta>

<page-num>66</page-num>

when discussing divine natures, they observe that the Olympian Gods paid attention to the dispositions of those who sacrificed, and not to the quantity of the sacrifices; but that, on the contrary, the terrestrial Gods—having been allotted the government of less important things—rejoiced in banquets and lamentations, and furthermore, in continual libations, in delicacies, and in celebrating funerals with great expense. Because of this, due to his desire to receive, Pluto is called Hades. He therefore allows those who honor him sparingly to remain for a longer time in the upper world; but he always draws down someone from among those who are inclined to spend lavishly on funeral rituals, so that he may obtain the honors which take place in memory of the dead. As a result of this advice, the Crotonians who heard it were of the opinion that if they behaved moderately during misfortunes, they would preserve their own well-being; but that if they were immoderate in their expenses, they would all die prematurely.

A certain person, having also been made an arbitrator in an affair in which there was no witness, led each of the litigants to a particular monument and said to one of them, "The man who is buried in this monument was exceptionally fair." Consequently, the other litigant prayed that the dead man might receive much good; but the first man said that the deceased was not at all better off for the prayers of his opponent. Pythagoras, therefore, condemned what the first litigant said, but asserted that he who praised the dead man for his worth had done something that would be of no small importance to his claim to be believed. 

At another time, in a case of great importance, he decided that one of the two who had agreed to settle the affair by arbitration should pay four <term>talents</term>, but that the other should receive two. Afterward, he ordered the defendant to pay three <term>talents</term>; and thus he appeared to have given a <term>talent</term> to each of them. Two persons had also fraudulently

A certain Pythagorean, however, took up the case and said that the woman had acted according to the agreement, as both parties had been present.

Two other people also seemed to have a strong friendship with each other, but they had fallen into silent suspicion because of a flatterer. This flatterer told one of them that his wife had been seduced by the other. It happened, however, that a Pythagorean entered a metalworker’s shop where the man who believed himself to be wronged was showing the craftsman a sword he had given him to sharpen; he was angry with the craftsman because it was not sharp enough. The Pythagorean, suspecting that the sword was intended to be used against the man accused of adultery, said, "This sword is sharper than everything except slander." These words caused the man to reflect on his intentions <note>what he intended to do</note> and not to sin rashly against his friend, who was inside and had already been summoned <note>by him so that he might be killed</note>.

<meta>This page continues the series of anecdotes illustrating the moral conduct and social influence of the Pythagoreans, specifically focusing on their sense of justice, conflict resolution, and mutual recognition.</meta>

A similar circumstance, which is mistakenly attributed to other places by those who are uninformed, is said to have happened in Crotona. During a public spectacle, some cranes flew over the theater, and one of the men who had sailed into the port said to the person sitting near him, "Do you see the witnesses?" A certain Pythagorean heard this and brought them into the court, which consisted of a thousand magistrates. Upon being examined, it was discovered that they had thrown certain boys into the sea, and that they had called the cranes flying over the ship at the time "witnesses of the deed."

Similarly, when certain persons who had recently become disciples of Pythagoras were at odds with each other, the younger of the two went to the other and said that there was no need to refer the matter to a third person; instead, it was up to them to let their anger be forgotten. The elder person, to whom these words were addressed, replied that he was very pleased with what had been said in other respects, but he was ashamed that, being the elder, he had not been the first to say the same thing to the younger man.

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of mutual aid between Pythagoreans, specifically describing how Eubulus was saved by Nausithus and how Miltiades saved Possiden. It then transitions into the political influence and legislative contributions of the Pythagoreans in Italy and Sicily.</meta>

<page-num>69</page-num>

by him from the pirates, and brought safely to Messina. When the Carthaginians, also, were about to send more than five thousand soldiers to a desert island, Miltiades the Carthaginian, recognizing the Argive Possiden among them (both of them being Pythagoreans), went to him. Without revealing what he intended to do, he advised him to return to his native country with all possible speed. Having placed him on a ship that was then sailing near the shore, he supplied him with everything necessary for his voyage, and thus saved the man from the dangers to which he was exposed. In short, anyone who tried to relate all the events that have occurred among the Pythagoreans in their interactions with each other would, by the length of the narration, exceed the proper limits and the purpose of this treatise.

I shall, therefore, move on to show that some of the Pythagoreans were political figures and well-suited to govern. They were guardians of the laws and ruled over certain Italian cities, explaining and advising them to adopt the most excellent measures while abstaining from the use of public funds. Although they were greatly slandered, the integrity of the Pythagoreans and the wishes of the cities themselves prevailed, so that they were requested to manage their political affairs. At this time, the most excellent forms of government seem to have existed in Italy and in Sicily. Charondas of Catana, who appears to have been one of the best legislators, was a Pythagorean, as were the Locrians Zaleucus and Timares, who were celebrated for their legislation. Those who established the Rhegian governments—the one called <term>Gymnasiarchic</term> and the one named after Theocles—are also said to have been Pythagoreans. Phytius, Theocles, Elecaon, and Aristocrates also excelled among the Pythagoreans in their studies and conduct, which the cities in those regions also adopted.

<summary>This page concludes the accounts of mutual aid among Pythagoreans and describes their significant influence as legislators and political leaders in Southern Italy and Sicily.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Pythagoreans' role in government and introduces Pythagoras's philosophical views on politics, the nature of elements, and the geometric modeling of a political system. It also describes his moral influence on the citizens of Croton.</meta>
