at those times. In short, it is asserted that Pythagoras was the inventor of all political learning when he said that nothing is pure among existing things; instead, earth partakes of fire, fire of air, air of water, and water of spirit. In a similar way, the beautiful partakes of the deformed, the just of the unjust, and other things accordingly. From this hypothesis, however, the reasoning extends in both directions. He also said that there are two motions of the body and the soul: one being irrational, and the other the result of deliberate choice. He also said that three specific lines constitute <term>polities</term>, the ends of which touch each other and form one right angle; so that one of them is <term>sesquitertian</term> in nature, another is a <term>diapente</term>, and the third is a medium between the other two.<note>These lines correspond to the numbers 4, 3, and 2. For the ratio of 4 to 3 is <term>sesquitertian</term>, 3 to 2 is <term>sesquialter</term>, and 3 is an arithmetical medium between 4 and 2.</note> But when we consider, through a process of reasoning, how these lines coincide with each other, and also the areas beneath them, we shall find that they represent the best image of a <term>polity</term>. Plato claimed the glory of this invention for himself; for he clearly says in his *Republic*, "that the <term>sesquitertian</term> progeny joined with the pentad produces two harmonies."<note>For an explanation of this statement by Plato in the *Republic*, see my *Theoretical Arithmetic*.</note> It is also said that Pythagoras cultivated the moderation of the passions and the middle path, and that by combining them with a certain preceding good, he made the life of each of his disciples happy. And in short, it is said that he discovered how to choose what is good for us and the works adapted to our nature. It is likewise told of him that he drew the Crotonians away from prostitutes and generally from associating with women who were not their wives. For the wives of the Crotonians came to Theano, the wife of Brontinus, who was one of the

<summary>Pythagoras is credited with originating political philosophy through his theories on the mixture of elements and the use of geometric ratios to model an ideal state. He also emphasized the moderation of passions and successfully reformed the moral conduct of the citizens of Croton.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, political learning, elements, geometry, Plato, Republic, Theano, Crotonians, morality, sesquitertian, diapente, middle path</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras's influence on the social and political life of Italy and Sicily, specifically focusing on his moral impact on the women of Croton and his strict adherence to ethical principles in diplomacy.</meta>

<page-num>71</page-num>

Among the Pythagoreans, there was a woman of a wise and excellent soul (who was the author of that beautiful and admirable saying, “that it is lawful for a woman to sacrifice on the very day she has risen from her husband’s embrace,” which some attribute to Theano, the wife of Pythagoras). The wives of the Crotonians therefore came to her and pleaded with her to persuade Pythagoras to speak to them about the self-restraint they owed their husbands. She promised to do this; and accordingly, Pythagoras delivered an oration to the Crotonians which had the desired effect: the lack of restraint that then prevailed was entirely eliminated. It is also related that when ambassadors from Sybaris came to the city of the Crotonians to demand the return of the exiles, Pythagoras, seeing one of the ambassadors who had killed one of his own friends with his own hand, gave him no answer. But when the man questioned him and wished to speak with him, Pythagoras said that it was not lawful to speak with murderers. Because of this, some people even thought he was Apollo. All these details, therefore, along with those we mentioned a little earlier concerning the destruction of tyrants and the liberation of the cities of Italy and Sicily, as well as many other circumstances, are signs of the benefits Pythagoras brought to humanity in political matters.

---

### CHAPTER XXVIII.

From this point forward, we will no longer discuss matters generally, but will focus specifically on the works resulting from Pythagoras's virtues. We shall begin first with the Gods, as is customary, and endeavor to demonstrate his piety and the wonderful works he performed. Let this, then, be one example of his piety, which

<summary>Pythagoras improves the moral conduct of the women in Croton and refuses to speak with a murderer acting as an ambassador. The text transitions into a specific discussion of Pythagoras's piety and his miraculous works concerning the Gods.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, Theano, Crotonians, Sybaris, Apollo, morality, self-restraint, piety, political influence, Italy, Sicily</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of Pythagoras's supernatural abilities and his divine reputation, transitioning from his political influence to specific miracles and his knowledge of the soul.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>

We have also mentioned before that he knew what his soul was, from where it came into the body, and also its former lives; he gave very clear evidence of these things. After this, let the following also be another example: once, while crossing the river Nessus with many of his associates, he spoke to it, and the river—in a distinct and clear voice heard by all his followers—answered, "*Hail, Pythagoras!*" Furthermore, nearly all historians of his life confidently assert that on one and the same day he was present at Metapontum in Italy and Tauromenium in Sicily, and conversed with his disciples in both places, even though these cities are separated from each other by many stadia <note>A stadium was an ancient Greek unit of distance, approximately 185 meters.</note> both by land and sea, and cannot be traveled between even in many days.

The report is also widely spread that he showed his golden thigh to Abaris the Hyperborean, who said that Pythagoras resembled the Apollo among the Hyperboreans, and of whom Abaris was the priest; he did this so that Abaris might understand this to be true and realize he was not mistaken in his opinion. Ten thousand other more divine and more admirable details are likewise uniformly and unanimously told about *the man*: such as infallible predictions of earthquakes, the rapid banishment of plagues and violent winds, the instantaneous stopping of hailstorms, and the calming of the waves of rivers and seas so that his disciples might easily cross over them. 

Having received the power to perform such things, Empedocles of Agrigentum, Epimenides the Cretan, and Abaris the Hyperborean also performed certain miracles of this kind in many places. Their deeds, however, are well known. To this we may add that Empedocles was nicknamed "*an expeller of winds*"; Epimenides, "*an expiator*"; and Abaris, "*a walker on air*"; because, being carried on the arrow which was given to him by the Hyperborean Apollo, he passed over rivers, seas, and inaccessible places as if walking on air. Certain

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagoras's miraculous deeds, specifically his ability to be in two places at once and his prophetic powers, before transitioning into the theological and philosophical foundations of Pythagorean piety.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>

Other people are also of the opinion that Pythagoras did the same thing when, on the same day, he spoke with his disciples at Metapontum and Tauromenium. It is also said that he predicted there would be an earthquake after tasting the water from a well, and that a ship which was sailing with a favorable wind would sink in the sea. And let these, indeed, be the indicators of his piety.

Starting again, however, from a more elevated beginning, I wish to show the principles of the worship of the Gods established by Pythagoras and his followers; namely, that all the specific rules they define regarding what should or should not be done aim for one goal: harmony with the divine. This is also their principle <note>of piety</note>, and their entire lives are organized with the goal to *follow God*. The language of their philosophy is also this: that men act ridiculously when they seek good from any source other than the Gods. Their behavior in this regard resembles that of a person living in a country ruled by a king who honors one of the city's minor officials but neglects the sovereign ruler of them all. For they were of the opinion that this is how mankind behaves. Since God exists and is the Lord of all things, it is universally acknowledged that good should be requested from him. For all people give good things to those they love and those they are pleased with, but they give the opposite of good to those they dislike. It is evident, therefore, that we should do those things in which God delights. However, it is not easy for a person to know what these things are unless he learns them from someone who has heard God, has heard God himself, or acquires the knowledge through the divine art. For this reason, the Pythagoreans were also dedicated students of divination. For this alone provides an interpretation of the benevolence of the Gods. And in short, he will consider an occupation of this kind to be worthy of regard, who

It emphasizes that true "good" comes only from the Gods and justifies the study of divination as a means to understand the divine will.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the Pythagorean belief in divinity and their acceptance of the supernatural, transitioning from the source of divine knowledge to the specific credibility they gave to miraculous stories and the absolute power of the Gods.</meta>

...believes that there are Gods; but he who thinks that either of these is folly will also be of the opinion that both are foolish. Many of the mandates of the Pythagoreans, however, were introduced from the mysteries; for they did not conceive them to be the products of arrogance, but to originate from a certain divinity. And in a similar manner, all the Pythagoreans believe such things as are mythologically related of Aristeas the Proconesian, and Abaris the Hyperborean, and other particulars of a like nature. For they consider everything of this kind to be credible; and of many [such] things they make trial themselves. They also frequently recollect such details that appear to be fabulous, as they do not disbelieve in anything which may be referred to divinity. 

Therefore, a certain person relates that Eurytus said that a shepherd feeding his sheep near the tomb of Philolaus heard someone singing. But the person to whom this was related did not at all disbelieve the narration, but asked what kind of harmony it was. Both of them, however, were Pythagoreans, and Eurytus was the disciple of Philolaus. It is likewise said that a certain person told Pythagoras that it seemed to him that he once conversed with his father who was dead, and he asked Pythagoras what this indicated. Pythagoras replied that it indicated nothing, but that he had in reality conversed with his father. He said, "As therefore nothing is signified by my now speaking with you, so neither is anything signified by your conversing with your father." 

Hence, in all particulars of this kind, they did not think that they were stupid, but rather those who disbelieved in them. For they did not conceive that some things are possible to the Gods while others are impossible, as those imagine who reason sophistically; but they believed that all things are possible to the Gods. And this very assertion is the beginning of the verses which they ascribe to Linus, and which are as follows:

But they thought that their opinions deserved to be believed because he who first promulgated them was not just any ordinary person, but a God. For this was one of their questions: "What was Pythagoras?" For they say that he was the Hyperborean Apollo; of which this was an indication: that standing up in the Olympic games, he showed his golden thigh; and also that he received the Hyperborean Abaris as his guest and was presented by him with the dart on which he rode through the air. But it is said that Abaris came from the Hyperborean regions in order to collect gold for the temple and that he predicted a plague. He also lived in temples and was never seen to either eat or drink. It is likewise said that rites which purify from evil are performed by the Lacedaemonians and that, for this reason, Lacedaemon was never infested with plague. Pythagoras, therefore, caused this Abaris to acknowledge <insert>that he was more than man</insert>, receiving from him at the same time the golden dart, without which it was not possible for him to find his way. In Metapontum also, certain people were praying that they might obtain what a ship contained that was then sailing into port; Pythagoras said to them, "You will then have a dead body." In Sybaris, too, he caught a deadly serpent and released it. In a similar manner likewise in Tyrrhenia, he caught a small serpent whose bite was fatal. But in Crotona, it is said that a white eagle allowed Pythagoras to stroke it. When a certain person also wished to hear him discourse, he said that he could not until some sign appeared. And after this, a white bear was seen in Cauconia; he predicted its death to someone who was about to tell him that it was dead. He likewise reminded Myllias the Cro-

...tonian that he had been Midas, the son of Gordius. And Myllias passed over to the continent of Asia in order to perform at the tomb <note>of Midas</note> those rites which had been commanded him by Pythagoras. It is likewise said that the person who bought his house, and who dug up that which had been buried in it, did not dare to tell anyone what he saw <note>on this occasion</note>. But instead of suffering for that offense, he was arrested at Crotona for sacrilege and put to death. For he stole a golden beard which had fallen from a statue. These things, therefore, and others of the same kind, are related by the Pythagoreans in order to make their opinions worthy of belief. And as these are acknowledged to be true, and it is impossible they should have happened to one man, they consequently think it is clear that what is related of Pythagoras should be received as pertaining to a being superior to man, and not to a mere human. This is also the meaning of their enigmatic assertion that *man, bird, and another third thing, are bipeds.* For the third thing is Pythagoras. Such, therefore, was Pythagoras on account of his piety, and such he was truly thought to be.

With respect to oaths, however, all the Pythagoreans religiously observe them, being mindful of the Pythagorean precept,

> First, pay your homage to the immortal Gods,
> As they are orderly disposed by law;
> And reverence your oath, but honor next
> The illustrious heroes.

Hence, a certain Pythagorean, being compelled by law to take an oath, chose to pay three talents instead of swearing—this being the fine he was ordered to pay to the defendant—even though he would have sworn truthfully, simply so that he might preserve a Pythagorean doctrine. Pythagoras, however, thought that nothing resulted from chance and fortune,

...but that all events happened in conformity with divine providence, and especially to good and pious men, is confirmed by what is related by <term>Androcydes</term> in his treatise on Pythagorean Symbols, concerning <term>Thymaridas</term> of Tarentum, a Pythagorean. For when, due to a certain circumstance, he was about to sail from his own country, and his friends who were present were embracing him and bidding him farewell, someone said to him, as he had just boarded the ship:
> May such things happen to you from the Gods, O Thymaridas, as are in accordance with your wishes!
But he replied:
> Predict better things; for I would rather wish that such things happen to me as are in accordance with the will of the Gods.
For he thought it was more rational and fair not to resist or be indignant toward divine providence. If, therefore, anyone wishes to learn the sources from which these men derived so much piety, it must be said that a clear model of Pythagorean theology based on numbers is, in a certain respect, to be found in the writings of <term>Orpheus</term>. Nor is it to be doubted that <term>Pythagoras</term>, receiving aid from Orpheus, composed his treatise *Concerning the Gods*, which for this reason he also titled the *Sacred Discourse*, because it contains the finest parts of the most mystical passages in Orpheus; whether this work was in reality written by Pythagoras, as most authors say it was, or—as some of the Pythagorean school who are both learned and worthy of belief assert—was composed by <term>Telauges</term>. They claim it was taken by him from the commentaries which were left by Pythagoras himself to his daughter <term>Damo</term>, the sister of Telauges, and which it is said after her death were given to <term>Bitale</term>, the daughter of Damo, and to Telauges, the son of Pythagoras and the husband of Bitale, when he reached a mature age. For when Pythagoras died, Telauges was left very young with his mother <term>Theano</term>. <meta>This page continues the exploration of Pythagoras’s teachings on number as the governing principle of divinity and its practical application in divination and faith.</meta>

number. *Through these same numbers, he also produced an admirable foreknowledge and worship of the Gods, both of which are especially closely allied to numbers.* This can be known from the following; for it is necessary to provide a certain fact in order to gain belief in what is said. When Abaris performed sacred rites in his accustomed manner, he obtained a foreknowledge of future events (which is diligently practiced by all the Barbarians) by sacrificing animals, especially birds; for they believe that the internal organs of such animals are useful for a more accurate inspection. Pythagoras, therefore—not wishing to suppress Abaris’s passionate pursuit of truth, but to impart it to him through a safer way without blood and slaughter, and also because he believed that a cock was sacred to the sun—*furnished him with a complete knowledge of all truth, as it is said, through the science of arithmetic.*

He also obtained faith concerning the Gods from his piety. For Pythagoras always proclaimed that nothing remarkable regarding the Gods or divine dogmas should be disbelieved, because the Gods are able to accomplish all things. And the divine dogmas which it is necessary to believe are those which Pythagoras delivered. Thus, the Pythagoreans believed in and accepted the principles they taught, because they were not the result of false opinion. For this reason, Eurytus the Crotonian, the student of Philolaus, said that a shepherd feeding his sheep near the tomb of Philolaus heard someone singing. But the person to whom this was told did not disbelieve the story at all, but instead asked what kind of harmony it was. Pythagoras himself, when asked by a person what it meant to seem to talk with his dead father in a dream, answered that it meant nothing. "For," he said, "nothing is foretold by your speaking with me either."

Pythagoras likewise wore pure and white garments,

and in a similar manner white and pure bedspreads; for he did not use those that were made of wool. This custom he also passed on to his listeners. In speaking also of beings superior to man, he used honorable titles and words of good omen, and on every occasion mentioned and reverenced the Gods. Thus, while at dinner, he performed libations <note>the pouring of a drink as an offering to a deity</note> to the divinities, and ordered his disciples to celebrate the beings above us with hymns every day. He likewise paid attention to rumors and omens, prophecies, and lots, and in short, to all chance circumstances. Moreover, he sacrificed to the Gods with millet, cakes, honeycombs, and other incense. But he did not sacrifice animals, nor did any of the contemplative philosophers. His other disciples, however—namely the *<term>acusmatici</term>* and the *<term>politici</term>*—were ordered by him to sacrifice animals, such as a cock, a lamb, or some other newborn animal, but not frequently. At the same time, they were prohibited from sacrificing oxen. This is also an indication of the honor which he paid to the Gods: that he urged his disciples never to use the names of the Gods uselessly in swearing an oath. For this reason, Syllus, one of the Pythagoreans in Crotona, paid a fine for not swearing, even though he could have sworn without violating the truth. An oath such as the following is also attributed to the Pythagoreans, as they were unwilling, out of reverence, to name Pythagoras; just as they mostly abstained from using the names of the Gods. Instead, they identified the man through his invention of the *<term>tetractys</term>*.

> I swear by him who the *<term>tetractys</term>* found,
> From which all our wisdom springs, and which contains
> Eternal *Nature's* fountain, cause, and root.

And, in short, it is said that Pythagoras was an emulator of the Orphic style of writing and [piety of] disposition; and that he honored the Gods in a similar way

<meta>This page continues the description of Pythagoras's religious and philosophical influences, specifically his adoption of Orphic traditions regarding divine images and his synthesis of various mystery traditions from Egypt, Chaldea, and beyond. to that of Orpheus, placing them in images and in brass, not joined to human forms, but to divine receptacles;¹ because they encompass and provide for all things, and have a nature and *morphe* <note>form or appearance</note> similar to the universe. He also introduced purifications and initiations, as they are called, which contain the most accurate knowledge of the Gods. And further still, it is said that he was the author of a combined divine philosophy and worship of the Gods; having learned some things from the followers of Orpheus, but others from the Egyptian priests; some from the Chaldeans and Magi; some from the mysteries performed in Eleusis, in Imbrus, Samothrace, and Delos; and some also from those performed by the Celts and in Iberia. It is also said that the *Sacred Discourse* of Pythagoras exists among the Latins, and is read not to all, nor by all of them, but by those who are readily inclined to learn what is excellent and apply themselves to nothing base. He likewise ordained that men should make libations three times, and observed that Apollo delivered oracles from the tripod because the triad is the first number. He also ordered that sacrifices should be made to Venus on the sixth day, because this number is the first that partakes of every number, and, when divided in every possible way, receives the power of the numbers subtracted and of those that remain. But he said that it is necessary to sacrifice

---

<note>1. That is, to spheres; Iamblichus indicates by this that Pythagoras, as well as Orpheus, considered a spherical shape as the most appropriate image of divinity. For the universe is spherical; and, as Iamblichus later observes, the Gods have a nature and *morphe* similar to the universe; *morphe*, as we learn from Simplicius, relates to the color, shape, and size of surfaces. Kiessling, having no understanding of this meaning and assuming the whole passage was corrupt, has made nonsense of it with his changes. According to his version, Pythagoras, in the manner of Orpheus, worshipped the Gods not as bound to a human form, but to *divine numbers*. For instead of ἵδρυμασι <note>foundations or receptacles</note> he reads ἀριθμοῖς <note>numbers</note>. But divine numbers, according to both Orpheus and Pythagoras, are the Gods themselves.</note>

<meta>This page continues the list of Pythagorean religious and ritualistic precepts, following the discussion of libations and sacrifices to Apollo and Venus. It details specific regulations for temple conduct, purification, and the treatment of the dead.</meta>

to Hercules on the eighth day of the month from the beginning, considering that he was born in the seventh month. He further asserted that it was necessary for anyone entering a temple to be clothed in a pure garment in which no one had slept; because sleep, in the same manner as black and brown colors, is an indication of sluggishness; but purity is a sign of equality and justice in reasoning. He also ordered that if blood should be found involuntarily spilt in a temple, a lustration <note>purification ritual</note> should be performed, either in a golden vessel or with seawater; the former of these <note>gold</note> being the most beautiful of things and a measure by which the price of all things is regulated; but the latter, as he conceived it, being the offspring of a moist nature and the nourishment of the first and more common matter. He likewise said that it was not proper to give birth to children in a temple; because it is not holy for the divine part of the soul to be bound to the body within a temple. He further ordained that on a festive day neither the hair should be cut nor the nails trimmed; not thinking it fit that we should leave the service of the Gods for the purpose of increasing our own good. He also said that a louse ought not to be killed in a temple, conceiving that a divine power ought not to participate in anything superfluous and corruptible. But he said that the Gods should be honored with cedar, laurel, cypress, oak, and myrtle; and that the body should not be purified with these, nor should any of them be divided by the teeth. He likewise ordained that what is boiled should not be roasted, signifying by this that mildness does not need anger. But he would not allow the bodies of the dead to be burned, following the Magi in this, as he was unwilling for anything divine to communicate with a mortal nature. He likewise thought it was holy for the dead to be carried out in white garments, obscurely signifying by this the simple and first nature, according to number and the

<summary>This page details Pythagoras’s strict religious regulations regarding temple purity, including rules on clothing, prohibited actions like childbirth or grooming, and specific rituals for purification and the treatment of the dead.</summary>

--- Page 97 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of Pythagorean religious and ritualistic precepts, focusing on the sanctity of oaths, burial customs, libations to the gods, and the symbolic significance of ritual movements within temples.</meta>

principle of all things. But above all things he ordained that an oath should be taken religiously, since what follows is long.¹ And he said that it is much more holy to be injured than to kill a man; for judgment is stored in Hades, where the soul and its essence, and the primary nature of things, are <note>properly</note> estimated. Furthermore, he ordered that burial chests <note>biers</note> should not be made of cypress, because the scepter of Jupiter was made of this wood, or for some other mystical reason. He likewise ordained that libations should be performed before the table of Jupiter the Savior, and of Hercules and the Dioscuri; in doing so, he celebrated Jupiter as the presiding cause and leader of this nourishment, Hercules as the power of nature, and the Dioscuri as the symphony of all things. But he said that libations should not be offered with closed eyes. For he did not think it right that anything beautiful should be undertaken with shame and bashfulness. Moreover, when it thundered, he ordained that the earth should be touched in remembrance of the generation of things. But he ordered that temples should be entered from the right side, and that they should be exited from the left side. For he asserted that the right hand is the principle of what is called the odd number and is divine, but the left hand is a symbol of the even number and of that which is dissolved. And such is the method which he is said to have adopted in the cultivation of piety. But other details which we have omitted concerning it may be inferred from what has been said. So I shall stop speaking further on this subject.

<note>¹ That is, futurity is long; Pythagoras signified by this that those who do not take an oath religiously will be punished in some future period, if they are not punished at present.</note>

<meta>This page transitions from describing Pythagoras's religious and ritualistic rules to evaluating his intellectual legacy through the commentaries of his followers and his own writings.</meta>

### CHAP. XXIX.

Of his wisdom, however, the commentaries written by the Pythagoreans provide, in short, the greatest indication; for they adhere to truth in everything and are more concise than all other compositions, so that they savor of the ancient elegance of style, and the conclusions are exquisitely deduced with divine science. They are also replete with the most condensed concepts and are in other respects various and diversified both in form and matter. At the same time, they are also transcendently excellent, without any deficiency in their diction, and are in an eminent degree full of clear and indubitable arguments, accompanied by scientific demonstration and, as it is said, the most perfect syllogism; as anyone will find who, proceeding in the proper paths, does not carelessly read them.

This science, therefore, concerning intelligible natures and the Gods, Pythagoras delivers in his writings from a heavenly <note>supernal</note> origin. Afterward, he teaches the whole of physics and completely unfolds ethical philosophy and logic. He likewise delivers all various disciplines and the most excellent sciences. In short, there is nothing pertaining to human knowledge which is not accurately discussed in these writings. If, therefore, it is acknowledged that of the Pythagorean writings which are now in circulation, some were written by Pythagoras himself, while others consist of what he was heard to say—and on this account are anonymous but are attributed to Pythagoras as their author—if this is the case, it is evident that he was abundantly skilled in all wisdom. But it is said that he very much applied himself to geometry among the Egyptians. For with the Egyptians there are many geometrical problems; since it is necessary that from

<summary>This chapter praises the Pythagorean commentaries for their concise, divine, and scientific nature, covering theology, physics, ethics, and logic. It notes that Pythagoras's vast wisdom is evident in both his attributed writings and his study of geometry in Egypt.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, Pythagoreans, intelligible natures, physics, ethical philosophy, logic, geometry, Egyptians, syllogism</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras’s education in geometry and his philosophical definitions of wisdom and being.</meta>

remote periods, and from the time of the Gods themselves,¹ on account of the rises and falls of the Nile, those who were skillful measured all the Egyptian land which they cultivated. Hence geometry also derived its name. Nor did they negligently investigate the theory of the celestial orbs, in which Pythagoras was likewise skilled. Moreover, all the theorems about lines appear to have been derived from there. For it is said that what pertains to computation and numbers was discovered in Phoenicia. For some persons refer the theorems about the celestial bodies to the Egyptians and Chaldeans in common. It is said, therefore, that Pythagoras, having received and increased all these theories, imparted the sciences and, at the same time, demonstrated them to his listeners with clarity and elegance. And he was indeed the first who named philosophy, and said that it was the desire and, as it were, the love of wisdom. But he defined wisdom to be the science of the truth which is in beings. And he said that beings are immaterial and eternal natures, and alone possess an effective power, such as incorporeal essences. But he said that the rest of things are only <term>homonymously</term> beings—called so only by name—and are so denominated through the participation of real beings; and such are corporeal and material forms, which are generated and corrupted, and never truly exist. And he taught that wisdom is the science of things which are properly beings, but not of such as are so only homonymously. For corporeal natures are neither the objects of science nor admit of a stable knowledge, since they are infinite and incomprehensible by science, and are, as it were, non-beings when compared with universals, and are incapable of being properly limited by definition. It is impossible, however, to conceive that there should be science of things which are not naturally the objects of science. Hence it is not probable that there will

---

<note>¹ i.e. From the time in which the Gods are mythically said to have reigned in Egypt.</note>

He argued that true science can only concern stable, incorporeal essences rather than the shifting material world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras's definitions of being and his method of teaching through universals and symbolic maxims.</meta>

...be a desire for knowledge which has no real existence, but rather that desire will be extended to things which are properly beings, which exist with invariable permanence, and are always consistent with a true name. For it happens that the perception of things which are <term>homonymously</term> beings, and which are never truly what they seem to be, follows the understanding of real beings; just as the knowledge of particulars follows the <term>science of universals</term>. For he who knows universals properly, says <term>Archytas</term>, will also have a clear perception of the nature of particulars. Hence, things which have an existence are not alone, nor only-begotten, nor simple, but they are seen to be various and multiform. For some of them are <term>intelligible and incorporeal natures</term>, which are called beings; but others are corporeal and fall under the perception of the senses, and by participation communicate with that which has a real existence. Concerning all these, therefore, he delivered the most appropriate sciences, and left nothing <note>pertaining to them</note> uninvestigated. *He likewise unfolded to men those sciences which are common <note>to all disciplines</note>,* as for instance the demonstrative, the definitive, and that which consists in dividing, as may be known from the Pythagorean commentaries. *He was also accustomed to pour forth sentences resembling oracles to his associates in a symbolic manner, which in the greatest brevity of words contained the most abundant and multifaceted meaning,* like the <term>Pythian Apollo</term> through certain oracles, or like nature herself through seeds small in size—the former exhibiting concepts and the latter effects, innumerable in multitude and difficult to be understood. Of this kind is the sentence, *The beginning is the half of the whole,* which is an <term>apothegm</term> of <term>Pythagoras</term> himself. But not only in the present <term>hemistich</term>, but in others of a similar nature, the most divine Pythagoras has concealed the sparks of truth, depositing them as in a treasury for those who are capable of being ignited by them,

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras's use of brief, symbolic maxims and transitions into his followers' interest in music, medicine, and divination.</meta>

and with a certain brevity of expression, an extension of theory most ample and difficult to be understood, as in the following <term>hemistich</term>:

->All things accord in number:<-

which he very frequently uttered to all his disciples. Or again, *Friendship is equality; equality is friendship.* Or in the word *<term>cosmos</term>*, that is, *the world*; or, by Jupiter, in the word *<term>philosophy</term>*, or in the so much celebrated word *<term>tetractys</term>*. All these and many other inventions of a similar kind were devised by Pythagoras for the benefit and improvement of his associates; and they were considered by those who understood them to be so venerable, and so much the progeny of divine inspiration, that the following was adopted as an oath by those who lived together in the common assembly:

> I swear by him who found the <term>tetractys</term>,
> And revealed it to our race; the cause and root,
> And fountain of ever-flowing Nature.

This, therefore, was the form of his wisdom, which is so admirable.

It is also said that of the sciences which the Pythagoreans honored, music, medicine, and divination were not among the least. But they were habitually silent and prompt to hear, and he who was able to hear <note>in a proper manner</note> was praised by them. Of medicine, however, they especially embraced the <term>dietetic</term> species, and in the practice of this they were most accurate. And in the first place, indeed, they endeavored to learn the indications of symmetry, of labor, food, and repose. In the next place, with respect to the preparation of food, they were nearly the first who attempted to apply themselves to it, and to define the manner in which it should be performed. The Pythagoreans likewise employed <term>cataplasms</term> more frequently than their predecessors; but they approved of medicated ointments to a lesser degree. These, however, they principally

<meta>This page continues the description of Pythagorean medical practices and focuses on their use of music and rigorous memory exercises.</meta>

used in the cure of ulcerations. But they admitted incisions and burnings least of all things. They also cured some diseases by incantations. Pythagoras, however, thought that music greatly contributed to health if it was used in a proper manner. The Pythagoreans likewise used selected sentences from Homer and Hesiod for the improvement of souls. But they thought it was necessary to retain and preserve in the memory things which they had learned and heard; and that it was necessary to be provided with <term>disciplines</term> and <term>auditions</term> to as great an extent as there was an ability for learning and remembering; the former of these being the power by which knowledge is obtained, while the latter is the power by which it is preserved. Hence, they greatly honored the memory, exercised it abundantly, and paid great attention to it. In learning, too, they did not dismiss what they were taught until they had firmly comprehended its first principles; and they recalled to their memory what they had daily heard in the following manner: A Pythagorean never rose from his bed until he had first recollected the events of the previous day; and he accomplished this by endeavoring to remember what he first said, or heard, or ordered his servants to do when he was rising, or what was the second and third thing which he said, heard, or commanded to be done. And the same method was adopted with respect to the remainder of the day. For again, he endeavored to recollect who was the first person he met on leaving his house, or who was the second; and with whom he conversed in the first, second, or third place. And he proceeded in the same manner with other things. For he endeavored to recall to his memory all the events of the whole day, and in the very same order in which each of them happened to take place. But if they had sufficient leisure after rising from sleep, they tried in the same manner to recollect the events of the third preceding day. And thus they endeavored

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean practices, moving from the cultivation of memory and the influence of his school on Italy and Greece to the foundational principles of justice.</meta>

...to exercise the memory to a great extent. For nothing is of greater importance regarding science, experience, and wisdom than the ability to remember. Because of these studies, therefore, it happened that all of Italy was filled with philosophers; and this place, which was previously unknown, was afterwards called Magna Graecia on account of Pythagoras. Consequently, it also contained many philosophers, poets, and legislators. For the rhetorical arts, <term>demonstrative reasonings</term>, and the laws written by them were transferred from Italy to Greece. Likewise, those who mention physics cite Empedocles and the Elean Parmenides as the principal <term>physiologists</term> <note>In this context, "physiologists" refers to natural philosophers or those who study the physical laws of nature.</note>. Those, too, who wish to cite maxims pertaining to the conduct of human life use the ideas of Epicharmus for this purpose. And nearly all philosophers make use of these. This is enough, therefore, concerning the wisdom of Pythagoras—how, in a certain respect, he greatly encouraged all his listeners to pursue it, as far as they were capable of participating in it, and how perfectly he delivered it.

### CHAPTER XXX.

Regarding justice, however, we shall learn in the best way how he cultivated and delivered it to mankind if we examine it from its first principle and from the initial causes from which it grows, and also direct our attention to the first cause of injustice. For in this way, we shall discover how he avoided the latter and what methods he adopted so that the former might be properly produced in the soul. The principle of justice, therefore, is commonality and equality, through which—in a way that most nearly approximates one body and one soul—all men may share the same feelings and may call the same thing "mine" and "thine"; as is also

<summary>This page explains how Pythagoras's emphasis on memory led to the intellectual flourishing of Magna Graecia and introduces his foundational principles of justice, which are rooted in equality and communal feeling.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, memory, Magna Graecia, Empedocles, Parmenides, Epicharmus, justice, equality, philosophy, Italy, Greece</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras's principles of justice, specifically focusing on his rejection of private property and his extension of ethical consideration to animals.</meta>

...testified by Plato, who learned this from the Pythagoreans. Therefore, Pythagoras accomplished this in the best way, eliminating everything private in manners, but increasing that which is common even to the extent of ultimate possessions, which are the causes of rebellion and unrest. For all things <note>among his disciples</note> were common and the same to all, and no one owned anything privately. And indeed, he who approved of this community used common possessions in the most just manner; but he who did not, received back his own property, which he had brought to the common stock, with an addition to it, and departed. Thus, he established justice in the best way, starting from its first principle.

In the next place, therefore, association with men introduces justice; but alienation and a contempt for the common species produce injustice. Wishing, therefore, to instill this familiarity from afar in men, he also ordained that his disciples should extend it to animals of the same kind, and commanded them to consider these as their associates and friends, so as neither to injure, nor kill, nor eat any of them. Therefore, he who associates men with animals—because they consist of the same elements as we do and share with us a more common life—will to a much greater degree establish fellowship with those who share a soul of the same species and also a rational soul. 

From this, it is also evident that he introduced justice produced from the most appropriate principle. Since, likewise, the lack of wealth sometimes compels many to do something contrary to justice, he well foresaw that this would be the case and, through management, procured for himself generous means and what was just in sufficient abundance. For again, a just arrangement of domestic affairs is the principle of all good order in cities. For cities are composed of households. It is said, therefore, that Pythagoras himself was the heir to the property of <term>Alcæus</term>, who died after performing an embassy to the <term>Lacedæmonians</term>, but that notwithstanding this,

<summary>Pythagoras promoted justice by establishing communal property among his disciples and extending ethical kinship to animals. He believed that eliminating private interests and recognizing a shared life with all sentient beings were the foundations of social order and personal integrity.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, justice, communal property, animal rights, rational soul, household management, Alcæus, Lacedæmonians</keywords>

--- Page 105 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras's principles of justice and his management of domestic and public affairs.</meta>

He was admired as much for his household management as for his philosophy. When he was married, he educated the daughter born to him—who was later married to Meno the Crotonian—so well that as a maiden she was the leader of choirs, and as a wife, she held the first place among those who approached the altars. It is likewise said that the people of Metapontum, preserving the memory of Pythagoras after his death, turned his house into a temple of Ceres and the street in which he lived into a museum.

Because arrogance, luxury, and a contempt for the law frequently drive men toward injustice, he daily encouraged his disciples to support the law and to oppose lawlessness. Hence, he made a distinction such as the following: that what is called luxury is the first evil that usually slips into houses and cities; the second is arrogance; and the third is destruction. Therefore, luxury should be excluded and expelled <note>from every house and city</note> by all possible means, and men should be trained from birth to live a temperate and courageous life. He further added that it is necessary to be purified from all harmful speech, whether it is mournful, provokes hostility, or is of a reviling, arrogant, or vulgar nature.

Besides these, he also established another most beautiful form of justice: the legislative, which mandates what ought to be done and forbids what ought not to be done. This form, however, is superior to the judicial form of justice. For legislative justice resembles medicine which heals the sick; yet it differs from it in this: it does not allow disease to begin but pays attention to the health of the soul from the very start. This being the case, the best of all legislators came from the school of Pythagoras: first, Charondas the Catanæan; and next, Zaleucus and Timaratus, who wrote laws for the

<summary>This page describes Pythagoras’s influence on his family and the public, his warnings against the social progression of luxury and arrogance, and his preference for preventive legislative justice. It also identifies several prominent Greek legislators as former students of his school.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, Meno the Crotonian, Metapontines, Ceres, luxury, arrogance, legislative justice, Charondas the Catanæan, Zaleucus, Timaratus</keywords>

--- Page 106 ---
<meta>This page continues the list of Pythagorean legislators and discusses Pythagoras’s views on divine justice and the immortality of the soul.</meta>

Locrians. Besides these, there were also Theætetus and Helicaon, Aristocrates, and Phytius, who became the legislators of the Rhegini. All of these likewise obtained honors from their citizens similar to those of the Gods. For Pythagoras did not act like Heraclitus, who said that he would write laws for the Ephesians and also petulantly <note>I wonder that the learned *Obrechtus* should translate *hēbēdon* (ἡβηδὸν) as "with all his youth." Had his translation, which is on the whole very excellent, been reviewed by English or Scottish critics, they would have immediately said from this circumstance that he did not understand Greek.</note> said that in those laws he would order the citizens to hang themselves. But Pythagoras endeavored to establish laws with great benevolence and political science. 

Why, however, is it necessary to admire these men? For Zamolxis, being a Thracian and the slave of Pythagoras, after he had heard the discourses of Pythagoras, obtained his liberty, and returned to the Getæ, gave laws to them, as we have previously observed in the beginning of this work, and exhorted the citizens to fortitude, having persuaded them that the soul is immortal. Hence, even at present, all the *Galatæ*, the *Trallians*, and many others of the *Barbarians* persuade their children that the soul cannot be destroyed, but that it remains after death, and that death is not to be feared, but danger is to be encountered with a firm and manly mind. Therefore, having instructed the Getæ in these things and written laws for them, he was considered by them as the greatest of the Gods.

Furthermore, he understood that the dominion of the Gods was most effective for the establishment of justice, and from this divine source, he constituted a polity, laws, and justice. It will not, however, be irrelevant to the purpose to specifically add the manner in which he thought we ought to conceive of divinity; *namely*, that we should believe that he exists, and that he is so disposed toward the human race that he

<summary>This page lists more Pythagorean legislators and recounts how Pythagoras's slave, Zamolxis, brought laws and the doctrine of the soul's immortality to the Thracians. It also explains that Pythagoras based his system of justice and government on the divine authority of the Gods.</summary>

<keywords>Theætetus, Helicaon, Aristocrates, Phytius, Rhegini, Pythagoras, Heraclitus, Zamolxis, Thracians, Getæ, Galatæ, Trallians, immortality of the soul, divine justice, Obrechtus</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the necessity of divine governance and the Pythagorean view of human nature as requiring external discipline and law.</meta>

inspects and does not neglect it. The Pythagoreans believed this concept, which they derived from Pythagoras, was very useful. We require oversight of this kind, which we do not think fit to resist in any way. Such is the watchful government of divinity. For if a divine nature is of this kind, it deserves to have rule over the universe. For the Pythagoreans rightly said that man—<note>so far as pertains to his irrational part</note>—is a naturally insolent animal, and changeable according to impulses, desires, and the rest of the passions. He therefore requires a transcendent oversight and government of this kind, from which a certain discipline and order may be derived. Therefore, they thought that everyone, being conscious of the instability of their nature, should never forget piety towards and the worship of divinity; instead, they should always place the Divine before the mind's eye, as one who inspects and diligently observes the conduct of mankind. After divinity and the daemonic nature, they thought that everyone should pay the greatest attention to their parents and the laws, and should be obedient to them, not falsely, but faithfully. And universally, they thought it necessary to believe that nothing is a greater evil than anarchy, since the human race is not naturally suited to be saved when no one rules over it.

These men also thought it right to adhere to the customs and legal institutions of their ancestors, even though they might be somewhat inferior to other customs and laws. To flee from existing laws and to be eager for innovation is by no means profitable or healthy. Pythagoras, therefore, gave many other examples of piety to the Gods, proving that his life conformed to his doctrines. It will not be irrelevant to the purpose to mention one of them, which may serve to clarify the rest. I will relate what Pythagoras said and did relative to

<summary>This page explains the Pythagorean belief that divine oversight is necessary to restrain the naturally insolent and fickle nature of humanity. It emphasizes obedience to the Divine, parents, and established laws as the only safeguard against the great evil of anarchy.</summary>

<keywords>Pythagoras, Pythagoreans, divinity, human nature, passions, anarchy, law and order, piety, daemonic nature, ancestral customs</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras's moral and philosophical teachings, following his interaction with the ambassadors of Sybaris and his demonstrations of piety.</meta>

...again speak with the same voice as men, which legends report they did at the beginning of all things.

Pythagoras likewise discovered another method of restraining men from injustice through the judgment of souls, truly knowing that this method can be taught, and also knowing that it is useful for suppressing injustice through fear. He asserted, therefore, that it is much better to be injured than to kill a man, for that judgment is placed in Hades, where the soul, its essence, and the primary nature of beings are properly evaluated. Desiring, however, to demonstrate a definite, equal, and proportionate justice in things that are unequal, asymmetrical, and infinite—and to show how it ought to be applied—he said that justice resembles that specific figure among geometrical diagrams which, despite having infinite combinations of figures arranged differently in relation to one another, still possesses equal demonstrations of power.$^1$

Since there is also a certain justice in how one treats another person, the following method of it is said to have been passed down by the Pythagoreans: Of associations with others, one kind is timely, while another is untimely. These are likewise distinguished from each other by differences in age, merit, the closeness of the relationship, acts of kindness, and whatever else there may be of a similar kind in the various ways men associate with each other. For there is a type of association—namely, of a younger person with another younger person—which does not appear

---

<note>$^1$ Iamblichus here alludes to a right-angled triangle and the Pythagorean theorem (Proposition 47 of Euclid’s first book). Since the square described on the longest side is equal to the two squares described on the other two sides, the longest side is said by geometers to be equal in power to the squares of the other sides. Kiessling, however, not understanding this, says “that power is the space contained between the meeting lines of figures, and is the area of the triangle.” “Δύναμις (Power) is the same as ἐμβαδον (area), the space which is contained within the meeting lines of figures, the area of the triangle.” But Kiessling, though a good philologist, is a poor geometer and no philosopher.</note>

<summary>Pythagoras argues that the fear of a post-mortem judgment in Hades acts as a deterrent against injustice and uses the geometric principles of a right-angled triangle to illustrate the nature of proportionate justice. He also begins to categorize human associations as either timely or untimely based on social factors like age and merit.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Pythagoras’s teachings on the justice of social interactions, specifically focusing on the concept of opportune timing and the hierarchy of principles within different systems.</meta>

to be unseasonable; but an association between a younger person and an elderly person is unseasonable. For no kind of anger, threat, or boldness is appropriate in a younger person toward an elderly man; instead, all untimely conduct of this sort should be cautiously avoided. Similar reasoning should also be applied with respect to merit. For it is neither proper nor timely to use unrestrained freedom of speech, or to adopt any of the previously mentioned behaviors, toward a man who has attained the true dignity of perfect virtue. In accordance with this, he also spoke regarding associations with parents and with benefactors. 

He added that there is a varied and multifaceted use of opportune timing. For among those who are enraged and angry, some are so at the right time, while others are so at the wrong time. And again, regarding those who aspire to, desire, or are driven toward anything desirable, opportune timing accompanies some, while unseasonable timing accompanies others. The same may be said concerning other passions, actions, temperaments, associations, and meetings. 

He further observed that opportune timing can, *to a certain extent*, be taught, and also that what happens unexpectedly can be discussed systematically; but when considered universally and simply, none of the aforementioned details apply to it. However, such things generally accompany it as follow the nature of opportune timing—namely, what is called the flourishing, the becoming, the appropriate, and whatever else may be of a similar nature. He likewise asserted that the first principle [or the beginning] in the universe is unity, and it is the most honorable of things; and that it is the same in science, in experience, and in creation. And again, that the number two is most honorable in a household, in a city, in a camp, and in all such similar systems. However, the nature of a principle is difficult to observe and understand in all the aforementioned

<summary>Pythagoras explains that social conduct must be governed by opportune timing, particularly regarding respect for elders and the virtuous. He also defines unity and the number two as the primary honorable principles within the universe and human organizations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of principles in science and society, transitioning into Pythagoras’s personal example of devotion to his teacher and his instructions regarding the integrity of agreements.</meta>

particulars. For in the sciences, it is not within the ability of any casual understanding to learn and judge, by carefully surveying the parts of things, what the nature of their principles is. He added that it makes a great difference—and that there is danger regarding the knowledge of the whole of things—when a principle is not correctly established. For, in short, none of the subsequent conclusions can be sound when the true principle is unknown. The same thing may also be said regarding a principle of another kind. For neither a house nor a city can be well established unless each has a true ruler who governs those who voluntarily submit to him. For it is necessary in both cases that the governor be willing to rule and the governed be willing to obey. Just as with respect to academic disciplines, when they are taught effectively, it is necessary that there be a concurrence in the will of both the teacher and the learner. For if there is resistance from either side, the intended work will never be accomplished properly. In this way, therefore, he proved that it was noble to be guided by rulers and to be obedient to teachers. But he presented the following as the greatest argument through his actions for the truth of his observations. He traveled from Italy to Delos to see Pherecydes the Syrian, who had been his teacher, so that he might offer him assistance, as he was then afflicted with what is called the <term>morbus pedicularis</term> <note>a disease characterized by an infestation of lice</note>. He carefully attended to him until the time of his death and piously performed whatever rites were due to his deceased teacher. So diligent was he in performing his duties toward the person from whom he had received instruction.

Moreover, regarding agreements and the truthfulness related to them, Pythagoras so prepared his disciples to observe them that, as the story goes, Lysis—having once performed his prayers in the temple of Juno—met Euryphamus the Syracusan near the vestibules as he was departing,

<meta>This page continues the narrative illustrating Pythagoras’s teachings on fidelity to agreements and transitions into his dietary laws and symbolic exhortations to justice.</meta>

who was one of his fellow disciples and was then entering the temple. Because Euryphamus asked Lysis to wait for him until he also had worshiped the goddess, Lysis sat down on a stone seat that was placed there. However, having finished his worship and becoming absorbed in certain profound thoughts, Euryphamus forgot his appointment and left the temple through another gate. But Lysis waited for him without leaving his seat for the rest of that day, the following night, and also the greater part of the next day. And perhaps he would have stayed there even longer, if Euryphamus had not heard in the lecture hall the following day that Lysis was being sought by his associates. Remembering his agreement, he went to Lysis and freed him from his commitment, explaining the reason for his forgetfulness. He added, “Some god caused this forgetfulness in me as a trial of your firmness in keeping your agreements.” 

Pythagoras also ordained abstinence from animal food for many other reasons, and also because it promotes peace. For those who are accustomed to detest the slaughter of animals as unjust and unnatural will think it much more unlawful to kill a man or engage in war. But war is the leader and legislator of slaughter. For through war, slaughter is increased and becomes strong and powerful. The instruction “not to step above the beam of the balance” is an exhortation to justice, declaring that whatever is just should be cultivated, as will be shown when we discuss the Pythagorean symbols. It appears, therefore, through all these details, that Pythagoras paid great attention to the practice of justice and to delivering it to mankind in both deeds and words.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Pythagorean ethics, transitioning from justice to the virtue of temperance and the specific practices used to cultivate it.</meta>

### CHAP. XXXI.

Next, it follows that we should speak of temperance, and show how Pythagoras cultivated it and how he taught it to his associates. We have, therefore, already related the common precepts regarding it, in which it is said that everything excessive should be cut away with fire and sword. Abstinence from animal food is also a precept of the same kind; as is the avoidance of certain foods calculated to produce intemperance and which block the alertness and natural energy of the power of reason. Furthermore, this category includes the rule that lavish food should indeed be brought into banquets, but should <note>shortly after</note> be sent away and given to the servants, being placed on the table merely to discipline one's desires. Similarly, no freeborn and noble woman should wear gold, but only harlots. There is also the practice of reticence and perfect silence for the purpose of controlling the tongue, as well as a diligent and persistent effort to study and investigate the most difficult theorems. Because of all these, we must attribute to this same virtue <note>that is, to temperance</note> the following: abstinence from wine; a scarcity of food and sleep; a genuine contempt for fame, wealth, and similar things; a sincere respect for those to whom respect is due, and an unfeigned consistency in behavior and kindness toward peers; the correction and encouragement of those who are younger, without envy; and everything else of a similar nature.

The temperance of these men, and how Pythagoras taught this virtue, may also be learned from what Hippobotus and Neanthes relate concerning Myllias and Timycha, who were Pythagoreans. For they say that the tyrant Dionysius could not obtain the friendship

<summary>This chapter outlines the Pythagorean discipline of temperance, which included dietary restrictions, the rejection of luxury, and the practice of silence. It introduces a historical account of the Pythagoreans Myllias and Timycha and their dealings with the tyrant Dionysius.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, temperance, abstinence, silence, Myllias, Timycha, Dionysius, Pythagorean ethics, self-discipline</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of the tyrant Dionysius’s failed attempts to win over the Pythagoreans, leading him to resort to force and treachery.</meta>

of any of the Pythagoreans, though he did everything to accomplish his purpose; for they had observed and carefully avoided his monarchical disposition. He therefore sent a troop of thirty soldiers to the Pythagoreans under the command of Eurymenes the Syracusan, who was the brother of Dion, so that their usual migration from Tarentum to Metapontum might be conveniently intercepted for his purposes through treachery. For it was their custom to change their residence at different seasons of the year, and they chose places that were suited for this migration. Therefore, in Phalae, a rugged part of Tarentum through which the Pythagoreans necessarily had to pass on their journey, Eurymenes deceitfully hid his troop. When the Pythagoreans reached that place around midday, expecting no such thing, the soldiers rushed upon them with shouts, in the manner of robbers. 

But the Pythagoreans, being disturbed and terrified by such an unexpected attack and by the superior number of their enemies (for there were only ten Pythagoreans in total), and considering also that they would be captured since they were unarmed and had to contend with men who were variously armed—they found that their only safety lay in flight, and they did not believe that this was contrary to virtue. For they knew that fortitude, according to the judgment of right reason, is the science of those things which are to be avoided and endured. And they achieved this now. For Eurymenes' men, being heavily armed, would have abandoned the pursuit of the Pythagoreans if the latter, in their flight, had not arrived at a certain field sown with beans, which were in a sufficiently flourishing condition. Being unwilling, therefore, to violate the <term>dogma</term> <note>a settled principle or tenet</note> which commanded them not to touch beans, they stood still; out of necessity, they attacked their pursuers with stones, sticks, and whatever else they happened to find, until they had

<meta>This page continues the account of the Pythagoreans' encounter with Eurymenes' soldiers and their refusal to compromise their principles, specifically their prohibition against beans.</meta>

killed some and wounded many of them. All the Pythagoreans, however, were at length killed by the spearmen, nor would any one of them allow himself to be taken captive, but preferred death to this, in accordance with the mandates of their sect.

Eurymenes, therefore, and his soldiers, were immensely disturbed on finding that they would not be able to bring even one of the Pythagoreans alive to Dionysius, though they were sent by him for this purpose alone. Hence, having piled earth on the slain and buried them in that place in a common grave, they turned their steps homeward. As they were returning, however, they happened to meet with Myllias the Crotonian and his wife Timycha the Lacedemonian, whom the other Pythagoreans had left behind because Timycha, being pregnant, was now in her sixth <note>In the original Greek, this is δέκατον, *the tenth month*; but as it very seldom happens that a woman is in a state of pregnancy for more than nine months, it appears to me that for δέκατον we should read ἕκτον, *the sixth month*, as in the above translation.</note> month, and on this account walked slowly. These, therefore, the soldiers gladly took captive and led them to the tyrant, paying every attention to them so that they might be brought to him safely. But the tyrant, having learned what had happened, was greatly dejected and said to the two Pythagoreans, "You shall obtain from me honors surpassing all others in dignity if you will consent to rule in conjunction with me." All his offers, however, being rejected by Myllias and Timycha, he said, "If then you will only teach me one thing, I will dismiss you with a sufficiently safe guard." Myllias therefore asking him what it was he wished to learn, Dionysius replied, "It is this: why did your companions choose rather to die than to tread on beans?" But Myllias immediately answered, "My companions indeed submitted to death so that they might not tread upon beans, but I would rather tread on them than tell you the cause of this." Dionysius therefore, being

<meta>This page continues the account of Dionysius the tyrant's interrogation of the Pythagoreans Myllias and Timycha. Myllias has just refused to explain the Pythagorean prohibition against beans.</meta>

[Dionysius], astonished at this answer, ordered him to be forcibly taken away, but commanded Timycha to be tortured. For he thought that as she was a woman, pregnant, and deprived of her husband, she would easily tell him what he wanted to know through fear of the torments. The heroic woman, however, grinding her tongue with her teeth, bit it off and spat it at the tyrant; demonstrating by this that although her sex, being overcome by the torments, might be compelled to disclose something which ought to be concealed in silence, the member serving to express it should be entirely cut off. They made so much difficulty in admitting foreign friendships, even though they should happen to be royal.

Similar to these also were the precepts concerning silence, which tended toward the exercise of temperance. For the control of the tongue is the most difficult of all forms of self-restraint. Persuading the Crotonians to abstain from profane and illegitimate associations with prostitutes likewise pertains to the same virtue. And besides this, there was the correction through music, by means of which Pythagoras restored a young man to temperance who had become furious through love. The exhortation also, which leads away from lewd insolence, must be referred to the same virtue. And Pythagoras delivered these things to the Pythagoreans, he himself being the cause of them. For they so attended to their bodies that they might always remain in the same condition, and not at one time be thin, but at another abounding in flesh. For they considered this to be an indication of an inconsistent life. In a similar manner also with respect to the mind, they were careful that they might not be cheerful at one time and sad at another, but that they might be uniformly and mildly joyful. They expelled rage, despondency, and turmoil. *And it was a rule with them that no human accidents ought to be unexpected by those who possess intellect, but*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean discipline, specifically focusing on how they managed their emotions and maintained communal harmony.</meta>

that they should expect anything to happen which it is not in their power to prevent. But if at any time they were in a rage, or oppressed with sorrow, or anything else of this kind, they separated themselves from the rest of their associates, and each, by himself alone, endeavored to digest and heal the passion.

This also is said of the Pythagoreans, that no one of them, when angry, either punished a servant or admonished any free man, but each of them waited until his mind was restored to its former [tranquil] condition. But they called this act of admonishing *<term>pædartan</term>*. For they accomplished this waiting by employing silence and quiet. Hence Spintharus relates of Archytas the Tarentine, that returning after a certain time from the war which the city of Tarentum waged against the Messenians to inspect some land which belonged to him, and finding that the bailiff and the other servants had not paid proper attention to the cultivation of it, but had greatly neglected it, being enraged, he was so indignant that he told his servants it was well for them he was angry; since, if this had not happened, they would not have escaped the punishment due to so great an offense. Spintharus likewise says that a similar thing is related of Clinias. For he also deferred all admonitions and punishments until his mind was restored to tranquillity.

It is further related of the Pythagoreans that they expelled from themselves lamentation, weeping, and everything else of this kind; and that neither gain, nor desire, nor anger, nor ambition, nor anything of a similar nature became the cause of dissension among them; but that all the Pythagoreans were so disposed toward each other as a worthy father is toward his offspring. This also is a beautiful circumstance, that they referred everything to Pythagoras and called it by his name, and that they did not ascribe the glory of their own inventions to themselves, except very rarely. For there are very few whose works are acknowledged

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean discipline and the preservation of their philosophical traditions, transitioning into their views on the value of opinion and the stages of human life.</meta>

to be their own. The accuracy, as well, with which they preserved their writings is admirable. For in so many ages, no one seems to have encountered any of the commentaries of the Pythagoreans before the time of Philolaus. He was the first to publish those three celebrated books, which Dion of Syracuse is said to have bought, at the request of Plato, for a hundred minae. For Philolaus had fallen into a state of great and severe poverty; and because of his alliance with the Pythagoreans, he was a sharer of their writings.

Regarding opinion,<note>Obrechtus, by translating <term>περὶ δέ δόξης</term> in this place as "Concerning fame and glory," has evidently mistaken the meaning of Iamblichus.</note> it is related that they spoke of it as follows: That it is the mark of a foolish man to pay attention to the opinion of everyone, and especially to that of the multitude. For it belongs to only a few to understand and form opinions rightly, since it is evident that this pertains to the intelligent alone. And they are few. It is clear, therefore, that a power of this kind does not extend to the masses. However, it is also foolish to despise the opinion of everyone. For it will happen that he who is so disposed will be unlearned and beyond correction. It is necessary, nonetheless, that he who lacks science should learn those things of which he is ignorant and has no scientific knowledge. It is likewise necessary that the learner should pay attention to the opinion of him who possesses science and is able to teach. Generally, it is necessary that those young men who wish to be saved should attend to the opinion of their elders and of those who have lived well. But in the whole of human life, there are certain ages (called by them, it is said, *endedasmenæ*) which it is not in the power of any ordinary person to connect with each other. For they are driven out by each other, unless someone guides a man from his birth in a beautiful and upright manner. It is necessary, therefore, when a child is educated well and is formed to temperance

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the various stages of human life according to the Pythagoreans, focusing on the specific education and discipline required during the transition from childhood to adolescence.</meta>

and fortitude, so that a great part of his education should be dedicated to the age of adolescence <note>which is the age of a boy</note>. Similarly, when a boy is trained in temperance and fortitude, it is necessary that a significant portion of his education should be carried over into the age of manhood. For what happens among the general public is absurd and ridiculous. They imagine it is necessary for boys to be orderly and temperate, and to abstain from everything that appears to be troublesome and improper; but they think that when they have reached adolescence, they may for the most part do whatever they please. Consequently, there is nearly a convergence of both kinds of errors in this age. For youths commit many faults that are both childish and adult. In short, avoiding every kind of diligence and order, and pursuing every type of sport, along with childish lack of self-control and insolence, are traits most suited to the age of a boy. Therefore, such a disposition is carried from childhood into the following age. However, the nature of strong desires and various types of ambition—and, in a similar way, the remaining impulses and tendencies, when they are of a harsh and turbulent nature—are brought from the adult age into that of adolescence. Thus, of all the ages, this one demands the greatest attention. And generally, no man should be allowed to do whatever he pleases; rather, it is always necessary for there to be a certain supervision, and a legal and well-structured government, to which each of the citizens is obedient. For the animal, when left to itself and neglected, rapidly degenerates into vice and depravity.

It is also said that the Pythagoreans frequently questioned and wondered why we train boys to take their food in an orderly and proportional manner, and show them that order and symmetry are beautiful, while their opposites—disorder and lack of proportion—are shameful; and why one who is prone to wine and is insatiable is held in great disgrace. For if none of these things is useful to us when we have arrived
