<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean discipline, arguing that childhood habits must persist into adulthood and that pleasure should not be the primary motivator for action.</meta>

at the age of manhood, it would be in vain that we were accustomed, when boys, to an order of this kind. And there is also the same reason concerning the other habits <note>to which we are accustomed when boys</note>. This, therefore, does not seem to happen in other animals which are trained by men; but immediately from the start, a puppy and a colt are accustomed to, and learn those things which it is necessary for them to do when they have arrived at the perfection of their nature. And universally, it is said that the <term>Pythagoreans</term> exhorted both those they happened to meet and their close associates to avoid pleasure as a thing that required the greatest caution. For nothing so much deceives us, or plunges us into error, as this passion. In short, it seems, they contended that we should never do anything with a view to pleasure as the end goal. For this aim is, for the most part, improper and harmful. *But they asserted that, especially looking to the noble and fitting, we should do whatever is to be done.*<note>The wise and noble <term>Pythagoreans</term>, <term>Platonists</term>, <term>Peripatetics</term>, and <term>Stoics</term> among the ancients looked to virtue as its own reward, and performed what is right because it is right to do so. And though they firmly believed in the immortality of the soul, their conduct was not at all influenced by the hope of future reward. This great truth indeed—that virtue brings with it its own recompense—is almost obsolete at present; and it is no unusual thing to hear a man, when afflicted, exclaiming with Methodistical cant, 
“The many troubles that I meet, 
In getting to a Mercy-seat!”</note> And that in the second place we should look to what is advantageous and useful. These things, however, require no superficial judgment.

With respect to what is called desire, these men are said to have asserted as follows: *That desire itself is a certain tendency, impulse, and appetite of the soul, in order to be filled with something, or to enjoy something present, or to be disposed according to some sensory activity; but that there is also a desire of the opposites to these, and this is a desire of the emptying and*

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean ethics, specifically moving from the general concept of caution toward pleasure to a detailed analysis of the nature and variety of human desires.</meta>

absence, and of having no sensible perception of certain things. This passion is likewise varied, and is almost the most multifaceted of all those that pertain to man. However, many human desires are acquired and produced by people themselves. Therefore, this passion requires the greatest attention, and more than just casual care and physical exercise. For the fact that the body, when empty, should desire food is natural; and again, it is also natural that, when full, it should desire an appropriate evacuation. But to desire unnecessary nourishment, or superfluous and luxurious garments, bed-covers, or houses, is acquired. They also reasoned in the same way concerning furniture, vessels, servants, and livestock used for food. And generally, with respect to human passions, they are of such a nature as to never be permanent, but instead to proceed to infinity. Therefore, attention should be paid to the young from their earliest years, so that they may aspire to proper things, avoid vain and unnecessary desires, and thus be undisturbed by and purified from such appetites. They may then look down upon those who are objects of contempt because they are bound by all kinds of desires. But it must be especially noted that vain, harmful, unnecessary, and insolent desires exist among those who possess the greatest power. For there is nothing so absurd that the souls of such boys, men, and women do not incite them to do. In short, the variety of food that is consumed is extremely diverse. For there are an infinite number of fruits and a vast multitude of roots that the human race uses for food. It also uses all kinds of meat; and it is difficult to find any land, air, or water animal that humans do not taste. They also employ all sorts of methods in preparing these foods, along with many mixtures of juices. Therefore, it naturally follows that the human race is unstable and multifaceted, according to the movements of the soul. For each kind of food that is

It emphasizes that the young must be trained to avoid these vain desires to prevent the soul from becoming "insane and multiform" through excessive indulgence.</summary>

introduced into the body, it becomes the cause of a certain unique disposition.

We perceive, however, that some things immediately cause a great change in quality, as is evident with wine. For when it is drunk in abundance, it first makes men more cheerful, but afterwards more insane and improper. Yet people are ignorant of those things which do not show a power of this kind, even though everything eaten is the cause of a specific disposition. Therefore, it requires great wisdom to be able to know and perceive what kind and what quantity of food should be used. This science, however, was first revealed by Apollo and Pæon; and later by Esculapius and his followers.

Regarding reproduction also, the Pythagoreans are said to have made the following observations. First, they thought it necessary to guard against what is called untimely <note>offspring</note>. For neither untimely plants nor animals are good; instead, before they bear fruit, it is necessary that a certain time should pass so that seeds and fruit may be produced from strong and healthy bodies. It is necessary, therefore, that boys and young women should be accustomed to labor, exercise, and appropriate endurance, and that they be given food suited to a life of labor, moderation, and stamina. But there are many things of this kind in human life which it is better to learn at a later period, and among these is the use of venery <note>sexual activity or desire</note>. It is necessary, therefore, that a boy should be so educated as not to seek such a connection before his twentieth year. But when he reaches this age, he should engage in venery rarely. This will be the case, however, if he believes that a healthy physical condition is an honorable and beautiful thing. For lack of self-control and a healthy physical condition do not easily exist together in the same person. It is also said that the Pythagoreans praised those laws which existed previously

For it is beautiful and advantageous for there to be many obstacles to this activity <note>the "energy" or impulse of sexual intercourse</note>. These men also understood, it seems, that unnatural births, and those which result from unrestrained arrogance, should be entirely prevented; but that those births should be allowed to remain which are according to nature, exist with moderation, and occur in the pure and lawful procreation of children.

They also believed that great foresight and care should be given by those who father children toward their future offspring. Therefore, the first and greatest care that should be taken by one who applies himself to the procreation of children is that he lives moderately and healthily—that he neither stuffs himself with food at improper times, nor consumes such foods as might make his physical condition worse than it was, and above all else, that he avoids drunkenness. For they thought that defective seed was produced from a bad, discordant, and disordered temperament. And universally, they were of the opinion that only a lazy and thoughtless person would attempt to produce a living being and bring it into existence without providing with all possible diligence that its entry into being and life might be most graceful and pleasing. For those who love dogs pay every possible attention to the breeding of puppies, so that they may be produced from proper stock, at the proper time, and in a proper manner, so that they may become good offspring. The same attention is also paid by those who love birds. And it is evident that others who are careful about the breeding of noble animals endeavor by all possible means to ensure that their breeding is not in vain. It would be absurd, therefore, for men

For among the common people, the procreation of children is undertaken in a beastly and rash manner. And such were the assertions, and such was the doctrine of these men, which they verified both in words and deeds regarding temperance; these precepts having been originally received by them from Pythagoras himself, like certain oracles delivered by the Pythian Apollo.

## CHAP. XXXII.

Regarding fortitude, however, many of the details which have been already related appropriately belong to it; such as the admirable deeds of Timycha, and of those Pythagoreans who chose to die rather than violate the decisions of Pythagoras concerning beans, and other things consistent with such pursuits. Such also are the deeds which Pythagoras himself generously accomplished, when he traveled everywhere alone and underwent immense labors and dangers, choosing to leave his country and dwell among strangers. Likewise, when he dissolved tyrannies, brought order to confused governments, and liberated cities. When also he caused illegality to cease and blocked the operations of insolent and tyrannical men; showing himself to be a kind leader to the just and mild, but expelling savage and immoral men from his association and refusing even to give them an answer; gladly, indeed, giving assistance to the former, but with all his might resisting the latter. Many instances, therefore, of these things might be

<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagoras's courageous actions, specifically his "invincible freedom of speech" when dealing with the tyrant Phalaris and his interaction with the Hyperborean sage Abaris.</meta>

adduced, and of upright actions frequently performed by him. But the greatest of all these is what he said and did to Phalaris, with an invincible freedom of speech. For when he was held in captivity by Phalaris, the most cruel of tyrants, a wise man of the Hyperborean race named Abaris was his associate. Abaris came to him for the sake of conversing with him and asked him many questions, especially those of a sacred nature regarding statues and the most holy worship, the providence of the Gods, celestial and terrestrial natures, and many other similar things. 

But Pythagoras, being under the influence of divine inspiration, answered Abaris vehemently and with all truth and persuasion, so as to convince those who heard him. Then, however, Phalaris was inflamed with anger against Abaris because he praised Pythagoras, and he was ferociously disposed toward Pythagoras himself. He also dared to utter blasphemies against the Gods themselves, of the kind he was accustomed to pour forth. But Abaris thanked Pythagoras for what he said; and after this, learned from him that *all things are suspended from and governed by the heavens;* which Pythagoras proved to be the case *from many other things, and also from the energy of sacred rites.* 

And Abaris was so far from thinking that Pythagoras, who taught these things, was an enchanter, that he admired him beyond measure as if he had been a God. To these things, however, Phalaris replied by endeavoring to subvert divination and openly denying the efficacy of the things which are performed in sacred rites. But Abaris shifted the discourse from these details to those that are clearly apparent to all men; and he endeavored to persuade him that there is a divine providence, using those circumstances that transcend all human hope and power—whether they are immense wars, incurable diseases, the corruption of crops, the incursions of pestilence, or certain other things of that kind, which are most difficult

Phalaris, however, shamelessly and audaciously opposed what was being said. Therefore, Pythagoras, again suspecting that Phalaris intended to put him to death, but at the same time knowing that he was not destined to die by Phalaris's hand, began to address him with great freedom of speech. For looking toward Abaris, he said that a transition was naturally suited to take place from the heavens to aerial and terrestrial beings. And again, he showed that all things follow the heavens, using examples most well known to all men. He also undoubtedly demonstrated that the <note>deliberative</note> power of the soul possesses free will. And proceeding even further, he fully discussed the perfect activity of reason and intellect. Afterward, also with his <note>usual</note> freedom of speech, he spoke concerning tyranny and all the prerogatives of fortune, and concerning injustice and human greed, and firmly taught him that all these are of no value. In the next place, he gave him a divine admonition concerning the most excellent life, and earnestly entered into a comparison of it with the most corrupt life. He also most clearly explained to him how the soul and its powers and passions exist; *and, what is the most beautiful thing of all, demonstrated to him that the Gods are not the causes of evil, and that diseases and such things as are the calamities of the body are the seeds of intemperance;* criticizing at the same time mythologists and poets for what they have poorly said in fables <note>on this subject</note>. Refuting Phalaris also, he admonished him and demonstrated to him through his works what the power of heaven is and the magnitude of that power; and proved to him by many arguments that legal punishment is reasonably established. He likewise clearly showed him

<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagoras's discourse to Phalaris, transitioning from the power of heaven and man to the specific differences between humans and animals, and the cultivation of fortitude.</meta>

what the difference is between men and other animals; and he scientifically discussed internal and external speech. He also perfectly demonstrated the nature of intellect and of the knowledge which descends from it, together with many other ethical doctrines following from these things.

Furthermore, he instructed him in what is most beneficial among the things that are useful in life; and in the mildest manner, he adapted admonitions harmonizing with these, adding at the same time prohibitions against what ought not to be done. And what is the greatest of all, he revealed to him the distinction between the products of fate and those of intellect, and also the difference between what is done by destiny and what is done by fate. He likewise wisely discussed many things concerning daemons and the immortality of the soul. These things, however, pertain to another treatise. 

But those particulars that belong to the cultivation of fortitude are more appropriate to our present purpose. For if, when situated in the midst of dreadful circumstances, Pythagoras appears to have philosophized with firmness of decision—if on all sides he resisted and repelled fortune, and strenuously endured its attacks, and if he employed the greatest freedom of speech toward him who brought his life into danger—it is evident that he perfectly despised those things which are thought to be dreadful, and that he considered them as undeserving of notice. If also, when he expected according to appearances to be put to death, he entirely despised this and was not moved by the expectation of it, it is evident that he was perfectly free from the dread of death.<note>1. Iamblichus informed us a little earlier that Pythagoras suspected that Phalaris intended to put him to death, but at the same time knew that he was not destined to die by Phalaris's hand. This being the case, therefore, Pythagoras has no claim to fortitude in this instance for being free from the fear of death. But he has a great claim to it when it is considered that he was in the power of a tyrant who might have caused him to suffer tortures worse than death.</note>

<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagoras's moral and philosophical influence, specifically detailing how his teachings led to the downfall of the tyrant Phalaris and demonstrating his heroic fortitude.</meta>

He performed, however, what is even more generous than this, by bringing about the end of tyranny, restraining the tyrant as he was about to inflict the most miserable disasters upon humanity, and freeing Sicily from the most cruel and overbearing power. The fact that Pythagoras was the one who accomplished this is clear from the oracles of Apollo, which predicted that Phalaris’s rule would end when those he governed became better people and more unified with one another. They became so when Pythagoras was among them, through the teachings and guidance he gave them. 

A stronger proof of this truth, however, is found in the timing of these events. For on the very same day that Phalaris put Pythagoras and Abaris in danger of death, the tyrant himself was killed by a clever plot. What happened to Epimenides may also serve as evidence for these claims. For just as he, a disciple of Pythagoras, invoked the Furies and the avenging gods when certain people tried to destroy him—causing his attackers to destroy one another—so too did Pythagoras. Acting for the benefit of humanity with the strength and courage of Hercules, he punished and brought about the death of the man who had behaved arrogantly and lawlessly toward others. He did this through the very oracles of Apollo, to whose divine lineage both he and Epimenides naturally belonged from birth. So far, we have found it necessary to mention this remarkable and vigorous deed as an example of his courage.

We shall, however, provide another example of it: namely, the preservation of correct opinion. In maintaining this, he did what seemed just to him and what was dictated by right reason, never being swayed from his purpose by pleasure, or

<keywords>Pythagoras, Phalaris, Sicily, Apollo, Epimenides, Abaris, Furies, Hercules, tyranny, fortitude, oracles</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the description of the character and self-discipline of Pythagoras’s followers, building on the previous discussion of his courage and his influence over the tyrant Phalaris.</meta>

labor, or any other passion or danger. His disciples also chose to die rather than violate his mandates. And when they were exposed to many diverse fortunes, they always maintained the same character. Even when they were involved in countless calamities, they never turned away from his teachings. It was a constant instruction among them to always support the law and oppose illegality, and to be accustomed from birth to a life of temperance and fortitude in order to restrain and repel luxury. They also had certain melodies which they devised as remedies against the passions of the soul, and likewise against despondency and lamentation; Pythagoras invented these as the greatest aids for these ailments. Furthermore, they employed other melodies against rage and anger, through which they increased or decreased the intensity of these passions until they reduced them to moderation and made them consistent with fortitude. *However, that which provided them with the greatest support in noble endurance was the conviction that no human occurrences should be unexpected by those who possess intellect, but rather that they should expect all things over which they have no absolute power.*

Moreover, if they ever happened to be angry or sorrowful, they immediately separated themselves from their associates; each one alone strenuously endeavored to process and heal the passion [by which he was oppressed]. They also generally believed that effort should be applied to disciplines and studies, and that they should be severely exercised in trials of the most diverse nature—including punishments and restraints by fire and sword—in order to be liberated from innate intemperance and an inexhaustible greed for possessions. For this purpose, they believed no labor or endurance should be spared. To accomplish this, they also nobly practiced abstinence from all animals and, in addition to this, from

<meta>This page continues the description of the character and self-discipline of Pythagoras’s followers, focusing on their methods for achieving self-mastery through silence, study, and the preservation of secret doctrines.</meta>

certain other kinds of food. From this also arose their restraint of speech and their perfect silence as a preparation for subduing the tongue, in which they exercised their fortitude for many years. To this may also be added their strenuous and diligent investigation and review of the most difficult theorems; and because of these things, their abstinence from wine, their scarcity of food and sleep, and their contempt for glory, wealth, and the like. In conjunction with all these particulars, they devoted themselves to fortitude.

It is likewise said that these men banished lamentations and tears, and everything else of this kind. They also abstained from entreaty, from supplication, and from all such undignified flattery, regarding it as effeminate and servile. <note>That is, *Humble* (ταπεινῆς οὔσης). For the Pythagoreans, therefore, humility was no virtue, though in modern times it is considered to be the greatest of the virtues. For Aristotle likewise, it is no virtue: for in his *Nicomachean Ethics* he says, "that all humble men are flatterers, and all flatterers are humble."</note> To the same mindset the uniqueness of their manners must also be referred, and the fact that all of them perpetually preserved among their <term>arcana</term> the most important doctrines in which their discipline was chiefly contained, keeping them with the greatest silence from being revealed to strangers, committing them unwritten to memory, and transmitting them orally to their successors, as if they were the mysteries of the Gods.

Hence it happened that nothing of their philosophy worth mentioning was made public, and that though it had been taught and learned for a long time, it was known only within their walls. But to those outside their walls—and, as I may say, to the uninitiated—if they happened to be present, these men spoke obscurely to each other through symbols, of which the celebrated precepts that are now in circulation retain a trace; such as, "Do not stir fire with a sword," and other symbols of the like kind,

<summary>This page details the Pythagorean discipline of silence, dietary restraint, and the rejection of emotional outbursts or humble flattery. It explains how their core philosophy was kept secret from the public and communicated only through cryptic symbols.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoreans, fortitude, silence, dogmas, arcana, Aristotle, Nicomachean Ethics, symbols, humility, precepts</keywords>

--- Page 131 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hidden meaning of Pythagorean teachings and their specific methods for developing fortitude and mental clarity.</meta>

which, taken literally, resemble old wives' tales; but when unfolded, they impart a certain admirable and venerable benefit to those who receive them.

The precept, however, which is more effective than all others for the attainment of fortitude, is that which has for its primary goal the defense and liberation from those bonds which hold the intellect captive from infancy. Without this, no one can learn or perceive anything healthy or true, through whichever sense he may operate. For according to the Pythagoreans,

> It is the mind that sees and hears all things;
> Everything else is deaf and blind.

But the precept which is next to this in effectiveness is that which exhorts one to be beyond measure diligent in purifying the intellect, and by various methods adapting it through <term>mathematical orgies</term> to receive something divinely beneficial. This is done so as neither to fear a separation from the body, nor, when led to incorporeal natures, to be forced to turn away one's eyes because of their brilliant splendor,<note>See the Cave of Plato, in the 7th book of his *Republic*.</note> nor to be turned back to those passions which nail and fasten the soul to the body. And, in short, it urges the soul to be untamed by all those passions which are the offspring of the realms of generation, and which pull it to an inferior condition of being. For the exercise and ascent through all these is the study of the most perfect fortitude. And such are the instances we have cited of the fortitude of Pythagoras and the Pythagoreans.

## CHAPTER XXXIII.

With respect to the friendship, however, which exists in all things toward all, Pythagoras taught it in the

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Chapter XXXIII regarding the Pythagorean concept of universal friendship and its practical application across various relationships, from the divine to the domestic.</meta>

clearest manner. And he revealed the friendship of the Gods toward men through piety and scientific cultivation; but he explained the friendship of doctrines toward each other, and generally of the soul toward the body, and of the rational toward the types of the irrational part, through philosophy and the theory pertaining to it. With respect to the friendship of men also toward each other: he established that of citizens through sound legislation, but that of strangers through a correct <term>physiology</term>; and that between husband and wife, or children, or brothers, and kindred, through uncorrupted communion. In short, he revealed the friendship of all things toward all, and even further, of certain irrational animals, through justice and a natural connection and association. But the pacification and conciliation of the body, which is naturally mortal, and of its hidden contrary powers, he explained through health and a diet and temperance suited to this, in imitation of the healthy condition of the world's elements. In all these, however, Pythagoras is acknowledged to have been the inventor and legislator of the summary grouping of them under one and the same name, which is that of friendship. And indeed, he transmitted such an admirable friendship to his associates that even now, those who are extremely benevolent toward each other are said to belong to the Pythagoreans. It is necessary, therefore, to relate the discipline of Pythagoras regarding these things, and the precepts which he used toward his disciples.

These men, then, exhorted others to remove competition and contention from true friendship—and if possible, indeed, from all friendship—but if not, at least from that which is parental, and generally from that which pertains to elders and benefactors. For to strive or contend with such as these, because of intervening anger or some other similar passion, does not preserve the existing friendship. But they said it is necessary that there should be the fewest possible scars

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter XXXIII regarding the Pythagorean principles of friendship, specifically focusing on how to resolve conflicts and the ethical duties between friends of different ages.</meta>

and ulcers in friendships; and that this will be the case if those who are friends know how to soften and subdue anger. If both indeed know this, or rather the younger of the two, and whoever ranks in one of the previously mentioned orders, <note>their friendship will be more easily preserved</note>. They were also of the opinion that corrections and admonitions, which they called *pædartases*, should take place from the elder to the younger with much gentleness and caution; and likewise, that much diligent and appropriate attention should be shown in admonitions. For in this way, they will be proper and beneficial. They also said that trust should never be separated from friendship, neither seriously nor even in jest. For it is no longer easy for an existing friendship to be in a healthy condition once falsehood creeps into the behavior of those who acknowledge themselves to be friends. Again, according to them, friendship should not be abandoned on account of misfortune or any other weakness to which human life is subject; but they said that the only acceptable rejection of a friend and friendship is that which arises from great and incorrigible vice. Likewise, they held that hatred should not be voluntarily felt toward those who are not perfectly bad; but if it is once formed, it should be nobly and vigorously maintained, unless the object of it changes his behavior so as to become a better man. That hostility also should not consist in words, but in deeds. And that this war is legitimate and holy when it is conducted in such a way as is fitting for one man to contend with another.

They likewise said that we should never, to the best of our ability, become the cause of dissension; but that we should avoid its source as much as possible. That in a friendship intended to be pure, the greater part of the things pertaining to it ought to be definite and lawful. And that these should be properly distinguished, and

Also, that no passion, such as desire or anger, be rashly excited in a bad and erroneous manner. And the same thing must be said of the remaining passions and dispositions.

Moreover, that they did not decline foreign friendships carelessly, but that they avoided and guarded against them with the greatest diligence; and also, that they rigidly preserved friendship toward each other for many ages, may be inferred from what Aristoxenus, in his treatise On the Pythagoric life, says he heard from Dionysius, the tyrant of Sicily, when, after losing his monarchy, he taught grammar at Corinth.

For Aristoxenus says as follows: “These men, as much as possible, prohibited lamentations and tears, and everything of this kind; and in a similar manner flattery, entreaty, supplication, and the like. Dionysius, therefore, after falling from his tyranny and coming to Corinth, narrated to us the details concerning Phintias and Damon the Pythagoreans; and these were regarding one being a sponsor for the death of the other. But the manner of the suretyship was as follows: He said that certain persons who were familiar with him had frequently mentioned the Pythagoreans, defaming and reviling them, calling them arrogant, and asserting that their gravity, their pretended fidelity, and apathy would be laid aside if anyone should cause them to fall into <note>some great</note> calamity. Certain people, however, contradicted this, and as a dispute arose on the subject, recourse was had to artifice, and one of the accusers of Phintias said before him that he had evidently conspired with certain others against the life of Dionysius. This also was testified by some who were present, and the charges against Phintias appeared to be very probable. Phintias, therefore, was astonished at the accusation. But when Dionysius had unequi-

<meta>This page continues the account by Aristoxenus of the test of Pythagorean friendship involving Phintias and Damon under the tyrant Dionysius.</meta>

stated out loud that he had accurately investigated all these details, and that it was necessary for him to die. Phintias replied that if it seemed necessary to him that this should occur, he requested that he would grant him the rest of the day, so that he might settle his own affairs, as well as those of Damon. For those men lived together and shared all things in common. However, since Phintias was the elder, the management of domestic matters was mostly handled by him. He requested, therefore, that Dionysius would allow him to leave for this purpose, and he would appoint Damon as his security. Dionysius then said that he wondered at the request, and asked him whether there was any man who was willing to become security for the death of another. But Phintias asserted that there was, and Damon was sent for. Upon hearing what had happened, Damon said that he would become the sponsor and would remain there until Phintias returned. Dionysius said that he was immediately astonished by these circumstances; however, those who had first proposed the experiment mocked Damon as someone who would be caught, saying sneeringly that he would be the *vicarious stag*. Therefore, when it was near sunset, Phintias arrived to die, at which all who were present were astonished and humbled. But Dionysius said that, after embracing and kissing the men, he requested that they would accept him as a third person into their friendship. They, however, would by no means consent to such a thing, even though he entreated them to comply with his request.” And these things are related by Aristoxenus, who heard them from Dionysius himself.

It is also said that the Pythagoreans endeavored to perform the duties of friendship for those of their sect, even though they were unknown to and had never seen each other, once they had received a certain sign of sharing the same doctrines; so

<summary>Dionysius recounts how Damon acted as a death-surety for Phintias, whose eventual return so impressed the tyrant that he begged to join their friendship. The text also notes the Pythagoreans' commitment to helping fellow members of their sect, even if they were strangers.</summary>
<keywords>Damon, Phintias, Dionysius, Aristoxenus, Pythagoreans, friendship, loyalty, vicarious stag, hospitality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of the deep bond between Pythagoreans, illustrating how their commitment to friendship extends even to members they have never personally met.</meta>

that through such friendly acts, the claim may be believed that worthy men, even if they live in the most remote parts of the earth, are friends to one another, even before they meet and greet each other. It is said, therefore, that a certain Pythagorean, traveling on foot along a long and lonely road, came to an inn. There, due to exhaustion and various other causes, he fell into a long and severe illness, eventually lacking the necessities of life. The innkeeper, however—whether out of pity for the man or from general kindness—supplied him with everything that was required, sparing no effort or expense for this purpose. But as the Pythagorean was dying from the disease, he wrote a certain symbol on a tablet before he passed away and asked the innkeeper, if he should happen to die, to hang the tablet near the road and observe whether any traveler read the symbol. "For that person," he said, "will repay you for what you have spent on me and will also thank you for your kindness." Therefore, after the death of the Pythagorean, the innkeeper buried him and paid the necessary respects to his body; however, he had no hope of being repaid or of receiving any reward from someone who might read the tablet. At the same time, though, being surprised by the Pythagorean's request, he was moved to display the writing on the public road. A long time later, another Pythagorean passing that way understood the symbol and learned who had placed the tablet there. After investigating every detail, he paid the innkeeper a much larger sum of money than he had spent.

It is also told of Clinias the Tarentine that when he learned that Prorus of Cyrene—who was devotedly committed to the Pythagorean doctrines—was in danger of losing all his property, he sailed to Cyrene after collecting a sum of money, and

<summary>This page illustrates the legendary loyalty of the Pythagoreans through the story of a dying traveler whose debts were paid by a stranger who recognized his symbol, and the beginning of an account of Clinias the Tarentine's journey to help a fellow member in financial ruin.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the account of Pythagorean friendship, concluding the story of Clinias’s generosity and summarizing the spiritual principles of the community before moving to miscellaneous anecdotes.</meta>

restored the affairs of Prorus to a better condition, not only incurring a loss of his own property in doing so, but also disregarding the danger to which he was exposed during the voyage. In the same way, Thestor Posidoniates, having learned from report alone that Thymaridas Parius the Pythagorean had fallen into poverty from a state of great wealth, is said to have sailed to Parus after collecting a large sum of money, and thus restored Thymaridas to his property. These, therefore, are beautiful instances of friendship. *The decisions, however, of the Pythagoreans respecting the communion of divine goods, the harmony of intellect, and things pertaining to a divine soul, are much more admirable than the examples above. For they perpetually exhorted each other not to divulge the God within them. Hence, all the endeavor of their friendship, both in deeds and words, was directed toward a certain divine mixture, a union with divinity, and a communion with intellect and a divine soul.* But it is not possible to find anything better than this, whether expressed in words or performed in deeds. For I am of the opinion that all the benefits of friendship are contained within this. Therefore, since we have gathered here, as if at a summit, all the privileges of the Pythagorean friendship, we shall refrain from saying anything further about it.

Since, however, we have thus generally and in an organized manner discussed what pertains to Pythagoras and the Pythagoreans, let us next narrate such scattered particulars relative to this subject as do not fall under the previously mentioned order. <meta>This page continues Chapter XXXIV, transitioning from the principles of Pythagorean friendship to miscellaneous observations regarding the sect's use of language and their preference for the Doric dialect.</meta>

And Metrodorus, the son of Thyrsus—who was the father of Epicharmus<note>The original text is Μητρόδωρός τε ὁ Θύρσου τοῦ πατρὸς Ἐπιχάρμου, which Obrechtus erroneously translates as “Metrodorus, son of Epicharmus and grandson of Thyrsus.”</note> and who applied the greater part of his doctrine to medicine—says, in explaining the writings of his father to his brother, that Epicharmus, and before him Pythagoras, believed that the best dialect, as well as the best musical harmony, is the Doric; that the Ionic and the Aeolic share in the chromatic harmony, but that the Attic dialect is filled with this to an even greater degree. They were also of the opinion that the Doric dialect, which consists of vocal letters, is enharmonic.

Myths also bear witness to the antiquity of this dialect. For in these it is said that Nereus married Doris, the daughter of Ocean, by whom he had fifty daughters, one of whom was the mother of Achilles. Metrodorus also says that according to some, Hellen was the offspring of Deucalion, who was the son of Prometheus and Pyrrha, the daughter of Epimetheus; and that from him came Dorus and Aeolus. He further observes that he learned from the sacred rites of the Babylonians that Hellen was the offspring of Jupiter, and that the sons of Hellen were Dorus, Xuthus, and Aeolus—accounts with which Herodotus also agrees. However, it is difficult for those in more recent times to know accurately, in details so ancient, which of these accounts is to be preferred. But it may be gathered from each of these histories that the Doric dialect is acknowledged to be the most ancient; that the Aeolic is next, receiving its name from Aeolus; and that the Ionic ranks third, deriving its name from Ion, the

<summary>This page explains the Pythagorean preference for the Doric dialect, linking it to musical harmony and ancient Greek genealogy. It details how the sect valued native language while still attracting members from various non-Greek regions like Rome.</summary>

--- Page 139 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the origins and antiquity of the Greek dialects, specifically focusing on the Attic dialect as the fourth in line of development, following the previous discussion of the Doric dialect.</meta>

son of Xuthus. The Attic is the fourth, which was named after Creusa, the daughter of Erechtheus, and is later than the previous dialects by three generations, as it existed around the time of the Thracians and the kidnapping of Orithyia, as is evident from the testimony of most histories. Orpheus also, who is the oldest of the poets, used the Doric dialect.

Regarding medicine, however, they especially embraced the dietetic branch, and were most precise in its practice. And first, indeed, they sought to learn the indicators of balance between labor, food, and rest. Next, regarding the preparation of food, they were among the first to apply themselves to it and to define the manner in which it should be performed. The Pythagoreans also used poultices <note>cataplasms</note> more frequently than their predecessors, but they approved of medicated ointments to a lesser degree. They mainly used these, however, for curing ulcers. But they accepted surgery <note>incisions</note> and cauterization <note>burnings</note> least of all. They also cured some diseases with incantations. However, they are said to have objected to those who put teachings <note>disciplines</note> up for sale; who open their souls like the gates of an inn to every man who approaches them; and who, if they do not find buyers that way, spread themselves through cities and, in short, rent gymnasiums and demand payment from young men for things that are priceless. Pythagoras, however, concealed the meaning of much of what he said so that those who were sincerely instructed might clearly share in it; but others, as Homer says of Tantalus, might be distressed in the midst of what they heard, because they received no enjoyment from it.

I also think it was said by the Pythagoreans, regarding those who teach for the sake of a reward, that they show themselves to be worse than sculptors <note>statuaries</note> or those artists who do their work while sitting. For these,

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the Pythagoreans’ selective process for students and their strict adherence to secrecy regarding their mathematical and philosophical discoveries.</meta>

...when someone orders them to make a statue of Hermes, they search for wood suitable for receiving the proper form; but those people pretend that they can readily produce the works of virtue from every nature.<sup>1</sup> The Pythagoreans likewise said that it is more necessary to pay attention to philosophy than to parents and agriculture; for it is because of the latter, indeed, that we live, but philosophers and teachers are the reasons why we live well and become wise, as a result of having discovered the right mode of discipline and instruction. Nor did they think it fit either to speak or write in such a way that their concepts might be obvious to any random person; instead, Pythagoras is said to have taught this first to those who came to him: that, being purified from all lack of self-control, they should preserve the doctrines they had heard in silence. It is said, therefore, that he who first revealed the theory of commensurable and incommensurable quantities to those who were unworthy to receive it was so hated by the Pythagoreans that they not only expelled him from their fellowship and from living with them, but even built a tomb for him, as if he had departed from human life and passed into another. Others also say that the Divine Power was angry with those who revealed the dogmas of Pythagoras: for that person perished in the sea as an unholy man who made public the composition of the <term>icostagonus</term>; that is, who delivered the method of inscribing the <term>dodecahedron</term> (which is one of what are called the five solid figures) within a sphere.

<sup>1</sup> This observation applies also to those of the present day who, from a profound ignorance of human nature, attempt to enlighten the *lowest* class of mankind through education. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the Pythagorean tradition of secrecy and the consequences for those who revealed their mathematical doctrines, transitioning into an account of the opposition they faced.</meta>

But according to others, this happened to the person who revealed the doctrine of irrational and incommensurable quantities.<note>This is also asserted, as I have previously observed, in the Scholia on the 10th book of Commandine’s edition of Euclid’s Elements, page 122.</note> Moreover, all the Pythagorean discipline was symbolic and resembled enigmas and riddles, consisting of aphorisms because they imitated the character of antiquity. Just as the truly divine and Pythian oracles appear to be, in a certain respect, difficult to understand and explain to those who carelessly receive the answers they give, such, therefore, are the many indications concerning Pythagoras and the Pythagoreans which may be collected from what is reported about them.

### CHAP. XXXV.

**There** were, however, certain people who were hostile to these men and rose against them. It is therefore universally acknowledged that plots were employed to destroy them during the absence of Pythagoras; but those who have written on this subject differ in their account of the journey he undertook at that time. For some say he went to Pherecydes the Syrian, while others say he went to Metapontum. Many causes for these plots are listed. One of them, which is said to have originated from the men called Cylonians, was as follows: Cylon the Crotonian held the highest rank among the citizens due to his birth, renown, and wealth; but otherwise, he was a severe, violent, and turbulent man with tyrannical manners. He had, however, the greatest desire to participate in the Pythagorean life, and having applied to Pythagoras—who was now an elderly man<note>Obrechtus has omitted to translate the words ἤδη πρεσβύτην ὄντα, meaning “being now an elderly man.”</note>—for this purpose, he was rejected

<meta>This page continues the account of the rejection of Cylon by Pythagoras and the subsequent hostility that forced Pythagoras to leave for Metapontum.</meta>

by him due to the reasons mentioned above. Consequently, he and his friends launched violent attacks against Pythagoras and his disciples. The ambition of Cylon and of those who sided with him was so vehement and immoderate <note>In the original Greek, the word is *akratos*, which Obrechtus very erroneously translates as *impotens*.</note> that it extended even to the very last of the Pythagoreans. For this reason, Pythagoras went to Metapontum and is said to have ended his life there. 

However, those known as the Cylonians continued to plot against the Pythagoreans and to show every sign of malevolence. Nevertheless, for a certain time, the integrity of the Pythagoreans—and the decision of the cities themselves—restrained this enmity, so that the cities were willing to have their political affairs managed by the Pythagoreans alone. Eventually, however, the Cylonians became so hostile toward the men <note>That is, toward the Pythagoreans.</note> that they set fire to the house of Milo, where the Pythagoreans were gathered and consulting about military matters. They burned all the men except for two, Archippus and Lysis; because these two were in peak physical condition and very strong, they escaped from the house. 

After this occurred, and because the public made no mention of the tragedy that had happened, the Pythagoreans stopped paying any further attention to the affairs of government. This happened for two reasons: the negligence of the cities (for they were not at all affected by such a great tragedy occurring) and the loss of those men who were most qualified to govern. Of the two Pythagoreans who were saved, both of whom were from Tarentum, Archippus returned to Tarentum; however, Lysis, hating the negligence of the cities, went to Greece and lived in Achaia in the Peloponnese. Later, he moved to Thebes, being

The most famous of them, however, were Phanto, Echecrates, Polymnastus, and Diocles, who were Phliasians; and Xenophilus Chalcidensis of Thrace. These things, therefore, are narrated by Aristoxenus.

Nicomachus, however, agrees with Aristoxenus in other respects, but as to the journey of Pythagoras, he says that this plot took place while Pythagoras was at Delos. For he went there in order to give assistance to his teacher, Pherecydes the Syrian, who was then afflicted with the <term>morbus pedicularis</term> <note>A disease characterized by an infestation of lice.</note>, and when Pherecydes died, Pythagoras performed the necessary funeral rites. Then, therefore, those who had been rejected by the Pythagoreans, and for whom monuments had been raised as if they were dead, attacked them and committed all of them to the flames. Afterward, they were overwhelmed by the Italians with stones and thrown out of the house unburied. At that time, therefore, it happened that knowledge failed along with those who possessed it, because until that period, it was preserved by them in their hearts as something secret and unspeakable. But only such things as were difficult to understand, and which were not fully explained, were preserved in the memory of those who did not belong to the Pythagorean sect—with the exception of a few things which certain Pythagoreans, who happened to be in foreign lands at that time, preserved as certain sparks of knowledge, though they were very obscure and difficult

investigation. These people as well, being left on their own and profoundly dejected by the calamity, were scattered in different places and could no longer bear to have any interaction with the rest of mankind. Instead, they lived alone in solitary places, wherever they happened to find them; and each greatly preferred his own company to that of any other person.

However, fearing that the name of philosophy might be entirely wiped out from mankind, and that they might incur the indignation of the Gods by allowing such a great gift of theirs to perish, they organized certain commentaries and symbols. They also collected the writings of the more ancient Pythagoreans and recorded the things they remembered. At their deaths, each left these to his son, daughter, or wife, with a strict command not to give them to anyone outside the family. This mandate, therefore, was observed for a long time and was transmitted in succession to their descendants.

However, since Apollonius disagrees in a certain place regarding these details and adds many things we have not mentioned, we shall also insert his account of the stratagem employed against the Pythagoreans. He says, therefore, that the envy of others followed Pythagoras from his childhood. For as long as he spoke with everyone who came to him, he was pleasing to them; but when he associated only with his disciples, the favorable opinion that the public held of him diminished. They indeed allowed him to pay more attention to strangers than to themselves; but they were indignant that he preferred some of their fellow citizens over others, and they feared that his disciples met together with hostile intentions toward them. Furthermore, as the young men who were indignant with him were of high rank and surpassed others in wealth, when they reached a proper age, they not only

<summary>Surviving Pythagoreans lived in isolation to preserve their teachings through private family records, while Apollonius attributes the public's growing hostility toward Pythagoras to his perceived elitism and secrecy.</summary>

--- Page 145 ---
<meta>This page continues the account by Apollonius regarding the political tensions in Crotona and the specific customs and reverence that characterized the Pythagorean community.</meta>

They held the highest honors within their own families and also managed the affairs of the city together. They formed a large body of men (for they were more than three hundred in number), and as a consequence of this, there was only a small part of the city that was not familiar with the same manners and the same pursuits as they were.

Moreover, as long as the Crotonians remained in their own country and Pythagoras lived among them, the form of government that had been established when the city was founded remained in place. However, it was not pleasing to the common people, and this led them to seek an opportunity to create a change. Therefore, when Sybaris was captured and the land taken in the war was not divided by lot according to the desire of the multitude, their silent hatred of the Pythagoreans burst forth, and the populace separated themselves from them. The leaders of this dissension were those who were closest to the Pythagoreans, both by alliance and familiarity. The cause of the discord, however, was that many of the actions of the Pythagoreans offended these leaders, as well as ordinary people, because these actions had something peculiar in them when compared with the actions of others. But in the greatest of these actions, they felt that disgrace fell upon themselves alone.

Furthermore, none of the Pythagoreans called Pythagoras by his name; while he was alive, when they wished to refer to him, they called him *divine*; and after his death, they named him *that man*, just as Homer represents Eumæus when he mentions Ulysses, saying,

> *Though he is absent, I fear to name him, O guest; such is the greatness of my love and care.*

Likewise, in accordance with the precepts of their master, the Pythagoreans always rose from bed before the rising of the sun; and they never wore a ring in which the

<meta>This page continues the description of Pythagorean daily rituals and the growing social and political friction between the sect and the broader community in Crotona.</meta>

image of God was engraved. They also carefully observed the practice of adoring the rising sun, and they avoided wearing a ring of the description mentioned above, so that they would not have it with them at funerals or in any impure place. In a similar manner, they were attentive to the command of Pythagoras not to do anything without prior deliberation and investigation; instead, they were to form a plan in the morning of what ought to be done <note>in the course of the day</note>, and at night, they were to recall the actions of the day. By this means, they simultaneously examined their conduct and exercised their memory. They also observed the precept that if any of their associates appointed to meet them at a certain place, they should stay there until he arrived, throughout the day and the night; in this, the Pythagoreans were again accustomed to remember what was said and not to speak carelessly. In short, Pythagoras ordered them to be attentive to order and method as long as they lived, and not to speak irreverently at the time of death, but to die with favorable words, such as those used by people sailing out of port into the Adriatic Sea. <note>This entire paragraph, the greater part of which is a repetition of what has been said elsewhere, certainly does not belong in this place.</note>

The relatives of the Pythagoreans, however, were indignant that the Pythagoreans gave their right hand only to those of their own sect (with the exception of their parents), and that they shared their possessions in common with each other while excluding their own relations from this fellowship as if they were strangers. As these relatives became the source of the dissension, the rest of the people readily grew hostile toward the Pythagoreans. Hippasus, Diodorus, and Theages also argued at the same time that every citizen ought to participate in the magistracy and the assembly, and that the rulers should give an account of their conduct to those elected by lot for this purpose from among the common people. But the

<meta>This page follows the description of the rising social tension between the Pythagoreans and the citizens of Crotona, transitioning into a specific political attack led by Cylon and Ninon.</meta>

Alcimachus, Dimachus, Meton, and Democedes opposed this and persisted in prohibiting the dissolution of the government inherited from their ancestors. However, those who supported the common people overcame the other party. Once the multitude was assembled, Cylon and Ninon, who were rhetoricians, accused the Pythagoreans. One of them belonged to the wealthy class, while the other was a commoner. They divided their speeches between themselves. Cylon delivered the longer of these speeches, and Ninon concluded it, pretending that he had explored the secrets of the Pythagoreans. He claimed he had compiled and written down specific details that were especially intended to incriminate them, and handing a book to a scribe, he ordered him to read it. The book was titled *The Sacred Discourse*. The following is a sample of what it contained: Friends are to be revered in the same way as the Gods, but others are to be treated like animals. This very sentence is also attributed to Pythagoras by his disciples and is expressed by them in verse as follows:

->He revered his friends like the blessed Gods,<-
->But considered other men of no account.<-

Homer, too, especially deserves praise for calling a king *the shepherd of the people*. For, being a friend to that government in which the rulers are few, he demonstrated by this title that the rest of men are like cattle. It is necessary to be hostile toward beans because they are the primary tools for decision by lot; for by these, men were assigned the administration of public affairs. Furthermore, power should be an object of desire, for they proclaim that it is better to be a bull for one day than to be an ox forever. They argued that while the legal institutions of others are praiseworthy, people should be encouraged to use only those known to themselves. In short, Ninon showed that their philosophy was a conspiracy against the common people, and

<summary>Cylon and Ninon lead a political assembly against the Pythagoreans, using a text called The Sacred Discourse to argue that the sect’s teachings are elitist and constitute a conspiracy against the common citizens.</summary>
<keywords>Cylon, Ninon, Pythagoras, political assembly, The Sacred Discourse, anti-Pythagoreanism, social hierarchy, beans, Homer</keywords>

--- Page 148 ---
<meta>This page continues the account of the political agitation against the Pythagoreans in Crotona, following Ninon's public accusations and his attempt to incite the populace against their influence.</meta>

He therefore exhorted them not to listen to the counselors, but to consider that they would never have been admitted into the assembly if the council of the Pythagoreans had been approved by the session of a thousand men. Therefore, it was not appropriate to allow those people to speak who had done everything in their power to prevent others from being heard. He remarked, therefore, that they should consider the right hand—which was rejected by the Pythagoreans—as hostile to them when they cast their votes by a show of hands or calculated the number of votes. They should also consider it a disgraceful circumstance that those who conquered thirty myriads of men at the River Tracis should be defeated by a thousandth part of that same number through rebellion within the city itself. In short, Ninon so exasperated his listeners with his slanders that a few days later, a great multitude assembled, intending to attack the Pythagoreans while they were sacrificing to the Muses in a house near the temple of Apollo. 

The Pythagoreans, however, foreseeing that this would happen, fled to an inn; but Democedes, with those who had reached puberty, withdrew to Platea. And those who had dissolved the laws issued a decree in which they accused Democedes of forcing the younger members of the community to seize power, and they proclaimed through a crier that thirty talents would be given to anyone who killed him. After a battle took place and Theages defeated Democedes in that contest, they gave him the thirty talents the city had promised. But as the city and the entire region were involved in many troubles, the exiles were brought to trial. The power of judgment was given to three cities—namely, the Tarentines, Metapontines, and Caulonians—but those sent by them to decide the case were corrupted by money, as we learn from the chronicles of the Crotonians. Therefore, the Crotonians, by their own decision, condemned those who were

<summary>Ninon incites a violent mob against the Pythagoreans, leading to the flight of the group and a bounty on Democedes. After a period of internal strife and a corrupted judicial process mediated by neighboring cities, the Crotonians condemn the exiles.</summary>
<keywords>Ninon, Pythagoreans, Democedes, Crotona, political sedition, River Tracis, Theages, corruption, Tarentines, Metapontines, Caulonians</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the political upheaval in Crotona against the Pythagoreans, following the legal proceedings overseen by the three neighboring cities.</meta>

accused, to exile. Because of this decision and the authority it gave them, they expelled everyone from the city who was dissatisfied with the current state of affairs. At the same time, they banished all their families, asserting that it was not right to be impious and that children should not be separated from their parents. They also canceled all loans and declared the land to be undivided. <note>In the original, *καὶ τὴν γην ἀνάδαστον ἐποίησαν*, which Obrechtus incorrectly translates as “and they introduced the division of fields.”</note>

Many years after this, when Dinarchus and his associates were killed in another battle, and Litagus—who had been the greatest leader of the rebels—was also dead, a certain pity and regret led the citizens to recall the remaining Pythagoreans from exile. For this purpose, they sent ambassadors from Achaia, and through them, they became friendly with the exiles and consecrated their oaths at Delphi. The Pythagoreans who returned from exile numbered about sixty, not including those of a more advanced age. Among them were some who applied themselves to medicine and restored the health of the sick through a specific diet; they were the original authors of this method of cure. 

It happened, however, that those Pythagoreans who were saved and were particularly honored by the public—at that time when it was said to the lawless, *This is not the condition of things which was under Ninon*—perished in battle while defending each other. They had left the city to find help against the Thurians who were invading the country. But the city's opinion had changed so much in favor of the Pythagoreans that, beyond the praise it gave them, the citizens believed they would please the Muses even more if they performed a public sacrifice in the temple of the Muses. This was the same temple they had previously built in honor of those goddesses at the request of the Pythagoreans.

<summary>Following the exile and land redistribution in Crotona, the citizens eventually regret their actions and recall the surviving Pythagoreans. Despite their contributions to medicine and the city's defense against the Thurians, many of the returning Pythagoreans die in battle, leading to a public shift in favor of honoring them and the Muses.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoreans, Crotona, exile, land redistribution, Dinarchus, Litagus, Achaia, Delphi, medicine, dietary cures, Thurians, Temple of the Muses, Ninon</keywords>

### CHAP. XXXVI.

The successor of Pythagoras, however, is acknowledged by all people to have been Aristaeus, the son of Damophon the Crotonian. Living at the same time as Pythagoras, he lived seven generations before Plato. Aristaeus was also thought worthy not only to succeed Pythagoras in his school but also to educate his children and marry his wife, Theano, because he was eminently skilled in the Pythagorean doctrines. For Pythagoras himself is said to have taught in his school for thirty-nine years and to have lived nearly one hundred years. But Aristaeus, when quite advanced in years, stepped down from the school; and Mnesarchus, the son of Pythagoras, succeeded him. Bulagoras succeeded Mnesarchus, and it was during his time that the city of the Crotonians was plundered. Gartydas the Crotonian succeeded Bulagoras upon his return from a journey he had taken before the war. Nevertheless, because of his country’s misfortune, he suffered such great anxiety that he died prematurely from grief. However, it was the custom among the rest of the Pythagoreans, when they became very old, to free themselves from the body as if from a prison.

Moreover, some time later, Aresas the Lucanian, having been saved by certain strangers, undertook the leadership of the school. Diodorus of Aspendus came to him and was received into the school because of the scarcity of Pythagoreans it then contained. In Heraclea were Clinias and Philolaus; at Metapontum, Theorides and Eurytus; and at Tarentum, Archytas. It is also said that Epicharmus

It also identifies the geographic locations of key Pythagorean philosophers in various cities like Heraclea, Metapontum, and Tarentum.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Aristaeus, Mnesarchus, school succession, Crotona, Pythagorean doctrines, Theano, Greek philosophy, Southern Italy</keywords>

### OF THE

# ETHICAL WRITINGS

### OF CERTAIN

## PYTHAGOREANS.
