<meta>This page begins the first specific fragment in the collection of Pythagorean ethical writings, transitioning from the previous title page to the work of Hippodamus the Thurian.</meta>

->### FROM<-
-># HIPPODAMUS THE THURIAN,<-
->### IN HIS TREATISE<-
->## ON HAPPINESS.<-

---

Of animals, some are the recipients of happiness, but others are incapable of receiving it. And those animals, indeed, are receptive to it that possess reason. For happiness cannot exist without virtue; and virtue is first generated in that which possesses reason. But those animals are incapable of receiving happiness that are destitute of reason. For just as that which is deprived of sight cannot perform the work or possess the virtue of sight, neither can that which is destitute of reason be the recipient of the work or the virtue of that which possesses reason. With respect to happiness, however, and virtue, the former is like a finished work, but the latter is like a certain art to that which possesses reason.

But of animals which possess reason, some are self-perfect; these are such as are perfect through themselves and lack nothing external, either for their existence or for their living well and nobly. And such a being, indeed, is God. Those animals, however, are not self-perfect which are not perfect through themselves, but are in need of external causes for their perfection. And man is an animal of this kind. Of animals, therefore, which are not self-perfect, some indeed are perfect, but others are not perfect. And those indeed are perfect which derive their existence both from their own proper <note>The word "proper" was an editorial addition in the original text.</note> causes and from external causes. And they derive it indeed from their

<summary>Hippodamus argues that only rational beings can achieve happiness because it requires virtue, and he distinguishes between self-perfect beings like God and humans who require external causes for perfection.</summary>
<keywords>Hippodamus the Thurian, happiness, virtue, reason, self-perfect, God, human nature, perfection</keywords>

--- Page 158 ---
<meta>This page continues Hippodamus the Thurian’s discourse on the nature of perfection and happiness, specifically distinguishing between the perfection of God and the perfection of man.</meta>

own causes, because they obtain from them both an excellent nature and deliberate choice; but from external causes, because they receive from them equitable legislation and good rulers. But those animals that are not perfect are either those that participate in neither of these, or in only one of them, or whose souls are entirely depraved. And such will the man be who is of a description different from the one mentioned above.

Moreover, there are two types of perfect men. For some of them are naturally perfect, while others are perfect according to their life. And indeed, only those who are good are naturally perfect. These are the ones who possess virtue. For the virtue of the nature of everything is its summit and perfection. Thus, the virtue of the eye is the summit and perfection of the nature of the eye. Similarly, the virtue of man is the summit and perfection of human nature.

Those are also perfect according to their life who are not only good, but happy. For happiness, indeed, is the perfection of human life. But human life is a system of actions, and happiness gives completion to those actions. Virtue and fortune also give completion to actions: virtue, indeed, through its use, but good fortune through prosperity. 

Therefore, God is neither good through learning virtue from anyone, nor is he happy through being accompanied by good fortune. For he is good by nature and happy by nature; he always was, is, and will be so, and will never cease to be such, since he is incorruptible and naturally good. But man is neither happy nor good by nature; instead, he requires discipline and providential care. To become good, he requires virtue; but to become happy, he requires good fortune. On this account, human happiness essentially consists of these two things: namely, praise and the declaration of blessedness. Praise comes from virtue, while the declaration of blessedness comes from prosperity. Therefore, man possesses virtue through a divine destiny, but prosperity through a mortal allotment.

<summary>Hippodamus distinguishes between natural perfection (possessing virtue) and perfection of life (possessing both virtue and happiness). He explains that while God is naturally perfect, humans must strive for virtue through divine destiny and hope for prosperity through mortal allotment to achieve full happiness.</summary>

<keywords>Hippodamus the Thurian, virtue, happiness, felicity, beatitude, human perfection, divine nature, fortune, prosperity, divine destiny, mortal allotment</keywords>

--- Page 159 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discourse on the nature of human happiness, virtue, and the relationship between the mortal and the divine.</meta>

But mortal things are dependent on divine concerns, and terrestrial things on those that are celestial. Subordinate things, also, are dependent on those that are more excellent. For this reason, the good man who follows the Gods is happy; but he who follows mortal natures is miserable. For to one who possesses wisdom, prosperity is good and useful. It is good because of his knowledge of how to use it; but it is useful because it cooperates with his actions. It is beautiful, therefore, when prosperity is joined with intellect, and when—sailing as if with a favorable wind—actions are performed with an eye toward virtue, just as a pilot looks to the motions of the stars. For in this way, whoever does this will not only follow God, but will also align human good with divine good.

It is also evident that <note>human</note> life is shaped by disposition and action. However, it is necessary that the disposition be either worthy or depraved, and that action be accompanied by either happiness or misery. A worthy disposition participates in virtue, while a bad one participates in vice. Regarding actions, those that are prosperous are accompanied by happiness (for they achieve their completion by following reason), but those that are unfortunate are accompanied by misery, for they fail to reach their goal. Hence, it is not only necessary to learn virtue, but also to possess and use it, whether for security, or for the increase <note>of property when it is insufficient</note>, or—which is the greatest thing of all—for the improvement of families and cities. For it is not only necessary to possess beautiful things, but also to use them. All these things, however, will happen when a man lives in a city that employs equitable laws. These laws, I say, are what is called the horn of Amalthea. For all things are contained within equitable legislation. Without this, the greatest good of human nature can neither be achieved, nor, once achieved, can it be increased or become permanent.

<summary>The author argues that human happiness and prosperity depend on aligning one's actions with divine virtue and wisdom. He concludes that such virtue can only be fully realized and preserved through equitable legislation within a city, comparing just laws to the "horn of Amalthea" or total abundance.</summary>
<keywords>divine concerns, virtue, prosperity, intellect, human happiness, equitable legislation, horn of Amalthea, social order</keywords>

--- Page 160 ---
<meta>This page continues the philosophical discussion regarding how equitable laws and a well-ordered society (compared to the horn of Amalthea) are essential for virtue and human happiness.</meta>

For this includes within itself virtue and the tendency toward virtue, because excellent characters are formed according to it. Likewise, manners, pursuits, and laws exist through this in the most excellent condition; and besides these, it includes correctly-judging reason, as well as piety and holiness toward the most honorable beings. Thus, it is necessary that he who is to be happy, and whose life is to be prosperous, should live and die in a country governed by fair laws, abandoning all lawlessness. At the same time, what has been said is necessarily true. For man is a part of society; therefore, by the same reasoning, he will become complete and perfect if he not only interacts with others, but interacts in an appropriate manner. 

For some things are naturally adapted to exist in many things and not in a single thing; others exist in one thing and not in many; but others exist both in many and in one thing—and they exist in the individual because they exist in the collective. For harmony, indeed, and symphony and number, are naturally adapted to be produced in many things. For no part that makes up a whole from these components is sufficient by itself. <note>In the original, οὐδέν γάρ αὐτάρκες, ὃ τουτῶν τῶν μορίων ποιεῖ τὸ ὅλον. Canter erroneously translates this: “*Since no part of these can constitute a whole.*” And Gale has not noticed the error.</note> But sharpness of sight and hearing, and swiftness of feet, exist in one thing alone. Happiness, however, and the virtue of the soul, exist both in one thing and in many, in a whole, and in the universe. And for this reason, they exist in one thing because they also exist in many; and they exist in many because they are inherent in a whole and in the universe. For the orderly distribution of the whole nature of things methodically arranges each particular part. And the orderly distribution of particulars completes the whole of things and the universe. But this follows from the whole being naturally prior to the part, and not the part to the

<summary>This text argues that human happiness and virtue are dependent on living in a society with equitable laws, as the individual is a part of a larger social and cosmic whole. It explains that while some qualities belong to individuals, higher virtues like happiness and harmony exist because they are part of the orderly distribution of the entire universe.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, society, equitable laws, harmony, happiness, social order, cosmology, Canter, Gale</keywords>

--- Page 161 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that the well-being and existence of individual parts (whether in nature, the body, or the state) are entirely dependent upon the existence and virtue of the whole.</meta>

whole. For if the world did not exist, neither the sun nor the moon would exist, nor the planets, nor the fixed stars. But because the world exists, each of these also exists.

The truth of this may also be seen in the very nature of animals. For if the animal had no existence, there would be neither eye, nor mouth, nor ear. But because the animal exists, each of these likewise exists. As the whole is to the part, however, so is the virtue of the whole to the virtue of the part. For if harmony and a divine inspection of worldly affairs did not exist, things which are ordered would no longer be able to remain in an excellent condition. And if equitable legislation did not exist in a city, it is not possible for a citizen to be good or happy. Likewise, if health did not exist in the animal, it is not possible for the foot or the hand to be strong and healthy. For harmony is indeed the virtue of the world; equitable legislation is the virtue of a city; and health and strength are the virtue of the body. Likewise, each of the parts in these things is arranged together for the sake of the whole and the universe. For the eyes see for the sake of the whole body. And the other parts and members are arranged together for the sake of the whole [body] and the universe.

<vocab>harmony, virtue, world, city, body, universe, equitable legislation</vocab>

<summary>This text argues that the existence and health of individual parts—such as celestial bodies, organs, or citizens—depend entirely on the existence and proper ordering of the whole system to which they belong. It concludes that harmony, fair laws, and health are the essential "virtues" that allow the world, the city, and the body to function correctly.</summary>
<keywords>holism, harmony, equitable legislation, virtue, cosmology, political philosophy, anatomy, interconnectedness</keywords>

--- Page 162 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general principle of parts and wholes to the specific nature of human life, positioning man between the self-perfection of God and the irrationality of animals.</meta>

### FROM
# EURYPHAMUS,
### IN HIS TREATISE
## CONCERNING HUMAN LIFE.

---

The perfect life of man indeed falls short of the life of God, because it is not perfect in itself; yet it surpasses the life of irrational animals because it shares in virtue and happiness. For God is not in need of external causes; since being naturally good and happy, he is perfect from within himself. Neither is any irrational animal in need of them. For since brutes are devoid of reason, they are also devoid of the knowledge pertaining to actions. But the nature of man consists partly of his own deliberate choice, and partly requires the assistance derived from divinity. For that which is capable of being shaped by reason—which has an intellectual understanding of things beautiful and base, can raise itself up from the earth and look toward heaven, and can perceive the highest Gods with the eye of the intellect—that which is capable of all this also shares in the assistance of the Gods. But because man possesses a will, deliberate choice, and a nature of such a kind that enables him to study virtue or be agitated by the storms of vice, to follow or also to turn away from the Gods, he is likewise able to be moved by himself. Hence, he shares in praise and blame, honor and dishonor, partly from the Gods and partly from men, according to whether he zealously applies himself either to virtue or vice. For the whole reasoning behind this is as follows: Divinity introduced man into the world as a most exquisite animal, to be reciprocally

<summary>Euryphamus describes the unique position of human life as being between God and animals, possessing both reason and free will. He argues that while humans require divine assistance, they are also responsible for their own choices, leading to either virtue or vice and the resulting praise or blame.</summary>
<keywords>Euryphamus, human life, divinity, reason, free will, virtue, vice, ethics, theology</keywords>

--- Page 163 ---
<meta>This page continues Euryphamus's discussion of man's role in the universe, specifically focusing on the creation of society and the analogy of human life to a musical instrument.</meta>

honored with himself, and *as the eye of the orderly distribution of things.* Hence also man gave names to things, becoming himself their representative character. He likewise invented letters, obtaining through these a treasury of memory. And he imitated the established order of the universe, harmonizing the community of cities through judicial proceedings and laws. For no work is performed by men more appropriate for the world, or more worthy of the notice of the Gods, than the proper constitution of a city governed by good laws, and an orderly distribution of laws and a political system. For though each man on his own is nothing, and is not sufficient by himself to lead a life consistent with the common concord and proper composition of a political system, yet he is well adapted to the whole and to the perfect system of society.

For the life of man is the image of a lyre accurately [harmonized] and in every respect perfect. For every lyre requires these three things: apparatus, proper composition, and a certain <term>musical contrectation</term> <note>Musical handling or touch</note>. And apparatus, indeed, is a preparation of all the appropriate parts; namely, of the strings and of the instruments which assist with the proper sound and striking of the lyre. But the proper composition is the mixing of the sounds with each other. And the <term>musical contrectation</term> is the movement of these in accordance with that proper composition. 

Thus also human life requires these same three things. Apparatus, indeed, is the completion of the parts of life. But the parts of life are the goods of the body: wealth, fame, and friends. The proper composition is the arrangement of these according to virtue and the laws. And the <term>musical contrectation</term> is the blending of these according to virtue and the laws; virtue sailing with a favorable wind, and having nothing from the outside resisting it. For happiness does not consist in being driven from the purpose of one's voluntary intentions, but in obtaining them; nor does it consist in virtue being without attendants and helpful aids;

<summary>Euryphamus argues that man's greatest achievement is the creation of a lawful society, comparing the structure of a successful human life to the components and performance of a lyre. He defines happiness as the successful pursuit of virtuous intentions, supported by external goods like health, wealth, and friendship.</summary>
<keywords>civilization, laws, society, lyre analogy, virtue, happiness, apparatus, musical contrectation, political system</keywords>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of man’s role in the universe, moving from the social and political structure to the individual’s path toward perfection through the balance of virtue and prosperity.</meta>

but in completely possessing its own proper powers which are adapted to actions. For man is not self-perfect, but imperfect. And he becomes perfect, partly through himself and partly through an external cause. He is also perfect either according to nature or according to life.

And he is indeed perfect according to nature if he becomes a good man. For the virtue of each thing is the summit and perfection of that thing's nature. Thus, the virtue <note>^1 Gale says in his notes that after "eyes" he adds "nature," but he should clearly have added "virtue," as in the translation above.</note> of the eyes is the summit and perfection of the nature of the eyes; and this is also true of the virtue of the ears. Likewise, the virtue of man is the summit and perfection of human nature. 

But man is perfect according to life when he becomes happy. For happiness is the perfection and completion of human goods. Hence, again, virtue and prosperity become the parts of human life. And virtue is indeed a part of him insofar as he is a soul, but prosperity is a part of him insofar as he is connected with a body. But both are parts of him insofar as he is a living being. 

For it is the role of virtue to use the goods that conform to nature in an appropriate manner; but it is the role of prosperity to provide the use of them. And the former provides deliberate choice and right reason, while the latter provides energy and actions. For to desire what is honorable in conduct and to endure terrifying things is the proper business of virtue. But it is the work of prosperity to make deliberate choice successful and to cause actions to reach their intended end. 

For the general conquers through a combination of virtue and good fortune. The pilot sails well through a combination of skill and favorable winds. The eye sees well through a combination of sharpness of vision <note>^2 In the original, this is "with sharpness of vision," which Canter translates very poorly as "the faculty of seeing."</note> and light. And human life becomes most excellent through virtue itself and prosperity.

<summary>This text argues that human perfection requires both internal virtue of the soul and external prosperity of the body. While virtue directs the will and reason, prosperity provides the means and successful outcomes for virtuous actions.</summary>
<keywords>virtue, perfection, happiness, prosperity, soul and body, ethics, Thomas Gale, Willem Canter, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 165 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a general discussion on the perfection of human nature and happiness to a specific treatise by Hipparchus regarding the attainment of tranquillity through self-knowledge and the recognition of life's inherent hardships.</meta>

### FROM
# HIPPARCHUS,
### IN HIS TREATISE
## ON TRANQUILLITY.

---

Since men live for only a very short period, if their life is compared with the whole of time, they will make a most beautiful journey, as it were, if they pass through life with tranquillity. This, however, they will possess in the highest degree if they accurately and scientifically know themselves; namely, if they know that they are mortal and of a fleshly nature, and that they have a body which is corruptible and can be easily injured, and which is exposed to everything most distressing and severe, even to their latest breath. And in the first place, let us direct our attention to those things which happen to the body; and these are pleurisy, inflammation of the lungs, frenzy, gout, strangury, dysentery, lethargy, epilepsy, putrid ulcers, and ten thousand other diseases. 

But the diseases which happen to the soul are much greater and more dreadful than these. For all the wicked, evil, illegal, and impious conduct in the life of man originates from the passions of the soul. For through unnatural and immoderate desires, many have become subject to unrestrained impulses and have not refrained from the most unholy pleasures, arising from being connected with daughters or even mothers. Many also have been induced to destroy their fathers and their own offspring. But what need is there to be lengthy in describing external impending evils, such as excessive rain, drought, violent heat and cold; so that frequently from the

<summary>Hipparchus argues that tranquillity is achieved through self-knowledge and an understanding of the frail, mortal nature of the human body. He contrasts physical ailments with the more severe diseases of the soul, which lead to immoral and unnatural acts.</summary>
<keywords>Hipparchus, Tranquillity, self-knowledge, mortality, physical diseases, passions of the soul, moral corruption, external evils</keywords>

--- Page 166 ---
<meta>This page continues Hipparchus's discourse on the instability of life, transitioning from environmental disasters to the necessity of maintaining internal peace despite the fleeting nature of physical health and external wealth.</meta>

an unusual state of the air, pestilence and famine are produced, and various kinds of calamities, and whole cities become desolate? Since, therefore, many such calamities are impending, we should neither be puffed up by the possession of physical goods, which may be rapidly consumed by the onset of a minor fever, nor by what are conceived to be prosperous external circumstances, which frequently, by their own nature, perish more rapidly than they arrive. For all these things are uncertain and unstable, and are found to exist through many and various changes; and none of them is permanent, immutable, stable, or indestructible. Hence, by considering these things well, and also being persuaded that if what is present and given to us is able to remain for even the smallest portion of time, it is as much as we ought to expect, we shall then live in tranquillity and with cheerfulness, generously bearing whatever may befall us.

Now, however, many people previously imagine that everything present with them and given to them by nature and fortune is better than it really is. Not thinking of it as it is in reality, but as it could become when it has arrived at the summit of excellence, they burden the soul with many great, wicked, and foolish evils when they are suddenly deprived of <note>these fleeting goods</note>. And thus it happens to them that they lead a most bitter and miserable life. This occurs in the loss of riches, or the death of friends or children, or in the deprivation of certain other things which are conceived by them to be their most honorable possessions. Afterward, weeping and lamenting, they claim for themselves that they alone are the most unfortunate and miserable, not remembering that these things have happened, and even now happen, to many others. Nor are they able to understand the lives of those who are now in existence, and of those who have lived in former times, nor to see the great calamities and surges of evil in which many of

<summary>Hipparchus explains that because physical health and external wealth are inherently unstable, one must accept their fleeting nature to maintain tranquillity. He argues that misery arises from unrealistic expectations of permanent prosperity and a failure to recognize that suffering is a universal human experience.</summary>

<keywords>Hipparchus, tranquillity, impermanence, physical goods, fortune, grief, resilience, stoicism</keywords>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page continues the discourse on the instability of human life and the philosophical necessity of maintaining internal peace despite external losses or misfortunes.</meta>

the present time are, and of the past have been, involved. Therefore, when we consider that many people who lost their property were saved because of that very loss—since they might otherwise have fallen into the hands of robbers or the power of a tyrant; and that many who have loved certain people and been extremely well-disposed toward them have later come to hate them greatly;—considering all these things, which have been passed down to us by history, and also learning that many have been destroyed by their own children and by those they loved most dearly; and comparing our own lives with those who have been more unhappy than we are, while taking into account human misfortunes <note>The original text uses "casualties" to mean chance occurrences or mishaps.</note> in general and not only those that happen to us, we will pass through life with greater peace. For it is not right that a man should think the calamities of others are easy to bear while his own are not, especially since he sees that all of life is naturally exposed to many disasters.

However, those who weep and lament—besides being unable to recover what they have lost or call the dead back to life—drive the soul into even greater disturbances because it becomes filled with much corruption. It is necessary, therefore, that after being washed and purified, we should use every possible means to wipe away our deep-rooted stains through the reasoning of philosophy. We will accomplish this by adhering to wisdom and moderation, being satisfied with our current circumstances, and not constantly aspiring for many things. For men who acquire a great abundance <note>of external goods</note> do not consider that the enjoyment of such things ends with this life. Therefore, we ought to use the goods we currently have; and with the help of the beautiful and venerable things that come from philosophy, we will be liberated from the insatiable desire for worthless possessions.

<summary>The author argues that human misfortune is universal and that reflecting on history and the troubles of others can bring perspective and peace. He concludes that philosophy, wisdom, and moderation are the keys to purging the soul of grief and the insatiable desire for temporary external goods.</summary>

<keywords>philosophy, history, human misfortunes, wisdom, moderation, external goods, calamities, soul, temperance</keywords>

### FROM
## ARCHYTAS,
### IN HIS TREATISE CONCERNING
# THE GOOD AND HAPPY MAN.

---

In the first place, it is necessary to know this: that a good man is not automatically happy by necessity, but this is the case for the man who is both happy and good. For the happy man obtains both praise and the declaration of blessedness, but the good man <note>so far as he is good</note> obtains praise alone. Praise also arises from virtue, but the declaration of blessedness comes from good fortune. And the worthy man, indeed, becomes so from the goods which he possesses, but the happy man is sometimes deprived of his happiness. For the power of virtue is perfectly free, but the power of happiness is subject to restraint. For long-continued bodily diseases and the loss of the senses cause the flourishing condition of happiness to waste away. God, however, differs from a good man in this: God not only possesses virtue that is genuine and purified from every mortal passion, but His power is also unwearied and unrestrained, as it is suited for the most venerable and magnificent production of eternal works. Man indeed, by the mortal condition of his nature, not only enjoys this power and virtue to a lesser degree, but sometimes through a lack of symmetry <note>For *ou metrian* here, I read *asymmetrian*.</note> in the goods he possesses, or through powerful custom, or a corrupt nature, or through many other causes, he is unable to possess to the highest degree a good which is perfectly true.

<summary>Archytas distinguishes between the "good man," who earns praise through virtue, and the "happy man," who possesses both virtue and the good fortune of blessedness. He explains that while God possesses perfect, unrestrained virtue and power, human happiness is fragile and often limited by mortality and internal or external imbalances.</summary>

<keywords>Archytas, virtue, happiness, blessedness, God, mortality, symmetry, asymmetria, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical breakdown of goods and virtue, following the distinction between a "good man" and a "happy man" by categorizing the types of goods that lead to those states.</meta>

Since, therefore, among goods, some are desirable for their own sake and not for the sake of something else; while others are desirable for the sake of something else and not for their own sake; there is also a certain third species of goods which is desirable both for its own sake and for the sake of something else. What, then, is the good which is desirable for its own sake and not for the sake of something else? It is evident that it is happiness. For we strive for other things for the sake of this, but we do not desire this for the sake of anything else. Again, what are those goods which we desire indeed for the sake of something else, but which we do not desire for their own sake? It is evident they are such things as are useful and pre-desirable goods, which become the means of our obtaining things that are desirable <note>for their own sake</note>; such as physical labors, exercise, and massages which are employed for the sake of a good physical condition; and also reading, meditation, and study, which are undertaken for the sake of beautiful things and virtue. But what are the things which are desirable for their own sake and also for the sake of something else? They are such things as the virtues and their habits, deliberate choice and actions, and whatever adheres to that which is truly beautiful. Hence, that which is desirable for its own sake, and not on account of something else, is a single and solitary good. But that which is desirable for its own sake and for the sake of another thing is divided into three parts. For one part of it exists within the soul; another within the body; and another pertains to external things. And that which concerns the soul consists of the virtues of the soul; that which concerns the body consists of the virtues of the body; and that which pertains to external things consists of friends, glory, honor, and wealth. There is likewise a similar logic with respect to that which is desirable for the sake of something else. For one part of it indeed produces the goods of the

<summary>Archytas classifies goods into three categories: those desired for themselves (happiness), those desired as means to an end (like exercise or study), and those desired for both (like virtue). He further categorizes these goods as relating to the soul, the body, or external circumstances like wealth and honor.</summary>
<keywords>Archytas, ethics, types of goods, happiness, felicity, virtue, soul, physical health, external goods, wealth, glory</keywords>

--- Page 170 ---
<meta>This page continues the division of goods, concluding the list of things desirable for their own sake and for the sake of something else, before defining the nature of human happiness and the relationship between the soul and the body.</meta>

soul; another part of it consists of the goods of the body; and that which pertains to external things is the cause of wealth, glory, honor, and friendship.

That virtue, however, happens to be desirable for its own sake is evident from the following considerations. For if things that are naturally subordinate—I mean the goods of the body—are desirable for their own sakes, and since the soul is better than the body, it is evident that we love the goods of the soul on their own account, and not for the sake of the consequences that follow them.

There are likewise three definite times of human life: one of prosperity, another of adversity, and a third existing between these. Since, therefore, a good man is one who possesses and uses virtue, and he uses it according to three seasons—for he uses it either in adversity, in prosperity, or in the time between these—and in adversity he is indeed unhappy, but in prosperity happy, and in the middle condition he is not happy (though he is not miserable); this being the case, it is evident that happiness is nothing else than the use of virtue in prosperity. We now speak, however, of the happiness of man. But man is not soul alone, but is also body. For the animal which consists of both, and that which is formed from such things, is man. For though the body is naturally adapted to be the instrument of the soul, yet the body, as well as the soul, is a part of man (so far as he is an animal <note>1</note>). Hence, among goods, some are the goods of man, but others are the goods of the parts of man. And the good of man, indeed, is happiness. But of the parts of man, the good of the soul is prudence, fortitude, justice, and

Regarding external things, however, wealth, glory, honor, and nobility are naturally suited to attend to man and to follow preceding goods. The lesser goods, also, serve the greater goods. Thus friendship, glory, and wealth serve both the body and the soul; but health, strength, and excellence of sensation are subservient to the soul; and prudence <note>meaning wisdom</note> and justice serve the intellect of the soul. *Intellect, however, is the satellite of the Deity.* For God is the most excellent, and the leader and ruler of all things. And for the sake of these, it is necessary that other goods should be present. For the general is the leader of the army; the pilot, of the ship; God, of the world; and intellect, of the soul. But prudence is the leader of the happiness pertaining to life. For prudence is nothing else than the science of the happiness that respects human life, or the science of the goods that naturally pertain to man.

And the happiness and life of God are indeed most excellent; but the happiness of man consists of science, virtue, and in the third place, embodied prosperity <note>1</note>. But by science, I mean the wisdom

However, it is necessary to define prosperity as the presence of goods beyond reason <note>or a supply of goods without the assistance of reason</note>, and which is not produced because of reason.

Since, therefore, virtue and science are within our power, but prosperity is not; and since also happiness <note>felicity</note> consists in the contemplation and performance of things <note>truly</note> beautiful; and since contemplations and actions, when they are not prosperous, are accompanied by subservient duties and necessity, but when they proceed on the right path, produce delight and happiness—and these things are achieved in prosperity—this being the case, it is evident that *happiness is nothing other than the use of virtue in prosperity.* Hence, the good man is disposed toward prosperity in the same way as one who has an excellent and robust body. For such a person is able to endure heat and cold, to lift a heavy burden, and to easily sustain many other hardships.

Since happiness is therefore the use of virtue in prosperity, we must speak about virtue and prosperity, and first regarding prosperity. For among goods, some indeed do not allow for excess, and this is the case with virtue. For there is no virtue that is excessive, nor any worthy man who is good beyond measure. For virtue has what is fit and becoming as its rule, and is the disposition of what is proper in practical concerns. But prosperity allows for excess and deficiency. And when it is indeed excessive, it generates certain vices and removes a man from his natural disposition, so that through this he frequently opposes the constitu-

For it is not at all proper to wonder that some who play the pipe are arrogant men—who, abandoning the truth, deceive those unskilled in music with a certain false impression—and yet to disbelieve that a thing of this kind occurs in virtue. Indeed, the more respected a thing is, the more numerous are those who pretend to possess it. For there are many things that distort the habit and form of virtue: some are deceptive arts and affectation; others are related physical passions, which sometimes produce an impropriety<note><sup>1</sup> Instead of the word for "decorum" here, I read "impropriety."</note> contrary to the true disposition of virtue.

Therefore, we should never be surprised if a distorted judgment is sometimes formed about everything when the true disposition has changed.<note><sup>2</sup> In the original Greek, the text reads: *ὥστε οὐδέποκα δεῖ θαύμαινεν, εἰ παντ' ἀντεστραμμένως ἐνιόκα κρίνεται, τὰς ἀληθινας διαθέσιος μεταπίπτοισας*, which Canter mistakenly translates as follows: “Therefore it is not to be wondered at if all things sometimes, the true affection being changed, turn out differently.” This error was not noticed by Gale.</note> Thus we see that even the most excellent carpenter frequently makes mistakes in the works that are the subjects of his craft; and this is also the case with the general, the pilot, the painter, and in short, with all artists. --- Page 174 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on how external circumstances like prosperity and adversity affect human judgment and the state of the soul, building on the previous observation that true virtue can be obscured by such conditions.</meta>

...of this kind is occasionally right, and that good men sometimes err. A true judgment, however, in these instances is to be formed not by looking at a specific occasion or a certain span of time, but at the whole of life. But just as poverty and excess are harmful to the body—though excess and what are called superfluities are naturally inclined to produce greater diseases than those caused by poverty—so also prosperity or adversity injure the soul when they occur at the wrong time. Yet, what all men call prosperity is naturally inclined to produce greater diseases than adversity, since it intoxicates the reasoning power of good men like wine.

Hence, it is more difficult to bear prosperity in a proper manner than adversity. For when men remain in adversity, they are mostly seen to be moderate and orderly in their behavior; but in prosperity, they are brave, magnificent, and magnanimous when they bear it in a proper manner. For adversity has the power of contracting and depressing the soul, while prosperity, on the contrary, elevates and expands it. Hence, all those who are unfortunate are cautious and prudent in their manners, but those who are fortunate are insolent and overconfident. But the limit of prosperity is that which a good man would deliberately choose to assist him in his own appropriate actions, just as the proper size of a ship and the proper size of a rudder are what enable a good pilot to sail across a vast sea and complete a long voyage. An excess of prosperity, however, is not naturally inclined to be overcome by the soul, but rather to overcome it. For just as a very bright light causes the eyes to lose their sight, so also excessive prosperity darkens the intellect of the soul. And this much may suffice concerning prosperity.

<vocab>prosperity, adversity, soul, poverty, excess, intellect, judgment, virtue</vocab>

<summary>This text compares prosperity and adversity to physical health, arguing that while both can harm the soul if mismanaged, prosperity is more dangerous because it intoxicates the intellect and is harder to bear with moderation. It concludes that prosperity should be used as a tool for virtuous action, much like a properly sized rudder helps a pilot, rather than being allowed to overwhelm the soul.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from discussing how external prosperity and adversity affect the soul to an analysis of the soul's internal structure and the specific virtues belonging to its three parts.</meta>

### FROM
# THEAGES,
### IN HIS TREATISE
## ON THE VIRTUES.

---

The order of the soul exists in such a way that one part of it is the reasoning power, another is anger, and another is desire. The reasoning power, indeed, has dominion over knowledge; anger over impulse; and desire boldly rules over the appetites of the soul. When, therefore, these three parts merge into one and exhibit one appropriate composition, then virtue and concord are produced in the soul. But when they are separated from each other by internal conflict, then vice and discord are produced in the soul. It is necessary, however, that virtue should have these three things: reason, power, and deliberate choice. The virtue, therefore, of the reasoning power of the soul is prudence; for it is a habit of judging and contemplating. But the virtue of the irascible part is fortitude; for it is a habit of resisting and enduring things of a terrifying nature. And the virtue of the <term>epithymetic</term> or appetitive part is temperance; for it is a moderation and restraint of the pleasures which arise through the body. But the virtue of the whole soul is justice. For men indeed become bad either through vice, or through a lack of self-control, or through a natural ferocity. But they injure each other either for gain, or for pleasure, or through ambition. Vice, therefore, more appropriately belongs to the reasoning part of the soul. For prudence indeed is similar to art; but vice to

<summary>Theages outlines the tripartite structure of the soul—reason, anger, and desire—explaining how their harmony produces virtue and their conflict produces vice. He identifies the specific virtues associated with each part: prudence for reason, fortitude for the irascible part, and temperance for the appetitive part, with justice governing the whole.</summary>
<keywords>Theages, soul, reasoning power, anger, desire, virtue, vice, prudence, fortitude, temperance, justice, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how the three parts of the soul—the reasoning, the irascible (associated with anger), and the appetitive (associated with desire)—relate to specific vices and virtues.</meta>

destructive art. For it invents schemes for the purpose of acting unjustly. But incontinence <note>Incontinence here refers to a lack of self-control or self-restraint.</note> rather pertains to the appetitive part of the soul. For self-control consists in subduing pleasures, and incontinence in failing to subdue them. And ferocity pertains to the irascible part of the soul. For when someone, by acting poorly out of desire, is gratified not as a human should be, but as a wild beast, then such a thing is called ferocity. The effects of these dispositions also follow from the goals for which they are performed. For avarice follows from vice, and vice follows from the reasoning part of the soul. And ambition follows from the irascible part; when this becomes excessive, it generates ferocity. Again, pleasure pertains to the appetitive part; but when it is sought after too vehemently, it generates incontinence. Hence, since acting unjustly is produced by so many causes, it is evident that acting justly is achieved through an equal number of causes. For virtue is naturally beneficent and profitable, but vice produces evil and is harmful.

Since, however, one of the parts of the soul is the leader while the others follow, and the virtues and the vices exist around and within these parts, it is evident that among the virtues as well, some are leaders, others are followers, and others are composed of these. The leaders are virtues such as prudence; the followers are those such as fortitude and temperance; and the composites of these are virtues such as justice. The passions, however, are the raw material of virtue, for the virtues exist around and within them. But among the passions, one kind is voluntary, and another is involuntary. Pleasure is the voluntary passion, while pain is the involuntary one. Men who possess the political virtues apply tension and relaxation <note>Modernized from "intension and remission," referring to the tuning or regulation of the passions.</note> to these, harmonizing the other parts of the soul with the part that possesses reason. But the boundary of this

<summary>Theages explains how vice, incontinence, and ferocity arise from the reasoning, appetitive, and irascible parts of the soul, respectively. He also categorizes the virtues into leaders (prudence), followers (fortitude and temperance), and composites (justice), noting that virtue involves harmonizing the passions with reason.</summary>
<keywords>soul, reasoning part, appetitive part, irascible part, vice, incontinence, ferocity, avarice, ambition, prudence, fortitude, temperance, justice, passions, political virtues</keywords>

--- Page 177 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the structure of the soul, moving from the definitions of vice and virtue to the principle of "co-adaptation," which dictates how the rational and irrational parts of the soul—and indeed all aspects of nature—are hierarchically ordered to serve a higher purpose.</meta>

co-adaptation means that the intellect is not prevented from accomplishing its proper work, either by deficiency or by excess. For that which is less excellent is arranged for the sake of that which is more excellent. Thus in the world, every part that is always passive exists for the sake of that which is always moved. And in the joining of animals, the female exists for the sake of the male. For the latter sows, generating a soul; but the former alone imparts matter to that which is generated. In the soul, however, the irrational exists for the sake of the rational part. For anger and desire are arranged in subservience to the first part of the soul; the former as a kind of satellite and guardian of the body, but the latter as a dispenser and provident caretaker of necessary wants. But the intellect, being established at the highest summit of the body and having a view of that which is splendid and transparent on all sides,<sup>1</sup> investigates the wisdom of [real] beings. And this is its work according to nature: namely, having investigated and obtained the possession [of truth], to follow those beings who are more excellent and more honorable than itself. *For the knowledge of things divine and most honorable is the principle, cause, and rule of human blessedness.*

---

<note><sup>1</sup> That is, in the ethereal vehicle of the soul, which is spherical and moves circularly when the soul acts intellectually. This vehicle is also <term>αὐγοειδής</term>, or luciform, transparent throughout, and of a star-like nature. Hence Marcus Antoninus beautifully observes: <term>σφαῖρα ψυχῆς αὐτοειδής</term> (read <term>αὐγοειδής</term>), <term>ὅταν μήτε ἐκτείνηται ἐπί τι, μήτε ἔσω συντρέχῃ μήτε συνιζάνῃ, ἀλλὰ φῶτι λάμπηται, ᾧ τὴν ἀλήθειαν ὁρᾷ τὴν πάντων, καὶ τὴν ἐν αὐτῇ</term>. Book 2. That is, "The sphere of the soul is luciform when the soul is neither extended toward anything [external], nor inwardly collapses with it, nor is depressed by it, but is illuminated with a light by which she sees the truth of all things and the truth that is within herself."</note>

<summary>This text explains the concept of co-adaptation, where lower or passive elements of nature and the soul are arranged to serve higher, rational purposes. It concludes that the intellect’s natural role is to seek divine truth, which is the foundation of human happiness.</summary>
<keywords>co-adaptation, intellect, rational soul, irrational soul, hierarchy of nature, ethereal vehicle, Marcus Antoninus, human blessedness, luciform</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the soul's structure, transitioning from the concept of co-adaptation between its rational and irrational parts to a specific definition of human virtue as the perfection of human nature.</meta>

### FROM
# METOPUS,
### IN HIS TREATISE
## CONCERNING VIRTUE.

***

The virtue of man is the perfection of human nature. For every being becomes perfect and arrives at the summit of excellence according to the proper nature of its virtue. Thus, the virtue of a horse is that which leads the nature of a horse to its peak. The same reasoning applies to the various parts of a thing. Thus, the virtue of the eyes is sharpness of vision, and this is the summit of the nature of the eyes. The virtue of the ears is also sharpness of hearing, and this is the summit of the nature of the ears. Similarly, the virtue of the feet is swiftness, and this is the summit of the nature of the feet. It is necessary, however, that every virtue should possess these three things: reason, power, and deliberate choice. Reason is that by which it judges and contemplates; power is that by which it prohibits and conquers; and deliberate choice is that by which it loves and delights in what is proper. To judge and contemplate, therefore, pertains to the <term>dianoetic</term> (intellectual) part of the soul; but to prohibit and conquer is the specialty of the irrational <note>1. Marcus Meibomius observes that Canter did not see that <term>logistikō</term> (rational) should be written in this place instead of <term>alogō</term> (irrational). Canter, however, was right in retaining <term>alogō</term>. For the dianoetic is the same as the <term>logistic</term> part of the soul; and it is evident that a part of the soul different from the dianoetic is intended to be signified here. Besides, as Aristotle shows in his *Nicomachean Ethics*, when the irrational becomes obedient to the rational part of the soul, the former then prohibits and conquers base appetites in conjunction with the latter.</note> part of the soul.

<summary>Metopus defines virtue as the fulfillment of a thing's nature and identifies the three essential components of virtue as reason, power, and deliberate choice. He assigns these functions to the rational and irrational parts of the human soul, specifically noting how the irrational part must work in harmony with reason to overcome base desires.</summary>

--- Page 179 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the components of the soul and the generation of virtue through the harmony of the rational and irrational parts.</meta>

...and to love and delight in what is proper belong to both the rational and irrational parts. For deliberate choice consists of <term>dianoia</term> <note>or the discursive energy of reason</note> and appetite. <term>Dianoia</term>, therefore, belongs to the rational part, but appetite belongs to the irrational part of the soul. However, the variety of all the virtues may be understood from the parts of the soul, and the generation and nature of virtue may be perceived in a similar manner. For among the parts of the soul, there are two that rank as the primary ones: namely, the rational and the irrational parts. Indeed, the rational part is that by which we judge and contemplate, while the irrational part is that by which we are impelled and feel desire. These, however, are either in agreement or in conflict with each other. But the conflict and dissonance between them are produced through excess and deficiency. It is evident, therefore, that when the rational part vanquishes the irrational part of the soul, endurance and continence are produced; and when the former leads and the latter follows, and both agree with each other, then virtue is generated. Hence, endurance and continence are generated accompanied by pain; but endurance resists pain, and continence resists pleasure. Incontinence, however, and effeminacy neither resist nor conquer <note>pleasure</note>. And because of this, it happens that men flee from what is good due to pain, but reject it due to pleasure. Praise, likewise, and blame, and everything beautiful in human conduct are produced in these parts of the soul. In short, the nature of virtue derives its existence in this manner.

The types, however, and the parts of virtue may be examined as follows: Since there are two parts of the soul, the rational and the irrational, the latter is divided into the <term>irascible</term> and <term>appetitive</term>. And the rational part, indeed, is that by which we judge and contemplate; but the irrational part is that by which we are impelled and feel desire. And within this, that part which is, as it were, adapted to defend us and avenge inci-

It is evident, therefore, that the multitude of the virtues, their differences, and their unique characteristics follow in accordance with these parts of the soul.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of virtue, transitioning from the psychological components of the soul to the specific causes and types of virtue, as well as the external influences that guide moral behavior.</meta>

# FROM CLINIAS.

---

EVERY virtue is perfected, as was shown by us in the beginning, by reason, deliberate choice, and power. Each of these, however, is not by itself a part of virtue, but the cause of it. Therefore, those who have the intellectual and cognitive part of virtue <note>That is, those who possess the theoretical virtues.</note> are called skillful and intelligent; but those who have the ethical and pre-elective part of it <note>That is, those who possess the ethical and political virtues.</note> are called useful and fair. However, since man is naturally inclined to act unjustly due to certain provocative causes—and these are three: the love of pleasure in bodily enjoyments, greed in the accumulation of wealth, and ambition in surpassing those who are equal and similar to him—this being the case, it is necessary to know that it is possible to oppose these with things that produce fear, shame, and desire in men; namely, fear through the laws, shame through the Gods, and desire through the activities of reason. Therefore, it is necessary that youth should be taught from the start to honor the Gods and the laws. For from these, it will be clear that every human work, and every kind of human life, through the participation in holiness and piety, will sail prosperously <note>over the sea of generation</note>.

<summary>Clinias explains that virtue arises from reason, choice, and power, and identifies the three main causes of injustice—pleasure, greed, and ambition—which must be countered by law, divinity, and reason. He emphasizes the importance of teaching youth to honor the Gods and the law to ensure a prosperous and pious life.</summary>

<keywords>Clinias, virtue, reason, deliberate choice, intellectual virtue, ethical virtue, injustice, pleasure, greed, ambition, law, Gods, piety</keywords>

--- Page 182 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the principles of virtue, moving from the general causes mentioned by Clinias to a specific psychological analysis by Theages concerning the tripartite nature of the soul and its harmonious function.</meta>

### FROM
# THEAGES,
### IN HIS TREATISE
## ON THE VIRTUES.

---

THE principles of all virtue are three: knowledge, power, and deliberate choice. Knowledge, indeed, is that by which we contemplate and form a judgment of things; power is, as it were, a certain strength of nature¹ from which we derive our existence, and is that which gives stability to our actions; and deliberate choice is, as it were, the hands of the soul by which we are impelled toward and take hold of the objects of our choice. The order of the soul also exists as follows: One part of it is the reasoning power, another part is anger, and another is desire. And the reasoning power, indeed, is that which has dominion over knowledge; anger is that which rules over the spirited impulses of the soul; and desire is that which willingly rules over appetite. Therefore, when these three merge into one, so as to exhibit a single coordination, then virtue and concord are produced in the soul; but when they are rebellious and separated from each other, then vice and discord are generated in the soul.

<note>
¹ The original is, ἁ δὲ δύναμις, οἷον ἀλκά τις τω σκάνεος ᾧ ὑφιστάμεθα, καὶ ἐμμένομες τοις πράγμασιν. This sentence in its present state is certainly unintelligible. For *skaneos* [body/tent], therefore, I read *physeos* [nature], and then the meaning will be as in the translation above. The version by Canter is certainly absurd; for it is “Ability, as if strength and causes, by which we endure, and remain in things.” And Gale, as usual, takes no notice of the absurdity.
</note>

<summary>Theages defines the three principles of virtue as knowledge, power, and deliberate choice, which correspond to the reasoning, spirited, and appetitive parts of the soul. He concludes that virtue arises from the harmonious cooperation of these three parts, while vice results from their internal conflict.</summary>

<keywords>Theages, On the Virtues, knowledge, power, deliberate choice, reasoning power, anger, desire, concord, discord, Canter, Gale, soul, Greek philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 183 ---
<meta>This page continues Theages' analysis of the soul's internal order, moving from the definition of the three parts of the soul—reasoning, anger, and desire—to how their interactions produce specific virtues or vices.</meta>

When the reasoning power prevails over the irrational parts of the soul, then endurance and continence are produced; endurance, indeed, in the bearing of pain, but continence in the abstinence from pleasures. But when the irrational parts of the soul prevail over the reasoning power, then effeminacy and incontinence are produced; effeminacy, indeed, in fleeing from pain, but incontinence in being vanquished by pleasures.

When, however, the better part of the soul governs and the less excellent part is governed—and the former leads while the latter follows, and both consent and are in harmony with each other—then virtue and every good are generated in the whole soul. Likewise, when the appetitive part follows the reasoning part of the soul, then temperance is produced; but when this is the case with the irascible part, fortitude is produced; and when it takes place in all parts of the soul, then justice is the result. For justice is that which distinguishes all the vices and all the virtues of the soul from each other. And justice is a certain established order of the fitting conjunction of the parts of the soul, and is perfect and supreme virtue. For every good is contained in this, and the other goods of the soul cannot exist without it. Hence justice possesses great strength among both Gods and men. For this virtue contains the bond by which the whole and the universe are held together, and also by which Gods and men are connected. Justice, therefore, is said to be Themis among the celestial Gods, but Dice among the terrestrial Gods, and Law among men. These assertions, however, are indications and symbols that justice is the supreme virtue. Hence virtue, when it consists in contemplating and judging, is called prudence; when in enduring things of a fearful nature, it is called fortitude; when in restraining pleasure, temperance; and when in abstaining from greed and from injuring our neighbors, justice.

Moreover, the arrangement of virtue according to

<summary>Theages explains that virtue arises when the rational part of the soul governs the irrational parts, leading to qualities like endurance, temperance, and fortitude. --- Page 184 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the soul's internal order, specifically how the alignment with "right reason" leads to virtue, which is defined as a harmonious state of the "decorous" falling between excess and deficiency.</meta>

Right reason, and the violation of it contrary to right reason, produce <note>in the former case</note> a tendency toward the decorous as the final goal, and <note>in the latter</note> the failure to reach it. The decorous, however, is that which ought to be. But this does not require either addition or removal, since it is exactly what it is necessary to be. But of the indecorous there are two types; one of which is excess, and the other deficiency. And excess indeed is more, but deficiency is less, than what is decorous. Virtue is also a certain habit of the decorous. Therefore, it is directly both a peak and a middle ground. For in this way, things that are decorous are both means and peaks. They are means, indeed, because they fall between excess and deficiency; but they are peaks because they do not require either addition or removal. For they are the very things themselves which they ought to be.

Since, however, the virtue of character is concerned with the passions, and of the passions, pleasure and pain are supreme, it is evident that virtue does not consist in rooting out the passions of the soul—pleasure and pain—but in harmonizing them together. For health, which is a certain appropriate mixture of the powers of the body, does not consist in expelling the cold and the hot, or the moist and the dry; but in these being <note>appropriately</note> mingled together. For health is, as it were, a certain symmetry of these qualities. Likewise in music, harmony does not consist in removing the sharp and the flat; but when these are harmonized together, then concord is produced and dissonance is eliminated. In a similar way, when the hot and the cold, and the moist and the dry are harmoniously mingled together, health is produced and disease is destroyed. But when anger and desire are harmonized together, the vices and the <note>other</note> passions are rooted out, and the virtues and character are produced. However, deliberate choice in noble conduct is the most significant characteristic of the virtue of character. For it is possible to use reason and power without virtue;

<summary>This page defines virtue as the "decorous" state—a perfect middle ground and peak that avoids both excess and deficiency. It argues that virtue is not the destruction of passions like pleasure and pain, but rather their harmonious integration, comparable to health in the body or concord in music.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of virtue and the soul, specifically addressing how the reasoning power interacts with the irrational parts—anger and desire—to produce either continence or incontinence.</meta>

but it is not possible to use deliberate choice without it. For deliberate choice indicates the dignity of manners. Hence also, when the reasoning power subdues anger and desire by force, it produces <term>continence</term> and endurance. And again, when the reasoning power is violently dethroned by the irrational parts, then <term>incontinence</term> and effeminacy are produced. Such dispositions of the soul as these, however, are half-perfect virtues and half-perfect vices. For the reasoning power of the soul is <note>according to its natural existence</note> in a healthy state, but the irrational parts are in a diseased condition. And so far indeed as anger and desire are governed and led by the rational part of the soul, continence and endurance become virtues; but so far as this is achieved by force, and not voluntarily, they become vices. For it is necessary that virtue should perform such things as are appropriate, not with pain, but with pleasure. Again, so far as anger and desire govern the reasoning power, effeminacy and incontinence are produced, which are certain vices. But so far as they gratify the passions with pain, knowing that they are wrong because the eye of the soul is healthy—so far as this is the case, they are not vices. Hence, it is evident that virtue must necessarily perform what is appropriate voluntarily; that which is involuntary, indeed, is not without pain and fear; and that which is voluntary does not exist without pleasure and delight.

By division also, it will simultaneously be found that this is the case. For knowledge and the perception of things are the province of the rational part of the soul; but power pertains to the irrational part. For the inability to resist pain, or to conquer pleasure, is the characteristic of the irrational part of the soul. But deliberate choice exists in both of these—namely, in the rational and also in the irrational part. Universally, therefore, virtue is a certain harmonization of the irrational parts of the soul with the rational part. Virtue, however, is produced through pleasure and pain receiving the limit of what is appropriate. For true virtue is nothing other than the habit of that which is appropriate. But the appropriate, or the decorous, is that which ought to be; and the inappropriate, or indecorous, is that which ought not to be. Of the indecorous, however, there are two types, namely: excess and deficiency. And excess, indeed, is more than is appropriate; but deficiency is less than is appropriate. But since the appropriate is that which ought to be, it is both a peak and a middle. It is a peak, indeed, because it requires neither subtraction nor addition; but it is a middle because it exists between excess and deficiency. The appropriate and the inappropriate, however, are related to each other as the equal and the unequal, or as that which is orderly and that which is without order; and both the two former and the two latter are finite and infinite.<note>Namely: the equal and that which is orderly belong to the order of limit, while the unequal and that which is without order belong to the order of infinity. And limit and infinity are the two great principles of things, following the ineffable cause of all. See the third book of my translation of Proclus, *On the Theology of Plato*.</note> On this account, the parts of the unequal are compared to the middle, but not to each other. For an angle is called obtuse if it is greater than a right angle; but it is called acute if it is less than a right angle. Diseases of the body are likewise generated when the body becomes hotter or colder <insert>than is proper</insert>. For that which is hotter <insert>than is appropriate</insert> exceeds moderation; and that which is colder

<meta>This page continues the discussion on virtue as the "mean" or middle state between excess and deficiency, focusing on the harmonization of the soul's passions.</meta>

<note>than is appropriate</note> is below mediocrity. The soul also, and such things as pertain to it, have this disposition and analogy. For audacity, indeed, is an excess of the decorous in the endurance of things of a fearful nature; but timidity is a deficiency of the decorous. And prodigality is an excess of what is appropriate in the expenditure of money; but illiberality is a deficiency in this. And rage, indeed, is an excess of the decorous in the impulse of the irascible part of the soul; but insensibility is a deficiency of this. The same reasoning also applies to the opposition of the other dispositions of the soul. 

It is necessary, however, that virtue—since it is a habit of the decorous and a middle state of the passions—should neither be <note>wholly</note> impassive, nor immoderately passive. For impassivity, indeed, causes the soul to be unimpelled and to be without an enthusiastic tendency toward the beautiful in conduct; but immoderate passivity causes it to be full of perturbation and inconsiderate. It is necessary, therefore, that passion should present itself within virtue just as shadow and outline do in a picture. For the animated and the delicate, and that which imitates the truth, in conjunction with the excellence of colors, are especially achieved in a picture through these <note>that is, through shadow and outline</note>. But the passions of the soul are animated by the natural incitement and enthusiasm of virtue. For virtue is generated from the passions, and when generated, again exists together with them; just as that which is well-harmonized consists of the sharp and the flat, that which is well-mingled consists of the hot and the cold, and that which is in equilibrium derives its equality of weight from the heavy and the light. It is not, therefore, necessary to take away the passions of the soul, for that would not be profitable; but it is required that they should be co-harmonized with the rational part, in conjunction with fitness and mediocrity.

It argues that passions should be harmonized with reason rather than eliminated, as they provide the necessary "color" and energy for virtuous conduct.</summary>

### FROM
### THE TREATISE OF
# ARCHYTAS
# ON ETHICAL ERUDITION.

---

I SAY that virtue will be found sufficient for the avoidance of misery, and vice for the failure to attain happiness, if we judiciously consider the habits <note>by which these are produced</note>. For it is necessary that the bad man should always be miserable; whether he is in wealth, for he employs it badly; or whether he is in poverty; just as the blind man, whether he has light and the most splendid visible object before him, or whether he is in the dark <note>is always necessarily without sight</note>. But the good man is not always happy; for happiness does not consist in the possession, but in the use of virtue. For neither does he who has sight always see; for he will not see, if he is without light. Life, however, is divided into two paths; one of which is more arduous, and in which the patient Ulysses walked; but the other is more free from trouble, and is that in which Nestor proceeded. I say therefore that virtue desires the latter, but is able to proceed in the former of these paths. The nature however of happiness proclaims it to be a desirable and stable life, because it gives perfection to the judgment of the soul. Hence the virtuous man who does not obtain such a life as this, is not indeed happy, nor yet entirely miserable. No one therefore will dare to say that the good man should be free from disease, and pain, and sorrow. For as we leave certain painful things to the

<summary>Archytas argues that while vice inevitably leads to misery, virtue requires both possession and active use within a stable life to achieve true happiness. He uses the examples of Ulysses and Nestor to illustrate that virtue can navigate both difficult and peaceful paths, though it prefers the latter.</summary>
<keywords>Archytas, virtue, vice, misery, happiness, Ulysses, Nestor, ethical erudition, soul's judgment, moral habits</keywords>

--- Page 189 ---
<meta>This page continues Archytas's discussion on the relationship between virtue, the soul, and the passions, specifically arguing for a middle path (moderation) rather than the Stoic ideal of total apathy.</meta>

body, so likewise we must permit them to be present with the soul. The sorrows of fools, however, are most irrational; but those of wise men proceed only as far as reason, which limits all things, permits. Moreover, the boast of apathy dissolves the generosity of virtue when it opposes itself to things of an indifferent nature, rather than to actual evils such as death, pain, and poverty. For things which are not truly evils are easily conquered. We should therefore practice moderation in our passions, as we shall then equally avoid insensibility and excessive passivity, and shall not speak more highly of our nature than we ought.

### FROM

# ARCHYTAS,

### IN HIS TREATISE ON

## THE GOOD AND HAPPY MAN.

---

I say, then, that the good man is one who uses great things and opportunities in a beautiful manner. He is also able to bear both prosperity and adversity well. In beautiful and honorable circumstances, he also becomes worthy of the condition in which he is placed; and when his fortune changes, he receives it in a proper manner. In short, on all occasions, he contends well with whatever contingencies may arise. Nor does he only prepare himself in this way [for whatever may happen], but also those who trust in him and strive alongside him.

<summary>Archytas defines the good man as one who uses opportunities beautifully and maintains his character through both prosperity and adversity. He notes that such a person not only manages his own life well but also prepares those who follow him to do the same.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the preceding excerpt by Archytas to a new treatise by Crito. While the previous page defined the character of the "good man" who can withstand changes in fortune, Crito now examines the philosophical relationship between prudence (reason and order) and prosperity (chance and the indefinite).</meta>

### FROM
# CRITO,
### IN HIS TREATISE ON
## PRUDENCE AND PROSPERITY.

---

**Prudence** and prosperity exist, in relation to each other, as follows: Prudence indeed is expressible and possesses reason; for it is something orderly and definite. But prosperity is inexpressible and irrational; for it is something disorderly and indefinite. And prudence, indeed, is primary, but prosperity is secondary in its beginning and in power. For the former is naturally suited to govern and define; but the latter to be governed and defined. Moreover, both prudence and prosperity undergo mutual adaptation, since they come together in one and the same thing. For it is always necessary that the thing which limits and arranges should have a nature that is expressible and participates in reason; but that the thing which is limited and arranged should be naturally inexpressible and irrational. For the principle of the nature of the infinite and of that which limits exists in all things in this way. For infinites are always naturally inclined to be limited and arranged by things which possess reason and prudence, since the former have the order of matter and essence in relation to the latter. But finites are arranged and limited by themselves, since they have the order of a cause and of that which is active.

The mutual adaptation, however, of these natures in different things produces a great and varied difference of adapted substances. For in the comprehension of the whole of things, the mutual adaptation of

<vocab>Crito, prudence, prosperity, expressible, inexpressible, mutual adaptation, infinite, finite, active</vocab>

<summary>Crito defines prudence as a rational, governing principle and prosperity as an irrational, indefinite force that must be shaped by reason. He explains that these two forces adapt to one another, with prudence acting as the defining cause and prosperity acting as the underlying matter.</summary>
<keywords>Crito, prudence, prosperity, reason, order, the infinite, the finite, metaphysics, causation</keywords>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues Crito’s treatise on Prudence and Prosperity, further detailing how the "co-adaptation" of active and passive principles functions within the universe, the human soul, the state, and the family.</meta>

both natures—that is, the nature which is always in motion and the nature which is always passive—make up the world. For it is not possible for the whole and the universe to be preserved in any other way than by that which is created being co-adapted to that which is divine, and that which is always passive to that which is always in motion.¹ In man, likewise, the co-adaptation of the irrational part of the soul to the rational part is virtue. For it is not possible in these cases, when there is conflict between both parts, for virtue to exist. In a city as well, the co-adaptation of the governors to the governed produces strength and harmony. For governing is the characteristic of the superior nature, while being governed is easier for the subordinate nature <note>rather than for the superior nature</note>. And strength and harmony are common to both.

There is, however, the same mode of adaptation in the universe as in a family; for charms² and education concur with reason in one and the same thing, as do pains and pleasures, prosperity and adversity. For the life of man requires tension and relaxation, sorrow and gladness, prosperity and adversity. For some things are able to focus and maintain the intellect toward industry and wisdom, while others provide relaxation and delight, and thus make the intellect vigorous and ready for action. If, however, one of these prevails in life, then the life of man becomes one-sided and leans toward one extreme, tending either toward sorrow and difficulty or toward relaxation and triviality. But the co-adaptation of all these things ought to exist with reference to prudence. For prudence separates and distinguishes³ the limit and the infinite in actions. Hence
