Prudence is the leader and mother of the other virtues. For all of them are harmonized and arranged together with reference to the reason and law of this virtue. And now my discussion of this subject is finished. For the irrational and the expressible are in all things. And the latter defines and bounds; but the former is defined and bounded. That, however, which consists of both of these is the proper composition of the whole and the universe.

The following beautiful fragment of **CRITO** on Prudence is from the *Physical Eclogues* of Stobæus, p. 198, and is omitted by Gale in his collection of *Pythagoric Ethical Fragments* in *Opusc. Mythol.* etc.

> God fashioned man in such a way as to make it clear that he is not—through a lack of power or deliberate choice—incapable of being driven toward what is beautiful in conduct. For he implanted in him a principle of such a kind as to understand at one and the same time what is possible and what is preferred; so that man might be the cause of power and the possession of good, but God the cause of impulse and encouragement according to right reason. For this reason also, he made him tend toward heaven, gave him an intellectual power, and implanted in him a vision called intellect, which is capable of beholding God. For it is not possible without God to discover that which is best and most beautiful, nor without intellect to see God, since every mortal nature is established in conjunction with a related lack of intellect. This, however, is not given to it by God, but by the essence of generation and by that impulse of the soul which lacks deliberate choice.

<note>He proposes to read αὐτὰ γὰρ ᾇδε ἐνεργοῖσα, which still leaves the sentence obscure. But if for διενεργοῦσα we read διοριζοῦσα as in the above translation, the meaning is clear.</note>

<summary>Crito concludes his argument that prudence is the foundational virtue that harmonizes all others and governs the relationship between the irrational and the expressible. He further asserts that God endowed humanity with intellect and right reason to enable the pursuit of beauty and the contemplation of the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Prudence, Crito, Stobæus, God, Intellect, Right Reason, Virtue, Ethics, Human Nature, Pythagorean philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page marks a transition from the fragments of Crito on prudence to a fragment from Archytas's treatise on the qualities of a good and happy man.</meta>

### FROM

# ARCHYTAS,

### IN HIS TREATISE ON

## THE GOOD AND HAPPY MAN.

---

The prudent <note>that is, the wise</note> man will especially become so as follows: In the first place, being naturally sagacious, possessing a good memory, and being a lover of labor, he should exercise his <term>dianoetic</term> power immediately from his youth in reasonings and disciplines, and in accurate theories, and adhere to genuine philosophy. But after this, he should acquire knowledge and experience in what pertains to the Gods, the laws, and human lives. For there are two things from which the disposition of prudence is produced: one of which consists in obtaining a mathematical and <term>gnostic</term> habit; while the other consists in a man perceiving many theorems and things for himself, and understanding other things through a certain different mode. For neither is he sufficient to the possession of prudence who has exercised his <term>dianoetic</term> power in reasonings and disciplines immediately from his youth; nor is he who, being destitute of these, has merely heard and been familiar with a multitude of things. For the latter will have his <term>dianoetic</term> power blind through judging only of particulars; and the former will be blind through always surveying only universals. For just as in computations the amount of the whole is obtained by the addition of the parts, so also in reality, reason is able to outline the theory of universals; but experience has the power of forming a judgment of particulars.

<vocab>Archytas, dianoetic power, gnostic habit, universals, particulars, prudence, philosophy</vocab>

<summary>Archytas explains that true prudence requires a balance of theoretical reasoning and practical experience, combining the study of universal principles with the observation of specific details. He argues that education in philosophy and mathematics must be joined with an understanding of divinity, law, and human life to achieve wisdom.</summary>
<keywords>Archytas, Prudence, Dianoetic power, Universals vs Particulars, Gnostic habit, Philosophy, Education, Wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 195 ---
<meta>This page marks a transition to a fragment from Archytas's treatise "On Disciplines," continuing the collection of Pythagorean ethical and philosophical fragments.</meta>

### FROM
# ARCHYTAS,
### IN HIS TREATISE
## ON DISCIPLINES.

---

It is necessary that you should become scientific, either by learning from another person or by discovering for yourself the things of which you have scientific knowledge. If, therefore, you learn from another person, what you learn is foreign; but what you discover yourself is through your own effort and is your own. Moreover, if you investigate, discovery will be easy and soon achieved; but if you do not know how to investigate, discovery will be impossible for you. And <note>right</note> reasoning, indeed, when discovered, causes discord to cease and increases concord. For through this, the insatiable desire for possession is suppressed and equality prevails, since by this we obtain what is just in contracts. Hence, because of this, the poor receive from those who are able to give, and the rich give to those who are in need—both of them believing that through this they shall achieve equality. This, however, will be a rule and a hindrance to those who act unjustly: namely, that men who possess scientific knowledge will appease their anger prior to the commission of an injury, being persuaded that the perpetrators of it will not be hidden when it is committed; but those who do not possess scientific knowledge, being exposed in the commission of an injury, will be restrained from acting unjustly.

<vocab>Archytas, scientific knowledge, reasoning, sedition, concord, equality, contracts, injury</vocab>

<summary>Archytas discusses how scientific knowledge is acquired through learning or personal discovery and how right reasoning fosters social harmony. He argues that such reasoning ensures justice in contracts and discourages injustice by making the consequences of harmful actions clear.</summary>
<keywords>Archytas, scientific knowledge, investigation, reasoning, social concord, economic equality, justice, prevention of injury</keywords>

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the ethical reasoning of Archytas to a fragment from the philosopher Polus, specifically his thoughts on the universal nature of justice.</meta>

### FROM
# POLUS,
### IN HIS TREATISE
# ON JUSTICE.

---

It seems to me that the justice which exists among men may be called the mother and the nurse of the other virtues. For without this, a man can be neither temperate, nor brave, nor prudent. For it is the harmony and peace of the whole soul, combined with elegance. The strength of this virtue, however, will become more obvious if we direct our attention to other habits. For they have a limited utility directed toward a single thing; but justice is directed toward whole systems and toward the many. 

In the universe, therefore, it conducts the whole government of things, and is providence, harmony, and Dice <note>Dike, or Justice personified</note> by the decree of a certain class of Gods. But in a city, it is rightly called peace and fair legislation. In a household, it is the concord between husband and wife, the goodwill of the servant toward the master, and the master's concern for the welfare of the servant. In the body likewise—which is the first and dearest thing to all animals <note>as far as they are animals</note>—it is health and the wholeness of all the parts. But in the soul, it is the wisdom which, among men, arises from knowledge and justice. 

If, therefore, this virtue thus disciplines and preserves both the whole and the parts <note>of everything</note>, making things harmonious and familiar with each other, how is it possible that it should not be called, by the consensus of all men, the mother and the nurse of all things?

<summary>Polus describes justice as the fundamental virtue that sustains all others, acting as a unifying force across the universe, the state, the home, the body, and the soul. He argues that justice is responsible for harmony and health in every aspect of existence, from divine providence to human relationships.</summary>
<keywords>Polus, justice, virtue, harmony, soul, providence, Dike, legislation, concord, health</keywords>

The following fragments also, from the *Treatise of Archytas on Wisdom*, are preserved by Iamblichus in the third chapter of his *Protreptics*, or *Exhortations to Philosophy*.

“Archytas, therefore, at the beginning of his *Treatise on Wisdom*, encourages the possession of it as follows:

   1. “Wisdom excels in all human affairs as much as sight excels the other physical senses, as intellect excels the soul, and as the sun excels the stars. For sight is the most far-reaching and the most multifaceted of all the senses; intellect is the highest part of the soul, judging through reason and <term>dianoia</term> what is appropriate, and existing as the vision and power of the most honorable things; and the sun is the eye and soul of things that have a natural existence. For through the sun, all things become visible, are produced, and come into being.<note>1. For νοῆται in this place, I read φύεται.</note> Drawing their roots and being produced from there, they are nourished, increased, and stimulated by it in conjunction with sense.

   2. “Man was created by far the wisest of all terrestrial animals. For he is able to contemplate the things that exist and to obtain knowledge and wisdom from all things. To this it may also be added that divinity has engraved and displayed in him the system of universal reason, in which all the forms of existing things are distributed, along with the meanings of nouns and verbs. For a place is assigned for the sounds of the voice—namely the <term>pharynx</term>, the mouth, and the nostrils. But just as man was created as the instrument of sounds through which nouns and verbs are expressed, so he was also created for the concepts that are perceived in existing things. And this appears to me to be the work of wisdom, for the accomplishment of which man was created and formed, and received organs and powers from divinity.

<summary>Archytas describes wisdom as the supreme human faculty, comparing its importance to that of sight, the intellect, and the sun. He argues that humans were uniquely designed by divinity with the capacity for reason, language, and the understanding of universal truths.</summary>

...purpose of contemplating the reason of the whole of nature, and in order that, being himself the work of wisdom, he might survey the wisdom of the things which exist. For if the reason of man is contemplative of the reason of the whole of nature, and the wisdom of man also perceives and contemplates the wisdom of the things in existence—this being acknowledged, it is at the same time demonstrated that man is a part of universal reason and of the whole intellectual nature.

4. “Wisdom is not concerned with a specific definite existing thing, but is simply concerned with all things that exist. And it is necessary that it should not first investigate its own principles, but the common principles of all beings. For wisdom exists in such a way in relation to all beings that its role is to know and contemplate the universal accidents of all things. On this account, wisdom discovers the principles of all beings.

5. “Whoever, therefore, is able to analyze all the <term>genera</term> which are contained under one and the same principle, and again to compose and enumerate them, he appears to me to be the wisest of men and to possess the most perfect truth. Furthermore, he will also have discovered a beautiful vantage point from which it will be possible to behold divinity and all things that are in coordination with and successive to him, existing separately or distinct from each other.<note>¹ Neither of the Latin translators, North and Arcerius, understood this passage, and they have therefore translated it erroneously. North translates this as: “And all things in the series and order of things separated from it.” But Arcerius: “And all things which are in the kinship and order of nature separated from it.” However, by the things coordinate with and successive to God, Archytas means the other Gods who, though subordinate to the supreme, yet in consequence of partaking of the same nature, are said to be coordinate with him. Gale, likewise, did not perceive the error of the Latin translators.</note> Having likewise entered this most ample road,

# PYTHAGOREAN ETHICAL SENTENCES

->FROM<-

## STOBÆUS,

->*Which are omitted in the Opuscula Mythologica, etc.*<-
->*of Gale.*<-

---

Do not even *think* of doing what ought not to be done.
Choose rather to be strong in soul than in body.
Be convinced that things of a laborious nature contribute more to virtue than pleasures do.
Every passion of the soul is most hostile to its salvation.
It is difficult to walk in many paths of life at one and the same time.<note>1</note>
Pythagoras said it is necessary to choose the most excellent life, for habit will make it pleasant. Wealth is a weak anchor, and glory is even weaker; the same is true of the body, power, and honor. For all these are feeble and powerless. What, then, are powerful anchors? Prudence, magnanimity, and fortitude. No storm can shake these. This is the law of God: that virtue is the only thing that is strong, and that everything else is a trifle.
All parts of human life, just like those of a statue, ought to be beautiful.
A statue, indeed, stands on its base, but a worthy man, in the matter of his deliberate choice, ought to be immovable.

<note>1 The sentences above are from *Stobæi Sententiæ*, p. 3 (the 1609 edition), and are attributed to Pythagoras.</note>

<summary>This page presents a collection of ethical maxims attributed to Pythagoras, emphasizing the strength of the soul, the necessity of virtue, and the instability of worldly things like wealth and glory.</summary>

Frankincense should be given to the Gods, but praise to good men.

It is necessary to defend those who are unjustly accused of having acted harmfully, but to praise those who excel in some form of goodness.

A horse will not be judged noble because it is sumptuously adorned, but because its nature is illustrious; nor is a man worthy because he possesses great wealth, but because his soul is noble.

When the wise man opens his mouth, the beauties of his soul reveal themselves to view, like the statues in a temple.1

Remind yourself that everyone claims wisdom is the greatest good, but that there are few who strenuously endeavor to obtain this greatest good.2

->Pythagoras.<-

Be sober, and remember to be inclined to believe; for these are the nerves of wisdom. Epicharmus.

It is better to live lying on the grass, trusting in divinity and yourself, than to lie on a golden bed with anxiety.

You will not lack anything that is in the power of Fortune to give and take away.3

Despise all those things which you will not need when you are freed from the body; and by training yourself in those things which you will need when freed from the body, call upon the Gods to be your helpers.4

It is no more possible to conceal fire in a garment than it is to hide a shameful departure from righteousness over time.

<meta>This page continues the collection of ethical and philosophical maxims, specifically focusing on the nature of desire and the importance of admitting errors, drawing from the works of Aristoxenus and Pythagoras as recorded by Stobæus.</meta>

Wind indeed increases fire, but custom increases love.¹
Only those who are hostile to injustice are dear to divinity.²
Those things which the body necessarily requires are easily obtained by all men without labor or trouble; but those things for which labor and trouble are required to obtain are objects of desire not for the body, but for depraved opinion. *Aristoxenus the Pythagorean. Stobæus, p. 132.*
Concerning desire also, he [*i.e.* Pythagoras] said the following: This passion is varied, laborious, and takes many forms. Of desires, however, some are acquired and external, while others are innate. He defined desire itself as a certain tendency and impulse of the soul, and an appetite for a fullness or presence of sense, or for an emptiness and absence of it, or for non-perception. He also said that there are three most well-known types of erroneous and depraved desire: namely, the indecorous, the disproportionate, and the unseasonable. For desire is either immediately indecorous, troublesome, and ignoble; or it is not absolutely so, but is more intense and lasting than is appropriate. Or, in the third place, it is driven when it is not proper, and toward objects to which it ought not to tend. *From the Sentences of Aristoxenus the Pythagorean. Stobæus, p. 132.*
Do not endeavor to conceal your errors with words, but to remedy them with reproof. *Pythagoras. Stobæus, p. 146.*
It is not as difficult to err as it is to fail to reprove one who errs. *Pythagoras. Stobæus, p. 147.*
Just as a bodily disease cannot be healed if it is concealed or praised, so too can no remedy be applied to a diseased soul that is poorly guarded and protected. *Pythagoras. Stobæus, p. 147.*

<meta>This page transitions from general Pythagorean and Platonic ethical maxims to a specific set of sentences attributed to Sextus the Pythagorean, focusing on the nature of divinity and the relationship between the human soul and God.</meta>

# SELECT SENTENCES

->OF<-

# SEXTUS THE PYTHAGOREAN.

---

To neglect things of the smallest consequence is not the least significant matter in human life.
The wise man, and the despiser of wealth, resembles God.
Do not investigate the name of God, because you will not find it. For everything that is called by a name receives its designation from that which is more worthy than itself,1 so that it is one person who calls and another who hears. Who is it, therefore, that has given a name to God? God, however, is not a name for God, but an indication of what we conceive of him.
God is a light incapable of receiving its contrary <note>darkness</note>.
You have in yourself something similar to God; therefore, use yourself as the temple of God, because of that within you which resembles God.
Honor God above all things, so that he may rule over you.
Whatever you honor above all things—that which you so honor—will have dominion over you. But if you give yourself to the dominion of God, you will thus have dominion over all things.

<note>1 For as every cause of the existence of a thing is better than that thing, insofar as the one is the cause and the other the effect; so also that which gives a name to anything is better than the thing named, insofar as it is named—that is, insofar as pertains to its possession of a name. For the namer is the cause, and the name is the effect.</note>

<summary>This page contains a series of philosophical maxims attributed to Sextus the Pythagorean regarding the nature of God, the impossibility of naming the divine, and the importance of self-governance through honoring God.</summary>
<keywords>Sextus the Pythagorean, divinity, naming God, temple of God, dominion, philosophy of language</keywords>

--- Page 207 ---
<meta>This page continues the collection of philosophical maxims attributed to Sextus the Pythagorean, moving from the nature and name of God to the practical imitation of the divine through intellect, piety, and conduct.</meta>

The greatest honor that can be paid to God is to know and imitate him.

There is nothing, indeed, that wholly resembles God; nevertheless, the imitation of him as much as possible by an inferior nature is pleasing to him.

God, indeed, does not need anything, but the wise man needs God alone. He, therefore, who needs only a few things, and those necessary, emulates him who needs nothing.

Endeavor to be great in the estimation of divinity, but avoid envy among men.

The wise man whose reputation among men was small while he was living will be renowned when he is dead.

Consider all time in which you do not think of divinity to be lost to you.

A good intellect is the choir of divinity.

A bad intellect is the choir of evil demons.

Honor what is just for the very reason that it is just.

You will not be hidden from divinity when you act unjustly, nor even when you think of acting so.

The foundation of piety is self-control; but the summit of piety is the love of God.

Wish for what is beneficial, and not what is pleasing, to happen to you.

As you wish your neighbor to be to you, so also be to your neighbors.

What God gives you, no one can take away.

Neither do nor even think of what you are not willing for God to know.

Before you do anything, think of God, so that his light may go before your actions.

The soul is illuminated by the recollection of deity.

The use of all animals as food is a matter of indifference, but it is more rational to abstain from them.

God is not the author of any evil.

You should not possess more than what the body requires for its use.
Possess those things which no one can take from you.

The reason within you is the light of your life.

God dwells in the intellect of the wise man.
Every desire is insatiable and is therefore always in want.
The wise man is always consistent with himself.
The knowledge and imitation of the divine are alone sufficient for <term>beatitude</term>.

He who treats mankind badly treats himself badly.

Wish that you may be able to benefit your enemies.

Do not be willing to admit accusations against a man who is dedicated to the study of wisdom.

Every cup that quenches thirst should be sweet to you.

Flee from intoxication as you would from insanity.

No good originates from the body.

# PYTHAGOREAN SENTENCES,

### FROM THE

## PROTREPTICS OF IAMBLICHUS.<note>Several of these sentences, as published by Arcerius, are in a very defective state; but, as the learned reader will perceive, I have endeavored to amend them in my translation.</note>

---

Since we live through the soul, it must be said that by its virtue we live well; just as because we see through the eyes, we see well through their virtue.

It must not be thought that gold can be injured by rust, or virtue by baseness.

We should turn to virtue as to an inviolable temple, so that we may not be exposed to any ignoble insolence of soul regarding our communion with and continuation in life.

We should trust in Virtue as in a faithful wife, but trust in Fortune as in an inconstant mistress.

It is better that virtue should be received accompanied by poverty than wealth with violence; and frugality with health than veracity with disease.

An abundance of nourishment is harmful to the body; but the body is preserved when the soul is disposed in a fitting manner.

It is equally dangerous to give a sword to a madman and power to a depraved man.

Just as it is better for a part of the body that contains pus to be burned than to remain in its current state, it is also better for a depraved man to die than to live.

The theorems of philosophy are to be enjoyed as much

<summary>This page presents a series of Pythagorean ethical maxims from Iamblichus, emphasizing that virtue is the essential health of the soul and is more valuable than wealth or power.</summary>

--- Page 214 ---
<meta>This page continues the sequence of Pythagorean maxims from the Protreptics of Iamblichus, following the discussion of the body's preservation and the dangers of depravity.</meta>

as possible, as if they were ambrosia and nectar. For the pleasure arising from them is genuine, incorruptible, and divine. They are also capable of producing magnanimity; and though they cannot make us eternal beings, yet they enable us to obtain a scientific knowledge of eternal natures.

If vigor of sensation is considered by us to be a desirable thing, we should much more strenuously endeavor to obtain prudence; for it is, as it were, the sensitive vigor of the practical intellect which we contain. And as through the former we are not deceived in sensory perceptions, so through the latter we avoid false reasoning in practical affairs.

We shall venerate Divinity in a proper manner, if we render the intellect that is in us pure from all vice, as if from a certain stain.

A temple, indeed, should be adorned with gifts, but the soul with disciplines.

As the lesser mysteries are to be delivered before the greater, so also discipline must precede philosophy.

The fruits of the earth, indeed, are annually imparted, but the fruits of philosophy at every part of the year.

As land is especially to be attended to by him who wishes to obtain from it the most excellent fruit, so also the greatest attention should be paid to the soul, in order that it may produce fruit worthy of its nature.

## ADDITIONAL NOTES

---

P. 37. *Better worth saving than ten thousand corporeal eyes.*

Iamblichus here alludes to what Plato says in the seventh book of his Republic, respecting the mathematical disciplines. For he there says, “that the soul, through these disciplines, has an organ purified and enlightened, which is blinded and buried by studies of another kind—an organ better worth saving than ten thousand eyes, since truth becomes visible through this alone.”

P. 43. *That in which the Sirens subsist.*

> “The divine Plato (says Proclus in his manuscript Scholia on the Cratylus) knew that there are three kinds of Sirens: the celestial, which is under the government of Jupiter; *that which produces generation*, and is under the government of Neptune; and *that which is cathartic*, and is under the government of Pluto. It is common to all these to incline all things through a harmonic motion to their ruling Gods. Hence, when the soul is in the heavens, the Sirens are desirous of uniting it to the divine life which flourishes there. But it is proper that souls living in generation should sail beyond them, like the Homeric Ulysses, so that they may not be allured by generation, of which the sea is an image. And when souls are in Hades, the Sirens are desirous of uniting them through intellectual conceptions to Pluto. So that Plato knew that in the

<summary>This page contains commentary on the spiritual value of mathematical study as a means to purify the soul's intellect and explains Proclus's classification of three types of Sirens—celestial, generative, and cathartic—and their roles in guiding the soul.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Plato, Proclus, Republic, Sirens, Jupiter, Neptune, Pluto, Ulysses, Cratylus, Hades, mathematical disciplines, soul purification</keywords>

--- Page 218 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Sirens in Hades and begins explaining the symbolic meanings behind Pythagorean precepts from Iamblichus's Protreptics.</meta>

kingdom of Hades there are gods, demons, and souls, who dance as it were around Pluto, allured by the Sirens that dwell there." See more concerning the Sirens in my translation of Proclus on *The Theology of Plato*, Book 6.

### P. 44. *That it is necessary to put the shoe on the right foot first.*

This teaching is taken from what forms the 12th Symbol in the *Protreptics* of Iamblichus, and is as follows: "When stretching forth your feet to have your sandals put on, first extend your right foot; but when about to use a foot bath, first extend your left foot." "This Symbol," (says Iamblichus,) "exhorts us to practical prudence, admonishing us to place worthy actions around us as right-handed; but entirely to lay aside and throw away such as are base, as being left-handed."

### P. 44. *That it is not proper to walk in the public ways.*

This is the 5th Symbol in the *Protreptics* of Iamblichus, but it is expressed differently there: for it is, "Turning away from the public ways, walk in unfrequented paths." On which Iamblichus observes: "I think that this Symbol also contributes to the same purpose as the preceding one," <note>"Disbelieve nothing wonderful concerning the gods, nor concerning divine dogmas"</note>. "For this exhorts us to abandon a popular and merely human life, but considers it fit that we should pursue a separate and divine life. It also signifies that it is necessary to look above common opinions, but to greatly esteem those that are private and arcane; and that we should despise merely human delight, but ardently pursue that blissful mode of conduct which

<summary>This page explains the symbolic meanings behind Pythagorean precepts regarding the right and left feet and the avoidance of public roads, interpreting them as lessons in moral prudence and the pursuit of a divine rather than common life.</summary>
<keywords>Hades, Pluto, Sirens, Iamblichus, Protreptics, Pythagorean Symbols, practical prudence, divine life, arcane opinions, moral philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the interpretation of Pythagorean symbols from Iamblichus’s Protreptics, focusing on the pursuit of a divine life over a popular one, the importance of labor, and the rejection of materialistic philosophies.</meta>

adheres to the divine will. It likewise exhorts us to dismiss human manners as popular, and to exchange for these the religious cultivation of the Gods, as transcending a popular life.

**P. 44. *Do not assist a man in laying a burden down.***

In the *Protreptics*, this is the 11th Symbol and is explained by Iamblichus as follows:
> “This Symbol exhorts us to fortitude; for whoever takes up a burden signifies that he undertakes an action of labor and energy; but he who lays one down signifies rest and remission. So the Symbol has the following meaning: Do not become, either for yourself or another, the cause of an indolent and weak mode of conduct; for every useful thing is acquired through labor. But the Pythagoreans celebrate this Symbol as Herculean, naming it after the labors of Hercules. For during his association with men, he frequently returned from fire and everything dreadful, indignantly rejecting indolence. For rectitude of conduct is produced by acting and operating, but not from sluggishness.”

**P. 45. *Do not draw near to a woman for the sake***
***of begetting children, if she has gold.***

In the *Protreptics* of Iamblichus (Symbol 35), this is expressed as follows:
> “Do not draw near to that which has gold, in order to produce children.”

On which Iamblichus observes:
> “The Symbol does not speak here of a woman, but of that sect and philosophy which has much of the corporeal in it and a gravitating tendency downward. For gold is the heaviest of all things in the earth and pursues a tendency toward the center, which is the characteristic of corporeal weight. But the term *to draw near* not only signifies to be

### P. 45. *Do not speak about Pythagorean concerns without light.*

This is the 13th Symbol in the *Protreptics*, and is explained by Iamblichus as follows: “This Symbol exhorts one to possess intellectual prudence. For this is similar to the light of the soul; it gives a boundary to that which is indefinite and leads it, as it were, from darkness into light. It is proper, therefore, to establish intellect as the leader of everything beautiful in life, but especially in Pythagorean dogmas; for these cannot be known without light.”

### P. 45. *Do not wear the image of God in a ring.*

In the *Protreptics*, this is the 24th Symbol; but instead of *wear*, the word used there is *inscribe*. Iamblichus’s explanation of it is as follows: “This Symbol, in accordance with the previous idea, offers the following exhortation: Philosophize, and above all else, consider the Gods as having an incorporeal existence. For this is the primary root of Pythagorean dogmas, from which nearly all of them depend and by which they are strengthened to the very end. Therefore, do not think that the Gods use corporeal forms, or that they are contained by a material subject or by the body as a material bond, like other animals. Instead, the engravings in rings represent the bond that exists through the ring—its physical nature and sensible form—and the appearance, as it were, of some partial animal that becomes visible through the engraving. From such things, we should especially distinguish the nature of the Gods, as they are eternal and intelligible,”

<summary>This page explains two Pythagorean symbols: the requirement of "light" or intellectual prudence to understand philosophy, and the prohibition against representing the divine in physical forms like ring engravings, emphasizing the Gods' incorporeal nature.</summary>

...and always existing according to the same and in a similar manner, as we have particularly, most fully, and scientifically shown in our treatise *Concerning the Gods*.” <note>¹ This work is unfortunately lost.</note>

### P. 45. *Nor is it proper to sacrifice a white cock; for this also is a suppliant, and is sacred to the moon.*

In the *Protreptics*, the 18th Symbol is partly the same as, and partly different from, this. For it is, “Nourish a cock, but do not sacrifice it; for it is sacred to the sun and the moon.” And Iamblichus explains it as follows: “This Symbol advises us to nourish and strengthen the body and not neglect it, which would dissolve and destroy the mighty tokens of the union, connection, sympathy, and agreement of the world. So it exhorts us to engage in the contemplation and philosophy of the universe. For although the truth concerning the universe is naturally hidden and quite difficult to investigate, it must, however, be inquired into and investigated by man at the same time, especially through philosophy. For it is truly impossible to be discovered through any other pursuit. But philosophy, receiving certain sparks and, as it were, *viatica* from nature, excites and expands them into greatness, making them more visible through the disciplines it possesses. Therefore, we should philosophize.”

### P. 45. *It is proper to sacrifice, and to enter temples, barefoot.*

This in the *Protreptics* is the 3rd Symbol, but it is expressed this way by Iamblichus: “Sacrifice and worship barefoot.” Regarding this, Iamblichus observes: “This Symbol signifies that we ought to worship the Gods,

<summary>This page interprets the Pythagorean symbols regarding the preservation of the body as a reflection of cosmic harmony and the ritual requirement of entering temples barefoot to show proper reverence to the Gods.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Iamblichus, Protreptics, Symbol, sacrifice, white cock, sun and moon, philosophy, ritual purity, barefoot</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of Pythagorean symbols from Iamblichus’s *Protreptics*, focusing on the spiritual readiness required for worship and the hierarchy of values in religious practice.</meta>

and acquire a knowledge of them in an orderly and modest manner, and in a way that does not surpass our condition on the earth. It also signifies that in worshipping them and acquiring this knowledge, we should be free from bonds and properly liberated. But the symbol exhorts that sacrifice and worship should be performed not only in the body, but also in the energies of the soul, so that these energies may not be hindered by passions, nor by the weakness of the body, nor by generation, with which we are externally surrounded. But everything pertaining to us should be properly liberated and prepared for the participation of the Gods.”

### P. 57. *Do not enter into a temple negligently, nor, in short, worship carelessly, not even if you should stand at the very doors themselves.*

This in the *Protreptics* is the 2nd Symbol and is explained by Iamblichus as follows: 

> “If like is friendly and allied to like, it is evident that since the Gods have the most primary essence among all things, we ought to make the worship of them a primary object. But he who does this for the sake of anything else gives a secondary rank to that which takes precedence over all things, and subverts the whole order of religious worship and knowledge. Besides, it is not proper to rank illustrious goods in the subordinate condition of human utility, nor to place our concerns as the ultimate goal; rather, things more excellent—whether they be works or conceptions—should be the primary aim, while our own concerns are treated as an appendage.”

### P. 58. *These people, therefore, he ordered not to eat the heart.*

This is the 30th Symbol in the *Protreptics* and is explained by Iamblichus as follows: “This symbol signifies

<summary>This page explains Pythagorean symbols regarding the spiritual liberation required for worship and the necessity of prioritizing divine knowledge over human utility. Iamblichus argues that religious worship must be an end in itself rather than a secondary concern for human benefit.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Iamblichus, Protreptics, Symbolism, Religious Worship, Soul, Divine Essence, Human Utility</keywords>

--- Page 223 ---
<meta>This page continues the interpretation of Pythagorean symbols from Iamblichus’s *Protreptics*, completing the explanation of the 30th symbol regarding the heart and beginning the explanation of the 31st symbol regarding the brain.</meta>

that it is not proper to tear apart the union and harmony of the universe. And still further, it signifies this: Do not be envious, but philanthropic and willing to share; and through this, it encourages us to practice philosophy. For philosophy alone among the sciences and arts is neither pained by the good fortune of others nor rejoices in the evils of neighbors, as these are related and familiar by nature, subject to the same passions, and exposed to one common fate. It likewise demonstrates that the future is equally unpredictable for all men. Hence, it encourages us to sympathy and mutual love, and to be truly willing to share, as is fitting for rational beings.”

### P. 58. *Nor the brain.*

This is the 31st Symbol in the *Protreptics*, which Iamblichus explains as follows:
> “This Symbol also resembles the former: for the brain is the governing organ of intellectual wisdom. The Symbol, therefore, hiddenly signifies that we ought not to tear apart or mangle principles and doctrines which have been the objects of careful deliberation. But these will be such as have been the subject of intellectual consideration, thereby becoming equal to objects of a scientific nature. For things of this kind are to be examined, not through the organs of the irrational part of the soul, such as the heart and the liver, but through the pure rational nature. Hence, to tear these apart through contradiction is thoughtless folly; instead, the Symbol encourages us to respect the source of intelligence and the most direct organ of intellectual perception, through which we shall possess contemplation, knowledge, and wisdom; and by which we shall truly practice philosophy, and neither confuse nor hide the traces that philosophy produces.”

<vocab>Iamblichus, Protreptics, philosophy, brain, intellectual wisdom, rational nature, soul, Symbol</vocab>

<summary>This page concludes the explanation of the Pythagorean symbol against eating the heart, emphasizing universal harmony and philanthropy, and introduces the symbol against eating the brain to stress the preservation of rational thought and intellectual doctrines.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Protreptics, Pythagorean symbols, philosophy, intellectual wisdom, rational nature, philanthropy, universal harmony</keywords>

### P. 58. *To abstain from mallows, &c.*

The 38th Symbol in the *Protreptics* is: “Transplant mallows in your garden, but eat them not.” On which Iamblichus observes as follows: “This Symbol obscurely signifies that plants of this kind turn with the sun, and it suggests that this should be noticed by us. It also adds, *transplant*, that is to say, observe its nature, its tendency toward, and sympathy with the sun; but do not rest satisfied, nor dwell upon this, but transfer, and as it were transplant your conception to kindred plants and herbs, and also to animals which are not kindred, to stones and rivers, and, in short, to natures of every kind. For you will find them to be prolific and multiform, and admirably abundant; and this to one who begins from the mallows, as from a root and principle, is significant of the union and harmony of the world. Therefore, do not only avoid destroying or obliterating observations of this kind, but increase and multiply them as if they were transplanted.”

### P. 59. *Thus too he ordered them to abstain from the fish Melanurus.* <note>According to Aelian and Suidas also, *melanurus* is a fish; but as the word signifies that which has a black end, it is very appropriately used as a symbol of a material nature.</note>

The 6th Symbol in the *Protreptics* is, “Abstain from melanurus; for it belongs to the terrestrial Gods.” And this, according to Iamblichus, admonishes us to embrace the celestial journey, to join ourselves to the intellectual Gods, to become separated from a material nature, and to be led as it were in a circular progression to an immaterial and pure life. It further exhorts us to adopt the most excellent worship of the

<summary>This page explains two Pythagorean symbols: the instruction to transplant but not eat mallows, representing the harmony of nature and the sun, and the prohibition against eating the fish melanurus, symbolizing a rejection of material nature in favor of the divine.</summary>

### P. 59. *And also not to receive the fish Erythynus.*

In the *Protreptics*, this is the 33rd Symbol, which Iamblichus explains as follows: "This Symbol seems to refer merely to the etymology of the name. Do not receive an unblushing and impudent man; nor, on the contrary, one who is stupidly astonished, who blushes at everything, and is extremely humble because of the weakness of his intellect and reasoning power. From this, it is also understood: do not be such a person yourself."

### P. 59. *He likewise exhorted them to abstain from beans.*

In the *Protreptics*, this is the 37th Symbol; Iamblichus has not explained the more mystical meaning of this symbol for us. He only says that "it warns us to beware of everything that corrupts our conversation with the Gods and divine prophecy." However, Aristotle appears to have provided the true mystical reason why the Pythagoreans abstained from beans. For he says (according to Laertius) "that Pythagoras considered beans a symbol of generation <note>meaning the whole of a visible and corporeal nature</note>, which exists according to a straight line and is without bending; because among almost all seed-bearing plants, the bean alone is perforated through its entire length and is not obstructed by any intervening joints." Therefore, he adds, "it resembles the gates of Hades." For these gates are perpetually open, offering no obstacle to souls descending into generation. The exhortation to abstain from beans, therefore, is equivalent to warning us to

Since Pythagoras, as Iamblichus informs us on page 7, was initiated in all the mysteries of Byblus and Tyre, in the sacred operations of the Syrians, and in the mysteries of the Phoenicians, and also (p. 9) that he spent twenty-two years in the adyta <note>the innermost sanctuaries or holy of holies</note> of temples in Egypt, associated with the Magi in Babylon, and was instructed by them in their venerable knowledge—it is not at all surprising that he was skilled in magic or theurgy, and was therefore able to perform things which surpass *merely human power*, and which appear to be perfectly incredible to the common people. For “magic” (as we learn from Psellus in his manuscript treatise on Daemons) “formed the last part of the sacerdotal science.” He further informs us “that magic investigates the nature, power, and quality of everything sublunary; namely, of the elements and their parts, of animals, all various plants, and their fruits, of stones, and herbs: and in short, it explores the essence and power of everything. From this, therefore, it produces its effects. And it

--- Page 228 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of theurgy and ancient magic, specifically focusing on the philosophical foundation of "sympathy" and the interconnectedness of all levels of existence.</meta>

They considered that there is a certain alliance and sympathy among natural things toward each other, and of manifest things toward occult powers. Having discovered that all things exist within all others, they constructed a sacred science based on this mutual sympathy and similarity. Thus, they recognized supreme things in those that are subordinate, and the subordinate in the supreme: in the celestial regions, earthly properties exist in a causal and celestial way; and on earth, celestial properties exist, but according to an earthly condition. 

For how else shall we account for those plants called <term>heliotropes</term>, that is, attendants of the sun, moving in correspondence with the revolution of its orb, or <term>selenitropes</term>, or attendants of the moon, turning in exact conformity to her motion? It is because all things pray and sing hymns to the leaders of their respective orders; some do so intellectually, others rationally; some in a natural way, and others in a sensory manner. Hence the sunflower, as far as it is able, moves in a circular dance toward the sun; so that if anyone could hear the pulsation made by its circuit in the air, they would perceive a sound composed in honor of its king, such as a plant is capable of creating. 

Hence, too, we may behold the sun and moon on the earth, but according to an earthly quality; while in the celestial regions, all plants, stones, and animals possess an intellectual life according to a celestial nature. Now the ancients, having contemplated this mutual sympathy of things, applied both celestial and earthly natures for occult purposes. By means of these, through a certain similarity, they drew divine virtues down into this inferior world. For, indeed, similarity itself is a sufficient cause for binding things together in union and harmony. Thus, if a piece of paper is heated and then placed near a lamp, even if it does not touch the fire, the paper will suddenly ignite, and the flame will descend from the higher to the lower parts. This heated paper we may compare to a certain relationship of inferior things

The author uses the example of heliotropes following the sun to illustrate how all things "pray" to their divine leaders according to their own nature.</summary>

<keywords>sympathy, occult powers, sacred science, heliotropes, selenitropes, theurgy, celestial regions, divine virtues, Proclus</keywords>

> "Again, the lotus, before the sun rises, folds its leaves into itself, but gradually expands them as it rises: unfolding them in proportion to the sun's ascent to the zenith; but then gradually contracting them as that luminary descends to the west. Hence this plant, by the expansion and contraction of its leaves, appears to honor the sun no less than men do by the gesture of their eyelids and the motion of their lips. But this imitation and specific participation in heavenly light is not only visible in plants, which possess nothing more than a trace of life, but likewise in particular stones. Thus, the sun-stone, by its golden rays, imitates those of the sun; but the stone called the 'eye of heaven,' or of the sun, has a shape similar to the pupil of an eye, and a ray shines from the middle of the pupil. Thus too the lunar stone, which has a shape similar to the moon when crescent-shaped, follows the lunar motion by a certain change in itself. Finally, the stone called *helioselenus*, that is, of the sun and moon, imitates, in a manner, the union of those luminaries, which it represents by its color. So that all things are full of divine natures; terrestrial natures receiving the fullness of those that are celestial, but celestial natures receiving the fullness of super-celestial essences;<note>That is, natures which are not connected with a body.</note> while every order of things proceeds gradually in a beautiful descent from the highest to the lowest. For whatever details are collected into one above the order of things, are afterwards

> “Next, there are many solar animals, such as lions and cocks, which participate, according to their nature, in a certain solar divinity; from which it is wonderful how much inferior beings yield to superior ones in the same order, even though they do not yield in size and power. For this reason, it is said that a cock is greatly feared and, as it were, reverenced by a lion; the reason for which we cannot attribute to matter or the senses, but only to the contemplation of a celestial order. Thus, we shall find that the presence of the solar power aligns more with a cock than with a lion. This is evident from considering that the cock, as if with certain hymns, applauds and calls to the rising sun when it directs its course toward us from the antipodes; and that solar angels sometimes appear in forms of this kind—who, although they are without shape, nevertheless present themselves to us, who are connected with shape, in some perceptible form. Sometimes, too, there are demons with a lion-like face who, when a cock is placed before them, suddenly disappear unless they belong to a solar order. This occurs because those natures that have an inferior rank in the same order always revere their superiors, just as many people, upon beholding the images of divine men, are accustomed by the very sight to fear committing anything base.

> “Finally, some things turn in correspondence with the revolutions of the sun, such as the plants we have mentioned, while others imitate the solar rays in a certain way, such as the palm and the date; some imitate the fiery nature of the sun, such as the laurel; and others imitate a different property. Indeed, we may perceive that the properties which are collected in the sun are distributed everywhere to subsequent natures established in a solar order—that is, to angels, demons, souls, animals, plants, and stones. Thus, the authors of the ancient priesthood discovered the worship of superior powers from visible things,”

They indeed mixed many things together because they saw that some simple substances possessed a divine quality (though not when taken individually) sufficient to summon that specific power of which they were participants. Hence, by mixing many things together, they drew down a heavenly influx upon us; and by creating one thing from many, they achieved a resemblance to that One which is above all things; and they created statues from a mixture of various substances that aligned in sympathy and agreement. Besides this, they collected composite scents, through a divine art, into a single fragrance that encompassed a multitude of powers and symbolized the unity of a divine essence; they considered that separation weakens each of these powers, but mixing them together restores them to the form of their original model.

> “But sometimes one herb or one stone is enough for a divine operation. For example, a thistle is enough to bring about the sudden appearance of some higher power; but laurel, raccinum <note>Likely a reference to a specific thorny plant or a corruption of "vaccinium"</note> (or a thorny kind of sprig), the land and sea onion, coral, diamonds, and jasper serve as a safeguard. The heart of a mole is useful for divination, while sulfur and seawater are used for purification. Therefore, the ancient priests, through the mutual relationship and sympathy between things, gathered their virtues into one, but drove them away through resistance and antipathy; purifying with sulfur and bitumen when necessary, and sprinkling with seawater. For sulfur purifies because of its sharp smell, but seawater purifies because of its fiery quality. Besides this, in the worship of the gods, they offered animals and other substances consistent with their nature. They received, first of all, the powers of demons, as these are closest to natural substances and actions; and through these natural substances, they summoned into their presence those powers to which they were related. <meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between natural substances and divine powers, explaining how practitioners used material symbols to ascend to a spiritual understanding of the Gods.</meta>

daemons to the powers and energies of the Gods; partly, indeed, from demonic instruction, but partly by their own industry, interpreting suitable symbols and ascending to a proper understanding of the Gods. And lastly, setting aside natural substances and their operations, they brought themselves into the communion and fellowship of the Gods.”

It will no doubt be objected by most people of the present day, who believe in nothing beyond the information of their senses, that plants, animals, and stones no longer possess those wonderful sympathetic powers mentioned by Proclus in the extract above. In response to any such objector, whose *little soul* (in the language of the Emperor Julian) is indeed sharp but sees nothing with a vision healthy and sound, it must be said that this is not at all surprising at a time when, as the author of the *Asclepian Dialogue* rightly observes, “there is a lamentable departure of divinity from man, when nothing worthy of heaven or celestial concerns is heard or believed, and when every divine voice is, by a necessary silence, mute.”¹ But to the philosophic reader, it must be noted that just as in the realms of generation—or in other words, the sublunary region—wholes (namely, the spheres of the different elements) remain perpetually according to nature, while their parts are sometimes in accordance with and sometimes contrary to nature; this must also be true of the parts of the earth. Therefore, when those cycles take place during which the parts of the earth exist according to nature—which are rightly called “fertile periods” by Plato—the powers of plants, animals, and stones magically sympathize with superior natures. This occurs as a result of a more abundant participation in them, through a greater degree of capacity to receive and an alliance to the shared powers. But during those cycles in

### P. 78. *The eternal essence of number is the most providential principle of the universe, etc.*

The following account of the manner in which the Pythagoreans philosophized about numbers is extracted from my *Theoretic Arithmetic*, and the information contained in it is principally derived from the great Syrianus.

> “The Pythagoreans, turning from common paths and delivering their philosophy in secret only to those who were worthy to receive it, presented it to others through mathematical names. Hence, they called forms "numbers," as things which are the first to be separated from indivisible union; for the natures which are above forms are also above separation.<note>1 Forms exist at the extremity of the intelligible triad, which consists of *being*, *life*, and *intellect*. But being and life, along with all they contain, exist here involved in indivisible union. See my *Proclus on the Theology of Plato*.</note> Therefore, they obscurely signified the all-perfect multitude of forms through the duad; but they indicated the first formal principles by the monad and duad, as not being numbers; and also by the first triad and tetrad, as being the first numbers—the one being odd and the other even—from which the decad is generated by addition; for the sum of 1, 2, 3, and 4 is ten. But after numbers, in secondary and manifold lives, they introduced geometrical magnitudes prior to physical ones; these they also referred to numbers as their formal causes and principles; referring the point, indeed,

--- Page 234 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Pythagorean number theory, specifically how the monad, duad, triad, and tetrad correspond to geometric dimensions and levels of knowledge.</meta>

...as being indivisible, to the monad; but a line, as the first interval, to the duad; and again, a surface, as having a more abundant interval, to the triad; and a solid to the tetrad. They also called—as is evident from the testimony of Aristotle—the first length the duad; for it is not simply length, but the *first* length, so that by this they might signify cause. In a similar manner also, they named the *first* breadth the triad, and the *first* depth the tetrad. They also referred all psychical knowledge to formal principles. And indeed, they referred intellectual knowledge to the monad, as it is compressed according to indivisible union; but they referred scientific knowledge to the duad, as it is evolved and proceeds from cause to the thing caused, yet through the unwavering and always through the same things. They referred opinion to the triad, because its power is not always directed to the same thing, but at one time inclines to the true and at another to the false. And they referred sense to the tetrad, because it has an apprehension of bodies; for in the duad there is one interval from one monad to the other; but in the triad there are two intervals from any one monad to the rest; and in the tetrad there are three. They referred, therefore, everything knowable to principles—namely, beings and their gnostic powers. But they divided beings not according to breadth, but according to depth: into intelligibles, objects of science, objects of opinion, and sensibles. In a similar manner, they also divided knowledge into intellect, science, opinion, and sense. The extremity, therefore, of the intelligible triad, or the animal itself, as it is called by Plato in the *Timæus*, is assumed from the division of the objects of knowledge, manifesting the intelligible order in which forms themselves—namely, the first forms and the principles of these—are contained: namely, the idea of the one itself, of the first length, which is the duad itself, and also the ideas of the first breadth and the first depth; (for in common

<meta>This page continues the discussion on how the Pythagoreans and Plato applied the concept of "the first" to numerical and ontological principles, specifically expanding on the etymology and function of numbers as paradigms.</meta>

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;“Again, the Pythagoreans and Plato did not derive the name *idea* from one thing and *ideal number* from another. But since the statement is profoundly true that all things are similar to number, it is evident that number, and especially every ideal number, was named because of its role as a paradigm (a model). If anyone, however, wishes to understand this from the name itself, it is easy to infer that *idea* was so called because it makes its participants similar to itself, imparting to them *form*, *order*, *beauty*, and *unity*. This happens because it always preserves the same form, expanding its own power to an infinity of particulars and clothing its eternal participants with the same species. *Number* also, since it imparts proportion and elegant arrangement to all things, was given this name. For the ancients, says Syrianus,¹ used the word ἄρσαι *arsai* to mean to *adapt* or *compose*, which is the origin of ἀριθμός *arithmos*, or *number*. Therefore, ἀνάρσιον *anarsion* among the Greeks signifies *incomposite* (not composed). From this also come those Greek sayings, *you will adapt the balance*, *they placed number together with them*, and also *number and friendship*. Because of all this, the Greeks called number *arithmos*, as that which measures and orderly arranges all things and unites them in a friendly bond.
&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;“Furthermore, some of the Pythagoreans discussed only inseparable numbers—that is, numbers which are inseparable from worldly natures. Others, however, spoke about those that exist separately from the universe, in which they saw, as paradigms, those numbers that are perfected by nature. But others, making a distinction between the two, explained their doctrine in a clearer and more perfect manner. If it is necessary, however, to speak concerning the difference between these monads, and their lack of difference, we must say that the monads which exist in quantity,

<meta>This page continues Syrianus's commentary on Pythagorean and Platonic number theory, specifically addressing the distinction between essential, mathematical, and physical numbers and how they relate to the concepts of sameness and difference.</meta>

are by no means to be extended to essential numbers; but when we call essential numbers monads, we must assert that all of them mutually differ from each other by *difference* itself, and that they possess a lack of difference from *sameness*. It is evident also, that those which are in the same order are contained, through mutual comparison, in sameness rather than in difference, but that those which are in different orders deal with much diversity through the dominion of *difference*.

> “Again, the Pythagoreans asserted that nature produces sensible things <note>Objects perceived by the senses</note> by numbers; but then these numbers were not mathematical but physical. Since they spoke symbolically, it is not improbable that they demonstrated every property of sensible things by mathematical names. However, says Syrianus, to attribute to them a knowledge of sensible numbers alone is not only ridiculous, but highly impious. For they truly received, from the theology of Orpheus, the principles of intelligible and intellectual numbers; they assigned them an abundant progression and extended their dominion as far as to the sensible things themselves.”

Again, their conceptions about mathematical and physical number were as follows:

> “As in everything, according to the doctrine of Aristotle, one thing corresponds to matter and another to form, so in any number—as for instance the pentad <note>The number five</note>—its five monads (and in short its quantity and the number which is the subject of participation) are derived from the duad <note>The principle of duality or the Indefinite Dyad</note> itself. But its form, i.e., the pentad itself, is from the monad; for every form is a monad and unites its subject quantity. The pentad itself, therefore, which is a monad, proceeds from the principal monad, forms its subject quantity (which is itself formless), and connects it to its own form. For there are two principles of mathematical numbers in our souls: the monad, which comprehends in itself all the forms of numbers and corresponds to the monad in intel—”

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagorean number theory, specifically how the monad and the duad interact. It picks up from the previous page where the form of a number (like the pentad) was said to unite its subject quantity.</meta>

[intel]lectual natures; and the duad <note>The principle of duality or the Indefinite Dyad</note>, which is a certain generative principle of infinite power, and which on this account—as being the image of the never-failing and intelligible duad—is called "indefinite." While this proceeds to all things, it is not abandoned in its course by the monad <note>The principle of unity</note>; instead, that which proceeds from the monad continually distinguishes and forms boundless quantity, gives a specific distinction to all its orderly progressions, and incessantly endows them with forms. And just as in worldly natures there is nothing formless, nor any vacuum among the species of things, so likewise in mathematical number, no quantity is left unnumbered; for otherwise, the forming power of the monad would be overcome by the indefinite duad, and no middle ground exists between sequential numbers and the well-ordered activity of the monad.

"Neither, therefore, does the pentad <note>The number five</note> consist of substance and accident <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a non-essential quality, like being "white" is to a "man"</note>, as a white man does; nor of genus and difference, as 'man' consists of 'animal' (the genus) and 'biped' (the difference); nor of five monads <note>Units</note> mutually touching each other, like a bundle of wood; nor of mixed things, like a drink made from wine and honey; nor of things held in a specific position, as stones by their position complete a house; nor lastly, as things that can be counted, for these are nothing more than individual particulars. But it does not follow that numbers themselves, simply because they consist of indivisible monads, have nothing else besides monads (for the multitude of points in continuous quantity is an indivisible multitude, yet it is not for this reason that another entity is completed from the points themselves). Rather, this happens because there is something in them which corresponds to matter, and something which corresponds to form. Lastly, when we unite the triad <note>The number three</note> with the tetrad <note>The number four</note>, we say that we make seven. This assertion, however, is not true: for monads joined with monads produce indeed the underlying subject of the number 7, but nothing more. Who then imparts the

<summary>This page explains the interaction between the monad and the indefinite duad in creating mathematical numbers, arguing that a number is not merely a collection of units but a synthesis of matter and form. Syrianus refutes several common analogies for the composition of numbers, such as simple mixtures or physical arrangements, to emphasize their intellectual nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how formal principles (like the "pentad" or "heptad") give structure to quantity. It uses the analogy of a craftsman to explain how the "counting soul" imposes form upon mathematical units, distinguishing between the underlying "matter" of a number and its formal essence.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>

heptadic <note>Relating to the number seven</note> form to these monads <note>Units</note>? Who is it also that gives the form of a bed to a certain number of pieces of wood? Should we not say that the soul of the carpenter, through the art which he possesses, fashions the wood so as to receive the form of a bed, and that the numerative <note>Counting or numbering</note> soul, by possessing within herself a monad which acts as a principle, gives form and existence to all numbers? But in this only consists the difference: the carpenter’s art is not naturally inherent in us and requires manual operation because it deals with sensible <note>Physical or perceptible</note> matter; but the numerative art is naturally present within us, and is therefore possessed by all men, and has an intellectual matter which it instantaneously invests with form. And this is what deceives the multitude, who think that the heptad <note>The number seven as a formal principle</note> is nothing besides seven monads. For the imagination of the common people—unless it first sees a thing unadorned, then the subsequent energy of the one who adorns it, and lastly, above all, the thing itself, perfect and formed—cannot be persuaded that it has two natures: one formless and the other formal, and still further, that which imparts form beyond these. Instead, they assert that the subject is one, and exists without being created. Hence, perhaps, the ancient theologians and Plato ascribed temporal generations <note>Beginnings in time</note> to things that have no beginning, and to things which are perpetually adorned and regularly arranged, they ascribed a lack of order and ornament—the wandering and the boundless—so that they might lead men to the knowledge of a formal and effective cause. It is, therefore, by no means surprising that though seven sensible monads are never without the heptad, these should be distinguished by science; the former should be seen as the subject and be analogous to matter, while the latter should correspond to species and form.

It explains that the "counting soul" naturally imposes this form on intellectual matter, a process often misunderstood by the public who fail to distinguish between form and subject matter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the nature of numbers, arguing that numbers like the triad or pentad are not merely sums of units but possess distinct, indivisible forms that structure both mathematical thought and the physical world.</meta>

air; so when one number unites with another—as, for instance, the triad <note>The number three</note> with the duad <note>The number two</note>—the species or forms of the two numbers are not mingled, except in their immaterial reasons <note>Rational or productive principles; logoi</note> (or productive principles), in which, even though they are separate, they are not prevented from being united; however, the quantities of the two numbers which are placed together become the subject of the pentad <note>The number five</note>. The triad, therefore, is a single entity, and so is the tetrad <note>The number four</note>, even in mathematical numbers: for although in the ennead <note>The number nine</note>, or the number nine, you may conceive of a first, second, and third triad, you nevertheless see one thing assumed three times; and in short, in the ennead, there is nothing but the form of the ennead within the quantity of nine monads <note>Individual units</note>. But if you mentally separate its subject (for form is impartible <note>Indivisible</note>), you will immediately provide it with forms corresponding to its division; for our soul cannot endure to see that which is formless and unadorned, especially since she possesses the power of providing it with ornament.

Since separate numbers also possess a demiurgic <note>Creative or world-forming</note> or fabricative power, which mathematical numbers imitate, the sensible world <note>The physical world perceived by the senses</note> likewise contains images of those numbers by which it is adorned; so that all things are in all, but in a manner appropriate to each. The sensible world, therefore, exists based on immaterial and energetic reasons and from more ancient causes. But those who do not admit that nature herself is full of productive powers—fearing they might be forced to double the things themselves—wonder how magnitude and gravity are composed of things lacking magnitude and gravity. Yet they are never composed of things of this kind (which lack gravity and magnitude) as if they were made from parts. Instead, magnitude is generated from essentially impartible elements, since form and matter are the elements of bodies; and it is generated even more so from those truer causes which are considered in demiurgic reasons and forms. Is it not therefore necessary that all dimensions, and all moving masses,

<meta>This page continues the philosophical argument that physical objects and their qualities must originate from immaterial and indivisible principles, specifically focusing on how numbers serve as the underlying structure and "boundaries" of the sensible world.</meta>

must receive their generation from these? For either bodies are ungenerated, like incorporeal natures; or else things without extension are the causes of things with extension; indivisibles are the causes of things with parts; and things that are insensible and without contact are the causes of sensible things and their opposites. We must agree with those who claim that things possessing magnitude are generated in this way from indivisibles. Therefore, the Pythagorean Eurytus and his followers, seeing the images of things themselves within numbers, correctly assigned specific numbers to specific things according to their unique characteristics. Because of this, he said that a specific number is the boundary of this plant, and another number is the boundary of this animal; just as 6 is the boundary of a triangle, 9 of a square, and 8 of a cube. Just as a musician tunes his lyre using mathematical numbers, nature uses her own natural numbers to orderly arrange and modulate her creations.

> "Indeed, that the heavens participate in numbers, and that there is a solar number as well as a lunar number, is obvious—as the saying goes—even to the blind. For the returns of the heavenly bodies to their original positions (ἀποκατάστασεις <note>Apokatastaseis: restorations or cyclic returns to a starting point</note>) would not always occur through the same steps and in the same way unless one and the same number held dominion in each. All of these contribute to the movement of the celestial spheres and are contained within their perfect number. There is also a specific natural number belonging to every animal. For members of the same species would not have organs structured in the same way, nor would they reach puberty and old age at roughly the same time, or reproduce, nor would the fetus be nourished or grow according to regular periods, unless they were bound by the same natural measure. According to Plato himself—the greatest of the Pythagoreans—number is the cause of both superior and inferior births. Therefore, although the Pythagoreans sometimes speak of the squares and

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the relationship between mathematical principles and physical reality, specifically addressing how numbers govern the forms and powers of material objects.</meta>

cubes of natural numbers, they do not consider them to be merely monadic <note>composed of units or relating to the mathematical Monad</note>, such as the numbers 9 and 27. Instead, they signify through these names, by way of similarity, the progression of natural numbers into, and their rule over, the process of creation. In the same way, though they call them equal or double, they demonstrate the rule and harmony of ideas within these numbers. Therefore, different things do not use the same number in respects where they are different, nor do the same things use different numbers in respects where they are the same.

> "In short, physical numbers are material forms distributed within the subject that receives them. However, material powers are the sources of connection and modification for physical bodies. For form is one thing, and the power proceeding from it is another. Form itself is indeed indivisible and essential; but when it is extended and becomes bulky, it emits material powers—which are certain qualities—from itself, as if they were a breath or outflow. Thus, for instance, in fire, its form and essence are indivisible and are truly the image of the cause of fire; for in things that have parts, that which is indivisible has a foundational existence. But from this indivisible form in fire, which exists in it as a number, an extension involving physical space occurs within matter. From this, the powers of fire are emitted, such as heat, coldness, moisture, or something else of that kind. And these qualities are indeed related to the essence, but they are by no means the essence of fire itself. For essences do not arise from qualities, nor are essence and power the same thing. Instead, the essential always precedes power. From this single essence, a multitude of powers proceeds, and the distributed arises from that which is undistributed—just as many activities are the results of a single power."

<summary>The text explains that physical numbers and forms are the indivisible foundations of material objects, governing their creation and characteristics. It distinguishes between a thing's inner essence (form) and the outward qualities or powers (like heat in fire) that emanate from it.</summary>

P. 79. *For Pythagoras always proclaimed that nothing wonderful pertaining to the Gods, or divine <term>dogmas</term> <note>doctrines</note>, should be disbelieved.*

This forms the fourth symbol in the *Protreptics*, and is explained by Iamblichus as follows:— "This <term>dogma</term> sufficiently honors and reveals the transcendence of the Gods, providing us with a provision for our journey <note>viaticum</note> and reminding us that we should not judge divine power based on our own judgment. However, it is likely that some things appear difficult and impossible to us because of our physical existence and our involvement with birth and decay; from our having a brief existence; from being subject to various diseases; from the smallness of our dwellings; from our tendency to be pulled toward the center <note>gravity</note>; from our sleepiness, poverty, and overindulgence; from our lack of wisdom and our weakness; from the obstacles facing our soul, and a variety of other circumstances, even though our nature possesses many noble privileges. At the same time, however, we fall completely short of the Gods, and we possess neither the same power as them nor equal virtue. This symbol, therefore, specifically introduces the knowledge of the Gods as beings who are able to accomplish all things. For this reason, it urges us to disbelieve nothing concerning the Gods. It also adds, 'nor about divine <term>dogmas</term>'; namely, those belonging to the <term>Pythagorean</term> philosophy. For these, being secured by disciplined studies and scientific theory, are alone true and free from falsehood, being supported by every kind of demonstration and accompanied by necessity. The same symbol is also capable of urging us toward the science concerning the Gods; for it encourages us to acquire a kind of knowledge through which we will be in no way lacking in our understanding of things stated about the Gods. It is also able to urge the same things concerning divine <term>dogmas</term> and a disciplin-
