<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Pythagoras's discovery of musical ratios, specifically describing the mathematical relationship of the octave using two strings.</meta>

At point E, place what is commonly called the *tactus*, but which was named a hemisphere by the ancients because of its shape. The tactus, therefore, being placed under E, press the chord there so that only one half of it—for instance, ED—may be fully struck and resound. After striking each of the chords at the same time—namely, the whole of AB and the half of ED—so that they resound simultaneously, you will hear the sweetest of all consonances, composed from the sound of the whole chord AB and the sound of the half ED. 

The ancients called this consonance <term>diapason</term>, meaning *through all* <note>the chords</note>, because in the musical instruments of the ancients, the two extreme chords—that is, the deepest and the highest of all the chords—contained this consonance. Thus, by making a transition from the deepest chord through all the chords to the highest and most acute of all, they would hear this sweetest consonance. It was, likewise, said to be in a duple ratio in the proportion of one sound to the other. For the sound of the chord AB is twice as great, or deeper, than the sound of the half ED. For as sounding bodies are related to each other, so are their sounds; and the chord AB is double the length of ED. 

This, however, is now commonly called the octave, because from the first and deepest sound, which is called *ut*, to that sound which corresponds to it in the diapason consonance, there are these eight sounds: *ut, re, mi, fa, sol, re, mi, fa.* And of these, the first *ut* and the last *fa*, which is the eighth, produce the diapason consonance, or the double ratio, or the octave.

Again, let the same chord CD be divided into three equal parts at points F and G.

<image-desc>A diagram representing two parallel horizontal lines (chords). The top line has the letter A at its left end and B at its right end. The bottom line is labeled C at the left end and D at the right end. Two small vertical tick marks divide the bottom line CD into three segments; these marks are labeled F and G respectively.</image-desc>

<summary>This page explains the mathematical demonstration of the octave, or diapason, showing that a string half the length of another produces a sound in a duple ratio. It describes how this interval encompasses eight notes, from the deepest to the highest, forming the sweetest of musical consonances.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of musical ratios, moving from the octave (diapason) to the fifth (diapente) and the fourth (diatessaron) based on the division of a string.</meta>

FD, therefore, will be two-thirds of the whole CD as well as of the whole AB. Let the <term>tactus</term> now be placed at F, and let AB and FD be struck at the same time; a very sweet and perfect consonance will indeed be heard, yet it is not as sweet as the <term>diapason</term>. The ancients called this <term>diapente</term> <note>that is, through five chords</note>, because the first and the fifth chord produce this consonance. But according to proportion, it is called <term>sesquialter</term> because the chord AB is sesquialter to FD, and consequently the sounds of these chords are also in the same ratio. A <term>sesquialter</term> ratio is when the greater quantity AB contains the lesser FD once, plus half of it besides. Indeed, it is commonly called the fifth because it is composed from the first sound *ut* and the fifth, *sol*.

Again, let the same chord be cut into four equal parts at points H, E, and I,

This was called by the ancients <term>diatessaron</term> <note>that is, through four chords or sounds</note> for a reason similar to why the former were named. With reference, however, to the ratio of the chords and sounds, it is called <term>sesquitertian</term> because the greater AB contains the lesser once, plus a third part of it besides. But it is now commonly called a fourth because it is found between the first sound *ut* and the fourth, *fa*. <meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals, specifically defining the "tone" and "semitone" by comparing ratios of string lengths (HD, FD, CD, etc.) and identifying them with solfège syllables.</meta>

ratios, we shall find that the greater HD will have a sesquioctave<note>Because 1/8 is to 1/9 as 9 is to 8.</note> ratio to the lesser FD, and the sound of the greater HD to the lesser FD will have the same ratio; that is, in modern terms, there is a sesquioctave ratio between *fa* and *sol*. But if these two sounds are heard together, they will be discordant to the ear. Again, the distance between these sounds *fa* and *sol*, or between the chords HD and FD, or between the two harmonic intervals HD and FD—the ratio of which was sesquioctave—was called by the ancients a tone. Afterwards, they divided the whole of CD into nine equal parts, the first of which is marked at K, so that the whole CD may have a sesquioctave ratio to the remainder KD, which contains eight of those parts. This, in a similar manner, will be the interval of a tone; the first sound of which (that is, of the whole CD) is now called *ut*, while the second sound of the rest of the chord KD is called *re*. Afterward, they divided the remainder KD into nine parts in a similar manner, the first part of which is marked at point L. And for the same reason, there will be a sesquioctave ratio between the chord KD and the chord LD and their sounds. The sound of the chord LD is now called *mi*; but the interval which remains between the chord LD and the chord HD does not have a sesquioctave ratio, but rather one almost half that size; and therefore, an interval of this kind was called a semitone, and also <term>diesis</term> or a division. But that interval which remains between points F and E they divided in the same manner as the space between C and H was divided, and they again found the same sounds. Let those divisions be marked by the points M and N; and here, also, between N and E—or between *mi* and *fa*—there is another semitone in the same way. These eight sounds, therefore, are *ut*, *re*, *mi*, *fa*, *sol*, *re*, *mi*, and *fa*, which compose the whole <term>diapason</term>. For as we have observed before, the consonance <term>diapason</term> exists between *ut* and the last *fa*, or between the chord CD

or AB, and the chord ED. But among the intervals between the sounds, there are two semitones: specifically, one between *mi* and *fa*, indicated by the letters L and N, and the other between the last *mi* and *fa*, indicated by the letters N and E. The remaining five intervals are whole tones. It must also be observed that from *ut* to the first *sol* is the consonance <term>diapente</term> <note>a perfect fifth</note>, which contains three whole-tone intervals and one semitone; nonetheless, there are five sounds in total: *ut, re, mi, fa, sol*.

Again, from *sol* to the last *fa* there are four sounds—*sol, re, mi, fa*—which are perfectly similar to the first four: *ut, re, mi, fa*. However, the former are lower in pitch, while the latter are higher. And just as from *ut* to the first *fa* is the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>a perfect fourth</note>, likewise from *sol* to the last *fa* is another diatessaron. From this, it must finally be observed that it follows that the two consonances, the diatessaron and the diapente, constitute the whole <term>diapason</term> <note>an octave</note>; or, that the diapason is divided into one diatessaron and one diapente. For from *ut* to *sol* is the diapente, but from *sol* to the last *fa* is the diatessaron. This would also be the case if we said that from *ut* to the first *fa* is the diatessaron, as is evident from the division of the chord; but from the first *fa* to the last *fa* is the diapente, as is evident from the four intervals of the chord, three of which are tones and the remaining interval is a semitone—which were also contained between *ut* and *sol* in the other diapente.

Now again, let the <term>tactus</term> <note>the point of contact or measurement on the string</note> be placed at I; but I is the fourth part of the whole CD. Let AB and ID also be struck at the same time, and the sweetest consonance, called <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>a double octave</note>, will be produced. It is so named because it is composed of two diapasons, the first of which is between AB (or CD) and ED, and the second between ED and ID; for the ratio of the latter is double, just like the ratio of the former. The ratio of the bisdiapason is also quadruple, as is evident from

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals on a monochord, building on the division of string lengths to define composite consonances and their relationship to ancient Greek terminology.</meta>

the division; and it is commonly called a *fifteenth*, because from the first *ut* to this sound, which is also named *fa*, there would be fifteen sounds if the interval *EI* were divided in the same way as the first *CE* is divided.

Furthermore, let *GD* be a third part of the whole *CD*, and let the <term>tactus</term> <note>the point of measurement or contact on the string</note> be placed at *G*. Then, at one and the same time, let *AB* and *GD* be struck, and a sweet consonance will be heard, which is called <term>diapasondiapente</term> <note>an interval of an octave plus a fifth</note>, because it is composed of one <term>diapason</term> <note>an octave</note> contained by the interval *CE* (or the two chords *CD* and *ED*) and one <term>diapente</term> <note>a perfect fifth</note> contained by the interval *EG* (or the chords *ED* and *GD*). For the chord *ED* is in a <term>sesquialter</term> <note>a ratio of 3:2</note> ratio to the chord *GD*, which ratio constitutes the nature of the <term>diapente</term>. Also, the proportion of this consonance is triple. For the chord *AB* or *CD* is triple the length of *GD*; and it is commonly called the *twelfth*, because between *ut* and *sol* (denoted by the letter *G*) there would be twelve sounds if the interval *EG* were divided into its parts. From all this, it is clear through the experience of the ear that there are five consonances in total: three simple—the <term>diapason</term>, the <term>diapente</term>, and the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>a perfect fourth</note>; and two composite—the <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>a double octave</note> and the <term>diapasondiapente</term>.

Finally, it is necessary to observe that the ancient Greeks named these sounds (*ut*, *re*, etc.) differently. For the first (that is, the lowest sound or chord), which is now called *ut*, they named *hypate*, and the others in the following order:

| | | |
|---|---|---|
| *Ut*, | *Hypate*, | i.e., *Principalis* <note>Principal</note>. |
| *Re*, | *Parhypate*, | — *Postprincipalis* <note>After the principal</note>. |
| *Mi*, | *Lychanos*, | — *Index* <note>Pointer or Forefinger</note>. |
| *Fa*, | *Mese*, | — *Media* <note>Middle</note>. |
| *Sol*, | *Paramese*, | — *Postmedia* <note>After the middle</note>. |
| *Re*, | *Trite*, | — *Tertia* <note>Third</note>. |
| *Mi*, | *Paranete*, | — *Antepenultima* <note>Second to last</note>. |
| *Fa*, | *Nete*, | — *Ultima, vel suprema* <note>Last or highest</note>. |

--- Page 249 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical and musical ratios, transitioning from the division of the monochord to the philosophical and numerical foundations of these harmonies according to the Pythagorean tradition.</meta>

P. 80. *I swear by him who found the tetractys.*

The Pythagoreans called the tetrad "every number" because it contains within itself all numbers up to the decad, as well as the decad itself; for the sum of 1, 2, 3, and 4 is 10. Therefore, both the decad and the tetrad were said by them to be "every number"—the decad in actuality, but the tetrad in potentiality. Similarly, they said that the sum of these four numbers constitutes the tetractys, in which all harmonic ratios are included. For 4 to 1, which is a quadruple ratio, forms the bisdiapason symphony; the ratio of 3 to 2, which is sesquialter, forms the diapente symphony; 4 to 3, which is sesquitertian, the diatessaron symphony; and 2 to 1, which is a double ratio, forms the diapason.

However, because of the great reverence paid to the tetractys by the Pythagoreans, it is appropriate to give it a more thorough discussion and, for this purpose, to show from <term>Theo of Smyrna</term>¹ how many types of tetractys there are. "The tetractys," he says, "was honored by the Pythagoreans above all else, not only because all symphonies are found to exist within it, but also because it appears to contain the nature of all things." Consequently, the following was their oath: "No, by him who gave to our soul the tetractys, which contains the fountain and root of eternal nature." By "him who gave the tetractys," they mean <term>Pythagoras</term>; for the doctrine concerning it appears to have been his invention. This previously mentioned tetractys, therefore, is seen in the arrangement of the first numbers: 1, 2, 3, and 4. But the second tetractys arises from the increase by multiplication of even and odd numbers beginning from the <term>monad</term>.

Of these, the <term>monad</term> is assumed to be the first, because,

¹ In Mathematics, p. 147.

--- Page 250 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the tetractys, specifically the second type which arises from the multiplication of even and odd numbers starting from the monad.</meta>

As we have observed before, it is the principle of all even, odd, and evenly-odd numbers, and its nature is simple. But the three successive numbers receive their composition according to the even and the odd, because no number is purely even or purely odd. Hence, the even and the odd receive two <term>tetractys</term> according to multiplication; the even, indeed, in a double ratio, for 2 is the first of even numbers and increases from the <term>monad</term> by duplication. But the odd number is increased in a triple ratio; for 3 is the first of odd numbers and is itself increased from the <term>monad</term> by triplication. Hence, the <term>monad</term> is common to both of these, being itself both even and odd. The second number, however, among even and double numbers is 2; but among odd and triple numbers, it is 3. The third among even numbers is 4; but among odd numbers, it is 9. And the fourth among even numbers is 8; but among odd numbers, it is 27.

-> { 1. 2. 4. 8. } <-
-> { 1. 3. 9. 27. } <-

In these numbers, the more perfect ratios of symphonies are found; and in these also, a tone is comprehended. The <term>monad</term>, however, contains the productive principle of a point. But the second numbers, 2 and 3, contain the principle of a side, since they are incomposite and prime, are measured by the <term>monad</term>, and naturally measure a straight line. The third terms are 4 and 9, which are potentially a square <term>superficies</term>, since they are equally equal. And the fourth terms, 8 and 27, being equally equal, are potentially a <term>cube</term>. Hence, from these numbers and this <term>tetractys</term>, the progression takes place from a point to a solid. For a side follows after a point, a <term>superficies</term> after a side, and a solid after a <term>superficies</term>. In these numbers also, Plato in the *Timaeus* constitutes the soul. But the last of these seven numbers, that is, 27, is equal to the sum of all the numbers that precede it; for 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 8 + 9 = 27.

<summary>This page describes the second tetractys, which is formed by the geometric progression of even and odd numbers (powers of 2 and 3) starting from the monad. These numerical sequences represent the transition from a mathematical point to a three-dimensional solid and serve as the foundation for harmonic ratios and the soul's composition in Platonic philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>tetractys, monad, even and odd numbers, double ratio, triple ratio, duplication, triplication, symphonies, superficies, square, cube, Plato, Timaeus, geometry</keywords>

--- Page 251 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the numerical tetractys before moving on to the third and fourth types, which relate to geometric magnitude and the physical elements.</meta>

There are, therefore, two <term>tetractys</term> of numbers: one which exists by addition and the other by multiplication. They comprise musical, geometrical, and arithmetical ratios, which also constitute the harmony of the universe.

But the third <term>tetractys</term> is that which, according to the same analogy or proportion, includes the nature of all magnitude. For what the <term>monad</term> was in the former <term>tetractys</term>, a point is in this one. What the numbers 2 and 3 (which are potentially a side) were in the former <term>tetractys</term>, the extended types of lines—the circular and the straight—are in this one. The straight line exists in conformity to the even number, since it is bounded <note>Instead of περιττούται (to be odd), it is necessary to read περατούται (to be bounded); I am surprised that the learned Bullialdus did not observe the necessity of this correction.</note> by two points; but the circular line exists in conformity to the odd number, because it is contained by one line which has no end. Furthermore, what the square numbers 4 and 9 were in the former <term>tetractys</term>, the two types of planes—the <term>rectilinear</term> and the circular—are in this one. And what the cube numbers 8 and 27 were in the former (one being an even and the other an odd number), the two types of solids are in this <term>tetractys</term>: one which has a curved <term>superficies</term> (surface), such as the sphere and the cylinder, and the other which has a plane <term>superficies</term>, such as the <term>cube</term> and pyramid. Therefore, this is the third <term>tetractys</term>, which completes every magnitude, proceeding from a point, a line, a <term>superficies</term>, and a solid.

The fourth <term>tetractys</term> consists of the simple bodies—fire, air, water, and earth—which have an analogy according to numbers. For what the <term>monad</term> was in the first <term>tetractys</term>, fire is in this one. The <term>duad</term> is air, the <term>triad</term> is water, and the <term>tetrad</term> is earth. Such is the nature of the elements according to the thinness and density of their parts. Hence, fire has a ratio of 1 to 2 with air; but with water, a ratio of 1 to 3; and with earth, a ratio of

<meta>This page continues the enumeration of the eleven types of tetractys in Pythagorean philosophy, detailing how the number four structures physical elements, biological growth, social structures, mental faculties, the soul-body complex, the seasons, and the stages of human life.</meta>

1 to 4. In other respects also they are analogous to each other.

The fifth <term>tetractys</term> consists of the figures of the simple bodies. For the pyramid is the figure of fire; the <term>octahedron</term> is the figure of air; the <term>icosahedron</term> is the figure of water; and the cube is the figure of earth.

The sixth <term>tetractys</term> concerns things that come into existence through vegetative life. The seed is analogous to the <term>monad</term> and a point. If it increases in length, it is analogous to the <term>duad</term> and a line; if in breadth, it is analogous to the <term>triad</term> and a <term>superficies</term>; and if in thickness, it is analogous to the <term>tetrad</term> and a solid.

The seventh <term>tetractys</term> is of communities. Its principle, and as it were its <term>monad</term>, is man; the <term>duad</term> is a house; the <term>triad</term> is a street; and the <term>tetrad</term> is a city. For a nation consists of these. These, indeed, are the material and sensible <term>tetractys</term>.

The eighth <term>tetractys</term> consists of the powers which form a judgment of material and sensible things, and which are of a certain intelligible nature. These are intellect, science, opinion, and sense. Intellect corresponds in its essence to the <term>monad</term>; but science corresponds to the <term>duad</term>, for science is the science of a specific thing. Opinion exists between science and ignorance; but sense is like the <term>tetrad</term>. Since the sense of touch, which is common to all the senses, is fourfold, all the senses function through contact.

The ninth <term>tetractys</term> is that from which the animal is composed: the soul and the body. The parts of the soul are the rational, the irascible, and the <term>epithymetic</term> (or that which desires external goods); and the fourth is the body in which the soul lives.

The tenth <term>tetractys</term> is of the seasons of the year, through which all things come into existence: namely the spring, the summer, the autumn, and the winter.

And the eleventh is of the ages of man: namely the infant, the youth, the man, and the old man.

Hence there are eleven <term>tetractys</term>. The first is that

<meta>This page provides explanatory notes on the mathematical and symbolic properties of numbers, specifically focusing on the number 6 as a "perfect number" and the Pythagorean symbol regarding justice, continuing the commentary on Iamblichus's works.</meta>

**P. 81.** *This number is the first that partakes of every number, and when divided in every possible way, receives the power of the numbers subtracted, and of those that remain.*

Because 6 consists of 1, 2, and 3—the first two of which are the principles of all number, and also because 2 and 3 are the first even and odd numbers, which are the sources of all species of numbers—the number 6 may be said to partake of every number. In what Iamblichus adds afterward, I suppose he alludes to 6 being a perfect number and therefore equal to the sum of all its parts.

**P. 98.** *Do not step above the beam of the balance.*

This is the 14th Symbol in the *Protreptics* of Iamblichus, whose explanation of it is as follows: “This symbol exhorts us to the exercise of justice, to honoring equality and moderation in an admirable degree, and to the knowledge of justice as the most perfect virtue, which completes the other virtues and without which none of the rest are of any advantage. It also admonishes us that it is proper to know this virtue not in a careless manner, but through theorems and scientific demonstrations. But this knowledge is the business of no art or science other than the Pythagorean philosophy alone, which honors disciplines above everything else to a transcendent degree.”

The following extract also from my *Theoretic Arithmetic* (p. 194) will clarify this symbol to an even greater degree. The information contained in it is derived from the anonymous author of a very valuable work titled <term>Θεολογούμενα ’Ἀριθμητικής</term> (*Theologumena Arithmeticae*), which has lately been reprinted at Leipzig. “The Pythagoreans called the <term>pentad</term> providence and

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and symbolic exploration of the number five (the pentad) as a representation of justice, building upon the Pythagorean concepts mentioned on the previous page regarding moderation and the beam of the balance.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>241</page-num>
<sig>16</sig>

justice, because it equalizes unequal things, since justice is a medium between excess and deficiency, just as 5 is the middle of all the numbers that are equally distant from it on both sides as far as the decad, some of which it surpasses, and by others is surpassed, as may be seen in the following arrangement:

->1.  4.  7.<-
->2.  5.  8.<-
->3.  6.  9.<-

For here, as in the middle of the beam of a balance, 5 does not stray from the line of equilibrium, while one scale is raised and the other is lowered. In the following arrangement also, namely 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, it will be found that the sum of the numbers that follow it is triple the sum of those that precede 5; for 6+7+8+9=30; but 1+2+3+4=10. If, therefore, the numbers on each side of 5 represent the beam of a balance, with 5 being the tongue of it, when a weight lowers the beam, an obtuse angle is produced by the lowered part of the tongue, and an acute angle by the raised part of the beam. Hence it is worse to commit than to suffer an injury; and the authors of the injury sink downward as it were to the infernal regions; but the injured tend upward as it were to the Gods, imploring divine assistance. Hence the meaning of the Pythagorean symbol is obvious: “Do not step above the beam of the balance.” Since, however, injustice pertains to inequality, in order to correct this, equalization is required so that the beam of the balance may remain on both sides without tilting. But equalization is achieved by addition and subtraction. Thus, if 4 is added to 5, and 4 is also taken from 5, the number 9 will be produced on one side and 1 on the other, each of which is equally distant from 5. Thus too, if 3 is added to 5 and is also subtracted from it, on one side 8 will be produced and on the other 2.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of how the number five (the pentad) serves as a point of equilibrium and moves into the symbolic interpretations of Pythagorean teachings, specifically regarding the management of anger and the logical relationship between a whole and its parts.</meta>

If 2 is added to 5, and likewise taken from it, 7 and 3 will be produced. And by adding 1 to 5, and subtracting 1 from it, 6 and 4 will be the result; in all of these instances, the numbers produced are equidistant from 5, and the sum of each pair is equal to 10.”

### P. 116. *Do not stir fire with a sword.*

This is the 9th Symbol in the *Protreptics*, and is explained this way by Iamblichus: “This symbol encourages prudence. For it rouses in us a proper understanding of the appropriateness of not directing sharp words toward a man full of fire and wrath, nor contending with him. For you will frequently agitate and disturb an ignorant man with words, and you will suffer dreadful and unpleasant consequences yourself. Heraclitus also testifies to the truth of this symbol. For he says, “It is difficult to fight with anger: for whatever must be done redeems the soul.” And he says this truly. For many, by indulging their anger, have changed the condition of their soul and have made death preferable to life. But by governing the tongue and being quiet, friendship is produced from strife, as the fire of anger is extinguished; and you yourself will not appear to be lacking in intellect.”

### P. 146. *But this follows from the whole being naturally prior to the part, and not the part to the whole.*

For the whole <term>co-subverts</term>, but is not co-subverted by the part: since if the whole is taken away, the part is also taken away; but the reverse does not follow.

<summary>This page concludes a mathematical proof regarding the balanced properties of the number five and provides a Pythagorean symbolic warning against escalating conflict with angry individuals. It also explains the logical principle that a whole is naturally prior to its parts because the destruction of the whole necessitates the destruction of the parts.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the explanation of Pythagorean symbols and mathematical proofs to a detailed classification of the hierarchy of virtues as found in the works of Plato, specifically referencing the translator's notes on the *Phaedo*.</meta>

P. 167. *Those, therefore, who have the intellectual and gnostic part of virtue are called skillful and intelligent; but those who have the ethical and pre-elective part of it are called useful and equitable.*

The following account of the virtues is extracted from the notes to my translation of the *Phaedo* of Plato: The first of the virtues are the physical, which are common to brute animals, being mingled with the temperaments and, for the most part, contrary to each other—or rather, pertaining to the animal nature. Or it may be said that they are illuminations from reason when not hindered by a certain bad temperament, or that they are the result of energies in a former life. Plato speaks of these in the *Politicus* and the *Laws*. The ethical virtues, which are above these, are produced by custom and a certain right opinion, and are the virtues of children when well educated. These virtues are also to be found in some brute animals. They likewise transcend the temperaments and, on this account, are not contrary to each other. Plato presents these virtues in the *Laws*. However, they pertain at the same time both to reason and the irrational nature. 

In the third rank above these are the political virtues, which pertain to reason alone; for they are scientific. But they are the virtues of reason adorning the irrational part as its instrument: through prudence adorning the gnostic part, through fortitude the irascible part, and through temperance the epithymetic power (or the power which is the source of desire); but adorning all parts of the irrational nature through justice. And of these virtues Plato speaks much in the *Republic*. These virtues also follow each other. Above these are the cathartic virtues, which pertain to reason alone, withdrawing from other things to itself, throwing aside the instruments of sense as vain, and also repressing the

<sig>16*</sig>

<summary>This page outlines a hierarchy of Platonic virtues, categorizing them into physical, ethical, and political levels based on their relationship to reason, habit, and the irrational soul. It specifically defines political virtues like prudence, fortitude, temperance, and justice as the scientific application of reason to govern human desire and anger.</summary>

<keywords>Plato, Phaedo, virtues, physical virtues, ethical virtues, political virtues, cathartic virtues, prudence, fortitude, temperance, justice, irascible, epithymetic, gnostic</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues the classification of the hierarchy of virtues, specifically detailing the cathartic, theoretic, and paradigmatic levels and their relationship to the soul's ascent toward the intellect.</meta>

...activities through these instruments, and liberating the soul from the bonds of generation. Plato particularly explains these virtues in the *Phaedo*. Prior to these, however, are the theoretic virtues, which pertain to the soul introducing itself to natures superior to itself—not only gnostically (through knowledge), as someone might be led to think from the name, but also orectically (through desire). For the soul hastens to become, as it were, intellect instead of soul; and intellect possesses both desire and knowledge. These virtues are the opposite of the political virtues; for as the latter act upon subordinate things according to reason, so the former act upon more excellent things according to the intellect. Plato presents these virtues in the *Theaetetus*.

According to Plotinus, there is also another level of the virtues besides these: namely, the paradigmatic. For, just as our eye, when it is first illuminated by the solar light, is different from the light that illuminates it (being the thing illuminated), but afterwards is in a certain respect united and joined with it, and becomes, as it were, solar-form; so also our soul at first is indeed illuminated by intellect and acts according to the theoretic virtues, but afterwards becomes, as it were, that which is illuminated, and acts uniformly according to the paradigmatic virtues. And it is indeed the business of philosophy to make us intellect; but of theurgy to unite us to intelligibles, so that we may act paradigmatically. And just as when possessing the physical virtues, we know mundane bodies (for the subjects of virtues of this kind are bodies); so from possessing the ethical virtues, we know the fate of the universe, because fate is concerned with irrational lives. For the rational soul is not under fate; and the ethical virtues are irrational, because they pertain to the irrational part of the soul. According to the political virtues we know mundane affairs, and according to the cathartic virtues, super-mundane affairs; but by possessing the theoretic virtues we know intellectual things, and from the paradigmatic virtues, intelligible things.

<summary>This page outlines the higher hierarchy of virtues—theoretic and paradigmatic—explaining how they transition the soul from rational governance to intellectual union. It distinguishes the roles of philosophy and theurgy in reaching these states and explains how each level of virtue corresponds to a different realm of knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Plato, Phaedo, theoretic virtues, gnostically, orectically, intellect, Theaetetus, Plotinus, paradigmatic virtues, philosophy, theurgy, mundane, ethical virtues, rational soul, political virtues, cathartic virtues</keywords>

--- Page 259 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the hierarchy of virtues, specifically identifying how temperance, justice, fortitude, and prudence align with the ethical, political, cathartic, and theoretic levels of the soul's development.</meta>

natures. Temperance also pertains to the ethical virtues; justice to the political, because of agreements; fortitude to the cathartic, by not leaning toward matter; and prudence to the theoretic. Note also that Plato in the *Phaedo* calls the physical virtues servile, because they can exist in servile souls; but he calls the ethical virtues σκιογραφίαι (*skiographiai*), or *adumbrations*, because those who possess them only know *that* the actions of such virtues are right, but do not know *why* they are so. It is well observed here too by Olympiodorus that Plato calls the cathartic and theoretic virtues those which are, in reality, true virtues. He also distinguishes them in another way: namely, that the political virtues are not *telestic* (meaning they do not pertain to mystic ceremonies), but that the cathartic and theoretic virtues are *telestic*. Therefore, Olympiodorus adds, the cathartic virtues are named from the purification used in the mysteries, while the theoretic virtues are named from perceiving divine things. For this reason, he agrees with the Orphic verses, which say that:

> The soul that dies uninitiated,
> Lies plunged in the blackest mire in Hades.

For initiation is the divinely inspired activity of the virtues. Olympiodorus also further observes that by the "thyrsus-bearers," Plato means those who act according to the political virtues, but by the "Bacchuses," he means those who practice the cathartic virtues. For we are bound in matter like Titans, through the great divisibility of our nature; but we rise from the dark mire as Bacchuses. Therefore, we become more prophetic at the time of death; and Bacchus is the overseeing guardian of death, because he is likewise the guardian of everything pertaining to the Bacchic sacred rites.

Likewise, all the virtues exhibit their own characters; these characters are common everywhere, but they exist appropriately in each level. For the characteristic property of fortitude is not declining toward subordinate things; that of temperance is a turning away from an inferior nature;

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of virtues, specifically focusing on how justice and prudence relate to the higher orders of existence and the gods, before transitioning to a text by Porphyry.</meta>

of justice, a proper energy adapted to being; and of prudence, the choice and selection of things good and evil. Olympiodorus further observes that all the virtues exist within the Gods. For many Gods, he says, are distinguished by their names; and all goodness originates from the Gods. Likewise, before things that only sometimes participate in the virtues (as we do), it is necessary that there be natures which always participate in them. In what order, therefore, do the virtues appear? Shall we say in the psychical order? For virtue is the perfection of the soul; and choice and pre-choice are the energies and projections of the soul. Hence the Chaldæan oracles join <term>fontal</term> virtue with <term>fontal</term> soul—or in other words, with the soul existing according to its cause. But may it not also be said that the virtues naturally desire to bring order to what is disordered? If this is admitted, they will originate from the <term>demiurgic</term> order. How then will they be <term>cathartic</term> there? May we not say, Olympiodorus adds, that through the <term>cathartic</term> virtues—considered according to their causal existence in Jupiter the <term>demiurge</term>—he is enabled to remain in his accustomed state, as Plato says in the *Timæus*? And further still, according to ancient theologians, he ascends to the tower of Saturn, who is a *pure* intellect.

As this distribution of the virtues, however, is currently no less novel than it is important, the following discussion of them from the *'Αφορμαι πρὸς τὰ νόητα*, or *AUXILIARIES TO INTELLIGIBLES*, by Porphyry, is added for the sake of the genuinely philosophical reader:

> "There is one kind of virtue pertaining to the political character, and another to the man who tends toward contemplation, and for this reason is called <term>theoretic</term>, and is now a beholder. And there are also other virtues pertaining to the intellect, insofar as it is intellect, and separate from the soul. The virtues of the political character, which consist in the moderation of the passions, are characterized by following and being

<summary>This page explores the divine and causal origins of virtues, arguing that they exist eternally in the Gods and the demiurgic order before manifesting in the soul. It introduces Porphyry’s distinctions between political virtues, which moderate passions, and theoretic virtues, which pertain to contemplation and the intellect.</summary>

<keywords>Olympiodorus, Chaldæan oracles, fontal virtue, demiurgic order, cathartic virtues, Jupiter, Saturn, Porphyry, political virtues, theoretic virtues, Plato, Timæus</keywords>

--- Page 261 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion of the hierarchy of virtues, specifically detailing the transition from political virtues to the higher "purificatory" or cathartic virtues.</meta>

obedient to the reasoning about that which is appropriate in actions. Hence, looking toward a harmless interaction with neighbors, they are named—from the gathering of fellowship—political. And prudence indeed exists in the reasoning part; fortitude in the irascible part; temperance in the agreement and harmony of the <term>epithymetic</term> with the reasoning part; and justice in each of these performing its proper work with respect to governing and being governed. But the virtues of him who moves toward the contemplative life consist in a departure from earthly concerns. Hence also, they are called purifications, being observed in the restraint from bodily actions and the avoidance of sympathies with the body. For these are the virtues of the soul elevating itself to true being. The political virtues, therefore, adorn the mortal man and are the forerunners of purifications. For it is necessary that he who is adorned by these should abstain from doing anything primarily in conjunction with the body. Hence in purifications, not to form opinions with the body but to act alone gives existence to prudence, which derives its perfection through acting intellectually with purity. But not to be similarly passive with the body constitutes temperance. Not to fear a departure from the body as if into some void or nonexistence gives existence to fortitude. But when reason and intellect are the leaders and there is no resistance <note>from the irrational part</note>, justice is produced. The disposition, therefore, according to the political virtues is seen in the moderation of the passions, having for its goal to live as a human being in conformity with nature. But the disposition according to the theoretical virtues is seen in apathy <note>1</note>, the goal of which is a likeness to God.
“Since, however, of purification one kind consists in

---

<note>1 This philosophical apathy is not, as is foolishly supposed by most today, insensibility, but a perfect subjugation of the passions to reason.</note>

<summary>This text distinguishes between political virtues, which moderate the passions for social life, and purificatory virtues, which detach the soul from bodily concerns to achieve a likeness to God. It defines justice, prudence, temperance, and fortitude within both these human and contemplative frameworks.</summary>
<keywords>political virtues, purifications, epithymetic, irascible, contemplative life, apathy, similitude to God, prudence, fortitude, temperance, justice, Porphyry, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 262 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s discussion of the hierarchy of virtues, moving from the definition of cathartic virtues to the even higher level of intellectual virtues.</meta>

Purifying, while another pertains to those who are purified, the cathartic virtues are examined according to both these meanings of purification; for they purify the soul and are present during purification. For the goal of purification is to become pure. But since purification, and being purified, are the removal of everything foreign, the good resulting from them will be different from that which purifies; so that if that which was purified was good prior to the impurity with which it is defiled, purification is sufficient. That, however, which remains after purification is good, and is not the purification itself. The nature of the soul also was not good [in itself], but is that which is able to partake of good, and is boniform. For if this were not the case, it would not have become situated in evil. The good of the soul, therefore, consists in being united to its generator; but its evil consists in an association with things subordinate to itself. Its evil is also twofold: the one arising from an association with terrestrial natures, and the other from doing this with an excess of the passions. Hence all the political virtues, which liberate the soul from one evil, may be called virtues and are honorable. But the cathartic virtues are more honorable and liberate it from evil insofar as it is a soul. It is necessary, therefore, that the soul, when purified, should associate with its generator. Hence, its virtue after its conversion consists in a scientific knowledge of true being; but this will not be the case unless conversion precedes.

> "There is therefore another genus of virtues after the cathartic and political, which are the virtues of the soul acting intellectually. And here, indeed, wisdom and prudence consist in the contemplation of those things which the intellect possesses. But justice consists in performing what is appropriate in conformity to and acting according to the intellect. Temperance is an inward conversion of the soul to the intellect. And fortitude is apathy, according to a resemblance to that which the soul looks toward, and which is naturally"

<summary>This page distinguishes between the process of purification and the resulting state of being pure, defining the soul's good as union with its divine generator. It also introduces the intellectual virtues—wisdom, justice, temperance, and fortitude—as the highest level of virtue where the soul acts in perfect alignment with the intellect.</summary>

<keywords>purification, cathartic virtues, soul, boniform, generator, political virtues, intellectual virtues, wisdom, prudence, justice, temperance, fortitude, apathy</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues Porphyry’s classification of the hierarchy of virtues, moving from the intellectual virtues to the highest level: the paradigmatic virtues.</meta>

impassive. These virtues, also, in the same manner as the others, alternately follow each other.

> “The fourth species of the virtues is that of the paradigms existing in intellect; these are more excellent than the psychical virtues and exist as the paradigms of them, with the virtues of the soul being the likenesses of them. And intellect indeed is that in which all things exist at once as paradigms. Here, therefore, prudence is science; but the intellect that knows <insert>all things</insert> is wisdom. Temperance is that which is turned toward itself. The proper work of intellect is the performance of its appropriate duty, <insert>and this is justice <note>1</note></insert>. But fortitude is sameness, and the abiding with purity in itself, through an abundance of power. There are therefore four kinds of virtues; of which, indeed, some pertain to intellect, agree with its essence, and are paradigmatic. Others pertain to the soul when looking toward intellect and being filled by it. Others belong to the soul of man as it purifies itself and becomes purified from the body and the irrational passions. And others are the virtues of the soul of man, adorning the man by giving measure and limit to the irrational nature and producing moderation in the passions. And he, indeed, who has the greater virtues also necessarily has the lesser; but the contrary is not true—that he who has the lesser also has the greater virtues. Nor will he who possesses the greater act primarily according to the lesser, but only so far as the necessities of the mortal nature require. The goal of the virtues is also, as we have said, generically different among the different virtues. For the goal of the political virtues is to give measure to the passions in their practical activities according to nature. But the goal of the cathartic virtues is entirely to obliterate the remembrance of the passions. And the goal of the rest exists analogously to what has been said before.”

<note>1 The words *καὶ δικαιοσύνη* [and justice] are omitted in the original. But it is evident from *Plotinus* that they ought to be inserted.</note>

<summary>Porphyry defines the four levels of virtues—political, cathartic, intellectual, and paradigmatic—explaining that the higher virtues contain the lower ones. He focuses on the paradigmatic virtues as the essential models within the intellect that guide the soul's development and its liberation from the passions.</summary>

<keywords>Porphyry, Neoplatonism, paradigmatic virtues, political virtues, cathartic virtues, intellect, Plotinus, justice, prudence, temperance, fortitude, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of virtues, shifting from the definitions of paradigmatic virtues to the specific character of the individuals who practice them and the practical necessity of purification.</meta>

Hence, he who acts according to the practical virtues is a *worthy man*; but he who acts according to the cathartic virtues is a *daemonic man*, or is also a *good daemon*. He who acts according to the intellectual virtues alone is a *God*. But he who acts according to the paradigmatic virtues is *the father of the gods*. Therefore, we ought especially to pay attention to the cathartic virtues, since we may attain these in the present life. For through these, the ascent leads to the more honorable virtues. Consequently, it is necessary to examine to what degree purification may be extended. For it is a separation from the body and from the passive motion of the irrational part. But how this may be achieved, and to what extent, must now be explained.

In the first place, it is necessary that he who intends to acquire this purification should—as its foundation and basis—know himself to be a soul bound in a foreign thing and in a different essence. In the second place, as a structure built upon this foundation, he should collect himself from the body, as if from different places, so as to be oriented in a manner perfectly unaffected by the body. For he who acts uninterruptedly according to sense, even though he may not do this with an attached affection and the enjoyment resulting from pleasure, still has his attention scattered across the body because he comes into contact with it through sense 1. We are prone to the pleasures or pains of sensible objects, combined with a readiness and converging sympathy; it is necessary to be purified from this disposition. *This, however, will be achieved by admitting necessary pleasures and the sensations of them merely as remedies, or as a liberation from pain, so that <insert>the rational part</insert> may not be hindered <insert>in its operations</insert>.* Pain must also be removed. But if this is not possible, it must be mildly diminished. And it

---

<note>1 Instead of "according to it" here, it is necessary to read "according to sense."</note>

<summary>The text classifies men into a hierarchy based on the virtues they exercise—ranging from worthy men to the father of the gods—and describes the process of soul purification as a detachment from the body and its sensations.</summary>
<keywords>virtues, purification, soul, daemonic, intellectual virtues, paradigmatic virtues, senses, Neoplatonism</keywords>

--- Page 265 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific practical instructions for purification, detailng how the rational soul must detach itself from the impulses of the irrational part and the sensations of the body.</meta>

will be diminished if the soul does not suffer along with it. Anger, likewise, must be removed as much as possible and must by no means be premeditated. But if it cannot be entirely removed, deliberate choice must not be mixed with it; instead, the unpremeditated movement must be merely the impulse of the irrational part. *That which is unpremeditated, however, is weak and small.* All fear must also be expelled. For he who achieves this purification will fear nothing. In this state, however, if fear should occur, it will be unpremeditated. Therefore, anger and fear must only be used for the purpose of warning or correction. 

But the desire for everything base must be exterminated. Such a person, insofar as he is a <term>cathartic philosopher</term>, will not desire food and drink. Nor must there be any unpremeditated impulse in natural sexual relations; *but if this should occur, it must only extend to that sudden imagination which is active during sleep.* In short, the intellectual soul itself of the purified man must be liberated from all these <note>bodily tendencies</note>. 

He must also endeavor that whatever is moved toward the irrational nature of bodily passions should be moved without sympathy and without conscious attention, so that the movements themselves may be immediately dissolved through their proximity to the reasoning power. This, however, will not occur while purification is still progressing toward perfection, but will happen to those in whom reason rules without opposition. Hence, in these individuals, the inferior part of the soul will so revere reason that it will feel indignant if it is moved at all—being upset that it was not quiet when its master was present—and will reprove itself for its own weakness. These states, however, are still only the moderation of the passions, but they eventually result in <term>apathy</term>. For when <term>co-passivity</term> is entirely exterminated, then apathy is present in the one who is purified from it. For passion is stirred when reason provides the excitation by turning <note>toward the irrational nature</note>.

<summary>This page details the process of purging the soul of passions like anger, fear, and physical desire to achieve a state of apathy. It explains that a perfected philosopher's reason should rule so completely that irrational impulses are either entirely removed or immediately dissolved without the soul's sympathy.</summary>

<keywords>purification, cathartic philosopher, apathy, reasoning power, irrational nature, soul, passions, co-passivity, self-discipline</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the previous discussion on the purification of the soul and the role of the philosopher, ending with a specific philological critique of a passage regarding the enjoyment of philosophical theorems.</meta>

Page 199. *The theorems of philosophy are to be enjoyed, as much as possible, as if they were ambrosia and nectar, etc. etc.*

This sentence in the original by Arcerius is as follows:
τῶν κατὰ φιλοσοφίαν θεωρημάτων ἀπολαυστέον, ἐφ᾿ ὅσον οἷόν, καθάπερ ἀμβροσίας καὶ νέκταρος· ἀκήρατόν τε γὰρ τὸ ἀπ᾿ αὐτῶν ἡδύ καὶ τὸ θεῖον τὸ μεγαλόψυχον δύναταί τε ποιεῖν, καὶ εἰ μὴ ἀϊδίους, ἀϊδίων γε ἐπιστήμονας.

In the edition of the *Protreptics* by Kiessling—which I did not see until the greater part of this work was printed—*sophian* is substituted for *philosophian*, but in my opinion very erroneously. This German editor, because he did not perceive the necessity of reading ἀκήρατόν τε γὰρ τὸ ἀπ᾿ αὐτῶν ἡδύ καὶ θεῖον, τὸ μεγαλόψυχον, etc., instead of retaining the reading of Arcerius, has made nonsense of this part of the sentence. For his version of it is: “Nam et sincera est eorum dulcedo, et divinam naturam, animum magnum efficere possunt.” <note>Translated from Latin: "For both their sweetness is pure, and they can produce a divine nature, a great mind."</note>

->THE END.<-

<summary>This final page critiques Kiessling's edition of the Protreptics, arguing that his textual substitutions and failure to correct Arcerius's original Greek reading result in a nonsensical translation.</summary>
<keywords>Arcerius, Kiessling, Protreptics, philology, Greek philosophy, ambrosia, nectar, translation criticism</keywords>

## IAMBLICHUS'S
# LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS,
### OR
# PYTHAGOREAN LIFE.

<vocab>Iamblichus, Pythagoras, Pythagorean Life</vocab>

<summary>This is the title page for Iamblichus's biography of the philosopher Pythagoras, titled "Life of Pythagoras" or "Pythagorean Life."</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Pythagoras, Pythagorean Life, biography, title page</keywords>

--- Page 295 ---
<meta>This page continues from the title page and provides the author's preface to the life of Pythagoras, emphasizing the divine nature of the subject and outlining the structure of the biography.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>prologue</page-type>

-> THE <-
# PREFACE.

Since it is the custom for all those who are well-disposed to call upon **God** at the beginning of every philosophical study, it is much more appropriate to do so in this instance. For as the philosophy which we are now about to consider is that which was delivered by the divine **Pythagoras**, it cannot be properly discussed without the assistance of the **Gods**. Besides this, its beauty and magnitude so far surpass human power that it is impossible for anyone to survey it all at once unless he is favored by some **Daemon** <note>Meaning a protective or inspiring spirit, not a malevolent entity.</note>. For these reasons, therefore, we shall invoke the **Gods** as our leaders at the beginning of this work; and we shall deliver our account of **Pythagoras** and his philosophy in the most accurate manner possible.

First, then, we shall speak of his homeland and his parents; afterward, we shall relate his education, and the different countries to which he traveled, and the different **Sages** with whom he was acquainted. We shall also mention his studies and his exercises; his institutions and laws; and his management of both private and public affairs. In the next place, we shall treat his medicinal and mathematical science; and finally, his death and the honors which were paid to him after his passing.

<column-break/>

But in the first place, we must observe that the ancestors of **Pythagoras** were not from the same country as those who are called **Samians**; but that they were originally from **Phlius**, a city of **Achaia**. For **Hippasus**, who was the great-grandfather of **Pythagoras**, fleeing from **Phlius** on account of civil unrest, came to **Samos**, where he settled and had a son named **Euphron**, who was the father of **Mnesarchus**, the father of **Pythagoras**.

<summary>This preface establishes the need for divine assistance in studying Pythagoras's life and outlines the biography's structure, which includes his education, travels, and laws. It also clarifies that Pythagoras's ancestors migrated from Phlius to Samos due to political unrest.</summary>
<keywords>preface, Pythagoras, divine invocation, Daemon, Phlius, Samos, Mnesarchus, Hippasus, ancestry</keywords>

### IAMBLICHUS’
# LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS,
#### OR
## PYTHAGORIC LIFE.

<vocab>Iamblichus, Pythagoras, Pythagoric Life</vocab>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>prologue</page-type>

-> THE <-
# PREFACE.

Since it is the custom for all well-meaning people to call upon **God** at the beginning of every philosophical study, it is even more appropriate to do so in this case. Because the philosophy we are now about to consider is the one handed down by the divine **Pythagoras**, it cannot be properly discussed without the assistance of the **Gods**. Beyond this, its beauty and magnitude so far exceed human power that it is impossible for anyone to comprehend it all at once unless they are favored by some **Daemon** <note>Meaning a protective or inspiring spirit, not a malevolent entity.</note>. For these reasons, therefore, we shall invoke the **Gods** as our leaders at the beginning of this work; and we shall provide our account of **Pythagoras** and his philosophy in the most accurate manner possible.

First, then, we shall speak of his homeland and his parents; afterward, we shall describe his education, the various countries to which he traveled, and the different **Sages** with whom he was acquainted. We shall also mention his studies and his exercises; his institutions and laws; and his management of both private and public affairs. Next, we shall discuss his medical and mathematical science; and fin...

The author outlines the biography's scope, including Pythagoras's origins, education, travels, and his contributions to science and society.</summary>

# PREFACE.

Since it is the custom for all right-minded people to call upon **God** at the beginning of every philosophical study, it is much more appropriate to do so in this instance. Because the philosophy we are now about to consider is the one handed down by the divine **Pythagoras**, it cannot be properly discussed without the assistance of the **Gods**. Beyond this, its beauty and magnitude so far exceed human capacity that it is impossible for anyone to comprehend it all at once unless they are favored by some **Daemon** <note>Refers to a protective or inspiring spirit or divine power, rather than a malevolent entity.</note>. For these reasons, therefore, we shall invoke the **Gods** as our guides at the beginning of this work; and we shall provide our account of **Pythagoras** and his philosophy in the most accurate manner possible.

<language>English</language>
<page-type>preface</page-type>

## THE LIFE OF PYTHAGORAS.

### CHAPTER I.

As it is the custom of all those who are truly wise, at the beginning of every philosophical study and instruction, to call upon **God**; so it is much more necessary to do this in the present instance, which treats of that philosopher who was given the surname the *Divine*, and from whom the name of Philosophy itself originated. For as he was the father and prince of this study, it is necessary to begin by invoking the assistance of the Gods, so that we may be enabled to give a brief and summary account of his life, and of the most beautiful and excellent parts of his philosophy.

<margin>The origin of philosophy.</margin>

And indeed, it would be a most difficult and laborious undertaking to describe all the particulars of his life and to deliver his doctrine in a proper manner. For since the Pythagorean school has long been neglected and almost extinguished, and the writings of the Pythagoreans have been scattered and lost, it is very difficult to recover the memory of such ancient things and to collect the fragments of his philosophy that remain.

<margin>The Pythagorean school neglected.</margin>

<summary>Iamblichus begins his account by invoking the Gods, as is traditional for wise men, noting that Pythagoras was the "father" of philosophy. He acknowledges the difficulty of writing this biography because the Pythagorean school had been neglected and its original writings lost or scattered over time.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagoras, Iamblichus, philosophy, divine invocation, Pythagorean school, history of philosophy</keywords>
